CVS Group · CVSG-GB · the new Memo conveyor's card of 28 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: CVS Group

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 48,718 words, set out under Richard's eleven ratings in the order of the eleven ratings page: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the eleven ratings page uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

C-6012th of 30

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment has moved from hostile to merely unhelpful. The CMA tail risk has gone, labour has turned from a constraint into a modest support, and the H2 FY2027 comparatives are easy; against that, the UK household squeeze peaks in 2027, routine care is deferred, transparency pushes medicine sales online and wage floors rise every April. For the model this means: no external tailwind to organic volume in FY2027, a gradual one from FY2028, price as the only organic lever, and a cost base that needs about 3 to 4 percent price a year to stand still. The one powerful external force in the investor's favour is the private-market valuation gap, which supports the equity rather than the earnings.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0244C-0548C-0738C-0957C-3550C-3950
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C+4510th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Competitive intensity for customers is rising modestly in the UK at the margin, driven by price transparency rather than by any corporate rival; competition among corporates is stable and rational; competition for acquisition targets is easing in the UK and rising in Australia. The direction is mildly negative for organic growth and mildly positive for UK acquisition returns.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Transparency makes the corporate price premium visible. Corporate groups charge about £70 to £75 for a consultation against about £50 at independents (expert, 2026); the CMA found large corporates priced about 18 percent above independents while Pets at Home's joint-venture practices priced near parity. Mandatory online price lists, written estimates above £500 and ownership branding now put that gap in front of the customer.
  • Independents are resurgent at the edges. Experts describe clinicians leaving corporate chains to open de novo practices that market local ownership; Dr Cees Bennett calls the UK a "three-speed" market in which independents and joint ventures are better placed than fully corporate groups. No share figure measures the gain.
  • Corporate rivals are not competing on price. Pricing is set centrally in percentile bands; consolidators compete through drug supply terms; prices rose about 5 percent a year through a volume decline.
  • Online pharmacy competition is rising. Molecule Group (Hyperdrug) is integrating its CloudRx interface into independents' practice-management systems; Chewy's PracticeHub connects to about 45 percent of US vets. This competes for medicines, not consultations.
  • UK acquisition competition is easing. Practice valuations have fallen from about 3.5x to 2.5x revenue as consolidation cools; CMA compliance burdens push independents to sell (pent-up supply, per the CEO); only 10 to 15 percent of remaining UK practices are attractive.
  • Australian acquisition competition is rising. EQT-backed VetPartners (7 percent share) and TPG-backed Greencross (6 percent) bid for the same practices; multiples are quoted at about 6x (company), 5x to 8x (a Vets Central CFO, who says CVS's entry pushed prices up) and 9x to 10x (a former CVS director).
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C4411th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The net effect is shifting from a strong headwind (2023 to 2025) to a mild headwind. Labour availability, the binding constraint for years, has turned: vacancies are falling, applicants per role are rising and the profession's own modelling sees no medium-term shortage, which cuts locum costs and should slow clinical salary inflation. Management is already monetising this through rostering. Against it, statutory wage floors rise every April on a cost base where employment is 52.3 percent of revenue, drug manufacturers are raising prices and cutting rebates, and medicine supply is concentrated in two contracts. Price must now rise by about 3 to 4 percent a year just to hold the margin.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Labour availability has improved materially. The vet vacancy rate fell to 5.8 percent in FY2026 from 6.5 percent (against a 10 percent UK public-sector average); recruitment moved from one candidate per role to several; EU vets are returning; new UK vet schools enlarge graduate cohorts; the Chief Veterinary Officer cites Royal College modelling of no companion-animal workforce shortage in the medium term; attrition fell. Average vets rose 3.5 percent to 2,532 including acquisitions, 0.4 percent organically.
  • It is showing in costs. Clinical staff costs fell to 34.3 percent of revenue in H2 FY2026 from 36.0 percent, and to 34.0 percent from 35.7 percent in H1, through rostering and lower locum use.
  • Wage floors keep rising. Employment costs were 52.3 percent of revenue (52.2 percent in FY2025). Each 1 percent of group employment-cost inflation costs about £3.7m (derived), about 2.6 percent of EBITDA and about 4 percent of EPS.
  • Drug prices are adverse. Clinic operators report aggressive manufacturer pricing (Zoetis named) and reduced rebates (expert calls, October 2026); against that, CVS retendered its main supply contracts and non-clinical cost of sales fell to 21.4 percent of revenue from 21.6 percent.
  • Supplier concentration is latent, not active. Two significant contracts cover all veterinary medicines; alternative sources exist; no stress is reported.
  • Energy: the January 2027 cap rise hits households more than CVS, which has locked in utility costs (Deutsche Bank).
  • Australia: vet shortages and wage pressure persist, offset by procurement synergies from moving acquired practices to a single wholesaler.

Implication for the model. On a cost base that is about 80 percent of revenue with wages at about two-thirds of it, wage inflation of 4 to 5 percent needs price of about 3 to 4 percent to hold the margin; margin expansion has to come from volume, mix (Australia, laboratories) or productivity, not from price.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F3523rd of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The UK macro squeeze bites hardest at the company level, less at the sector and least at the industry. The sector (healthcare services) is being bought as a defensive in a risk-off, rates-led market, and healthcare demand is broadly non-discretionary; but CVS is a UK consumer-facing, labour-heavy, privately paid service with about 89 percent of revenue in the UK and about a quarter of sales in deferrable categories, so a squeezed household in 2027 reaches its volumes while UK wage legislation reaches its costs. Rates matter more through the valuation than through interest cost.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The UK consumer: 2027 growth cut to 1.0 percent, inflation 3.5 percent in September peaking near 3.75 percent, a Bank of England rise to 4.00 percent on 5 Nov 2026 almost fully priced and about 4.25 percent by mid-2027, the energy cap up about 16 percent in January, a further two points of income tax on landlords and savers from April 2027; 45 percent of UK households limiting non-essential purchases (Deutsche Bank, mid-2026).
  • The sector: our Fitness for Fighting tilt has the Hospitals, Clinics and Care Homes sector Overweight on non-discretionary demand in a regime of high rates and low consumer confidence; CVS sits in the "private hospital, clinic, lab and vet networks" cohort, which has just turned (+9.0 percent over three months).
  • Rates directly: about £118m of drawn debt floats over SONIA (1.25 to 2.50 percent margin); £100m is swapped to February 2028; a 1 percentage point rise costs about £0.8m after tax; interest cover is 13.1x. Indirectly, the discount rate weighs on every multiple, and Fitness for Fighting's Momentum tilt is Underweight.
  • Australia: a stronger economy (practices ahead of plan), the Reserve Bank raising rates three times in 2026, trimmed-mean inflation about 3.5 percent. Translation is unhedged: a 10 percent move in GBP/AUD moves profit before tax by about £0.7m; houses expect sterling to weaken against the Australian dollar, a small tailwind.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–62

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The most material other dynamic is positive and sits in the capital market, not the business: private buyers are paying well above public-market multiples for European healthcare assets, and CVS, at about 7x EBITDA with an activist on its register and a separable laboratory business, is one of the stocks our Sector Digest names as early in that story. It puts a soft floor under the equity and gives the case a corporate-action option. Smaller dynamics: a sterling-weakness translation tailwind, an easy weather comparative, and, against, UK mid-cap fund flows and media scrutiny of vet pricing.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Private-market valuations: Spire Healthcare's recommended cash offer at about 9x its 2026 EBITDA; Clariane sold €1bn of assets at about 14x; Medicover selling Indian hospitals to KKR; healthcare takeovers of $191bn in H1 2026. At 9x FY2027 consensus EBITDA, CVS equity would be worth about 1,770p a share and at 10x about 1,990p (derived, using FactSet's enterprise-value bridge), against 1315p. A corporate buyer would face the CMA; a private-equity buyer would not.
  • The activist and the laboratories: Converium Capital (2 percent economic stake) said in May 2026 the shares stood at 2017 levels despite doubled revenue; Deutsche Bank estimates a laboratory sale could raise over £100m; management rejects it.
  • Currency: Morgan Stanley, Standard Chartered and Invesco expect sterling to weaken against the Australian dollar.
  • Weather: heat in late May to July 2026 deferred visits, giving an easy Q4 FY2027 comparative.
  • Against: the AIM-to-Main-Market move (January 2026) forced some holders out; UK mid-caps have not been rewarded for domestic earnings; media coverage of vet pricing continues (the company called the portrayal of the industry disappointing on 24 Sep 2026).
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

D5015th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The overall threat from new entrants is low to moderate and is concentrated in medicines. Adjacent entrants (online pharmacies and their software links) are the real threat, to perhaps a fifth to a quarter of practice revenue. Standard entrants (de novo independent practices) are a slow, local threat to share and talent. Orthogonal entrants (telehealth, mobile clinics, AI-led care) are a negligible threat in the horizon in the UK.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Adjacent: Chewy (PracticeHub on about 45 percent of US vets), Molecule Group (Hyperdrug; CloudRx interface into practice systems; veterinary segment 22 percent of its revenue), Tractor Supply's US acquisition of VIP Petcare to capture prescriptions (US only). Manufacturers and retailers avoid clinic ownership; Mars (Linnaeus) is the adjacent player in referral.
  • Standard: de novo independents founded by clinicians leaving corporates; low capital needed but constrained by vet supply and local reputation.
  • Orthogonal: telehealth limited by in-person examination rules and liability; AI tools are being adopted by incumbents, not used against them.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

D5015th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Suppliers are a mild source of cost pressure and a latent concentration risk, offset by CVS's purchasing scale. Manufacturers are raising prices and cutting rebates, which squeezes clinics that cannot pass it through, while CVS's retendered contracts, white-label medicines, buying-group services (MiVetClub, VetShare) and the move of Australian practices to one wholesaler are producing real savings. Labour, the largest supply input, is treated under C-05 and is turning supportive.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Concentration: two significant contracts for all veterinary medicines, alternatives in place.
  • Pricing: operators frustrated by aggressive manufacturer pricing (Zoetis named) and reduced manufacturer support (expert calls, October 2026).
  • Scale: volume rebates direct from manufacturers recorded in cost of sales; non-clinical cost of sales down to 21.4 percent of revenue; Australian procurement synergies described as immediate.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0142C-0345C-0463C-0844C-3242C-3758C-4360
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F3828th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. UK companion-animal demand is soft in volume but not collapsing, and the softness is mostly cyclical (households deferring routine and preventive care) layered on a temporary demographic valley (the large 2020 to 2022 pet cohort is in its low-need adult years), with a slow structural loss of medicine dispensing to online pharmacies on top. The cyclical part has no two-year visibility of recovery, because 2027 is forecast to be the weak UK year; the demographic part has good visibility of turning favourable as those pets pass age five, which helps FY2028 more than FY2027. Demand will not drive the next 18 months; price will. The single most useful finding for the model is that the reported second-half slowdown is mostly a comparative effect: on a two-year basis the trend improved through FY2026, but the first half of FY2027 laps the toughest comparative of the cycle.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • UK practice volume is falling about 2 to 4 percent a year (derived). UK practice revenue grew about 0.9 percent in FY2026 (Veterinary Practices LFL +1.0 percent) while prices rose: Healthy Pet Club revenue +3.5 percent on members -2.1 percent implies about 5 to 6 percent more revenue per member, and industry price rises have run at about 5 percent a year through the volume decline. Management describes volumes as flat to negative.
  • The second-half "slowdown" is a base effect plus heat. FY2025 LFL was 0.2 percent with H1 at -1.1 percent, so H2 FY2025 was about +1.5 percent (derived). Two-year stacked LFL was therefore about 1.6 percent in H1 FY2026 and about 3.0 percent in H2 FY2026 (derived): the trend improved. Hot weather in late May and June and a weather-hit July depressed the last months; Deutsche Bank reports August improved.
  • The next comparatives are uneven. H1 FY2027 laps 2.7 percent, the hardest comparative since the trough; H2 FY2027 laps about 1.5 percent and a heat-hit fourth quarter. Model LFL as H2-weighted.
  • The weakness is industry-wide, not CVS-specific. Pets at Home's vet growth slowed to +1.3 percent in its last quarter and it guides low-single-digit vet growth for FY2027; UK household pet spending fell 1 percent in 2025 (ONS, via Jefferies); an expert puts post-COVID UK visit declines at 3 to 4 percent a year; US veterinary visits have fallen for 16 consecutive quarters (Morgan Stanley).
  • Demand is bifurcated. Reactive, referral and diagnostic demand holds (laboratory volumes +4.3 percent; referral described as highly robust); routine wellness, preventive bloodwork and elective procedures are deferred; owners switch to generics (about half of US practices surveyed by Morgan Stanley have switched for two common drugs). Jefferies puts about 25 percent of group sales in discretionary categories.
  • The demographic valley is ending. About 2m UK lockdown pets are now four to six years old; Pets at Home says pets aged three to four are in their lowest-spending veterinary years and spend rises by £20 to £25 a year from about age five. This is biologically predictable but its revenue effect is gradual and has not shown in three years of numbers.
  • The macro backdrop for the next 6 to 12 months is adverse. The Office for Budget Responsibility (28 Oct 2026) has UK growth at 1.0 percent in 2027, inflation peaking near 3.75 percent, Bank Rate rising to about 4.25 percent by mid-2027, and the energy price cap forecast up about 16 percent in January 2027.
  • Membership is the cleanest volume indicator, and it is still falling. Healthy Pet Club members fell from 519,000 (FY2025) to 516,000 (H1) to 508,000 (FY2026); the new Healthy Pet Club Advanced tier (unlimited consultations, launched 1 Jul 2026) signed about 14,500 clients in three months.
  • Australia is better. Acquired practices are ahead of their business cases on a stronger economy, though Jefferies says Australian organic growth does not yet look stronger than the UK's.

Implication for the model. UK practice volume -1 to -2 percent in FY2027, improving towards flat by FY2029 as the cohort ages; price +4 to 5 percent in FY2027 (the larger summer 2026 increases), moderating to 3 to 4 percent; group LFL 2.5 to 3.5 percent for FY2027, with H1 about 1.5 to 3.0 percent and H2 stronger. The 4 to 8 percent target needs volume to turn positive, which we do not expect before FY2028.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

D4619th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. One disruption force is strengthening against CVS, one is strengthening in its favour, and one has been disproved. The shift of medicine sales to online pharmacies is strengthening with regulatory help, but most of the chronic-medicine shift is already in the base and goods are under 30 percent of revenue, so it is a slow drag rather than a break. Artificial intelligence (scribes, diagnostic reading) is strengthening as a workflow tailwind that favours scaled operators on cloud systems. Telehealth as a substitute for clinic visits has been largely disproved by in-person examination rules and liability. Net: a small negative on revenue, a small positive on cost.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The online medicine shift now has a regulatory push. The CMA's binding order (22 Sep 2026) requires clinics to tell owners they may take a written prescription elsewhere and may save money; online pharmacies price 50 to 60 percent below clinics. The written-prescription fee cap of £21 including VAT (plus £12.50 for each further drug) sits slightly above the industry average of about £20, so the cap itself is neutral; the disclosure is the risk.
  • Its size is limited. Fees were £473.1m against clinical goods of £200.1m (FY2025); an expert (18 Sep 2026) says most chronic medicine sales have already moved online; Morgan Stanley finds only 15 percent of offline buyers see uploading a prescription as friction, which cuts both ways (easy to switch, but most have not).
  • AI favours scale. Over 25 percent of UK clinical vets use AI scribes; cloud practice systems integrate them natively. CVS migrated its UK companion practices to Provet Cloud after the 2024 cyber attack and is trialling an AI scribe; IDEXX bought CoVet to lift diagnostic use.
  • Telehealth is constrained by mandatory physical examination rules and diagnostic liability; it is a triage tool, not a substitute.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

B657th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The largest regulatory change of the last 18 months has concluded in the company's favour: the CMA's final decision (24 Mar 2026) and legally binding order (22 Sep 2026) imposed no divestments and no service-price caps, removing the tail risk that dominated 2023 to 2026 and reopening UK acquisitions. What remains is a compliance cost, a transparency effect on medicine and elective spend, and the annual payroll-legislation lever, which is the only live political force on earnings. Against the last 18 months the change is clearly positive; looking forward it is neutral to mildly negative.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The CMA outcome: transparency remedies only (online price lists, ownership branding, prescription rights, written estimates for treatment above £500); the prescription cap raised to £21 from the £16 proposed. CVS is over 80 percent compliant and published price lists in late 2025; implementation cost £5.1m of exceptional costs in FY2026. Jefferies warns of a persistent administrative drag; experts expect the burden to fall harder on independents, which may sell.
  • The UK acquisition restart is the main consequence: the first deal since the pause, a two-site, nine-vet practice for an initial £15m. The CMA's 30 percent local share ceiling limits deals in concentrated towns.
  • Payroll legislation: employer National Insurance (about £8m a year) and National Living Wage rises (about £3m) from April 2025 are now in the base (about 7.6 percent of EBITDA unmitigated, per Jefferies). The Budget of 28 Oct 2026 funded household help from banks, gambling, online marketplaces, landlords and savers; our Geography Digest judges the statutory cost stack on labour-heavy sectors to be easing, and records no new employer-tax step. The April 2027 wage-floor uprating is the next step; its size is not in our files.
  • Slower items: the Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs consultation on the Veterinary Surgeons Act 1966 could widen nurses' clinical roles (a capacity positive, years away); Australia's new mandatory merger notification regime is described as non-onerous, with CVS at 1 to 2 percent of practices.
  • R&D tax credits (about £16m inside FY2026 EBITDA, derived) are a policy-exposed item; no change is reported in our files.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F4428th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. No industry or sector driver is accelerating CVS's demand now. The structural drivers (pet humanisation, the ageing pandemic cohort, rising diagnostic intensity, insurance) are slow; the one with a dated inflection, the cohort, is more likely to lift volumes in FY2028 than FY2027; and one driver, insurance, is currently working against routine and diagnostic spend. Consolidation in Australia drives CVS's acquired growth, not its demand.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Humanisation and spend: UK pet care about £10bn with a 5 percent ten-year growth rate; only 5.8 percent of owners would cut pet spending in a recession; senior dog and cat household share about 52 percent. Real, but it has not lifted three years of LFL (2.9, 0.2, 2.1 percent).
  • The cohort: spend rises from about age five (Pets at Home); the 2020 to 2022 adoptions reach five to seven in 2025 to 2029.
  • Diagnostics: the acuity theme is visible where it bites: laboratory revenue +11.5 percent, EBITDA +25.6 percent; Vimian diagnostics +12 percent; Zoetis companion diagnostics +13 percent.
  • Insurance: UK penetration about 50 percent (4.6m policyholders) buffers price rises, but premium inflation is reducing uptake and insurers are capping costly diagnostics such as MRI.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

F4526th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Customer demand supports the base of the thesis and not its growth leg. The base is defensive: reactive and referral care is non-discretionary, the Healthy Pet Club locks in over 40 percent of active patients and its revenue still rises, and insured owners spend inelastically. The growth leg is unsupported: routine visits, preventive care and wellness diagnostics are being deferred, members are falling and owners are trading down to generics. The support for the base is robust; the volume trend is fragile to further price rises, to the 2027 squeeze and to transparency.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Healthy Pet Club: 508,000 members (-2.1 percent), revenue £95.5m (+3.5 percent), about £15 a month by direct debit; management partly attributes the fall to phasing out legacy discount schemes and cancellations for deceased pets from migrated portfolios.
  • Defensive markers: only 5.8 percent of owners would cut pet spending in a recession; 91 percent say pets improve mental well-being; the global companion-animal health market grew 5.9 percent a year through 2007 to 2009 (Jefferies).
  • Fragile markers: UK pet spending -1 percent in 2025; owners "increasingly declining non-essential preventive care because price increases have outpaced wage growth" (expert); reduced insurance uptake; written estimates above £500 may push owners to cheaper options.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

D5019th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The regulator has moved from threat to neutral, which helps; the political actor raises CVS's costs every April, which hurts and cannot be hedged. The regulatory position is fairly robust for the horizon, since a concluded market investigation with binding orders is unlikely to be reopened soon, but it is fragile to media and political attention on vet prices, which transparency will now sustain.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • CMA: no divestments, no service-price caps, a £21 plus £12.50 prescription cap, transparency remedies; compliance costs £5.1m; experts expect the burden to drive independents to sell.
  • Political: employer National Insurance and wage-floor rises of about £11m a year since April 2025; the 28 Oct 2026 Budget did not add an employer-tax step per our Geography Digest; the April 2027 uprating is to come.
  • Slower: Veterinary Surgeons Act reform (Defra), likely to widen nurse roles; Australian mandatory merger notification.
  • Policy exposure: R&D tax credits of about £16m inside EBITDA.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

C-7212th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. The addressable market is large relative to CVS and is not the constraint on growth; capital, vets and organic volume are. The UK veterinary services market (about £6.7bn in 2024) gives CVS about 8 to 9 percent; Australia (about A$5.7bn, roughly £2.8bn) gives it about 3 percent by revenue. With nominal market growth of about 4 to 6 percent a year and a decade of Australian consolidation runway, the mid-term allows revenue growth of about 6 to 9 percent a year (organic 3 to 5 percent plus acquired 3 to 5 percent) and EPS growth of about 8 to 12 percent, but not the 12 to 20 percent band without a volume recovery.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Market growth: UK pet care about £10bn with a 5 percent ten-year growth rate; Morgan Stanley forecasts global companion-animal markets at about 6 percent a year to 2030; DNB Carnegie cites 9 percent compounded (product-weighted). These are price-heavy.
  • UK acquisitions: only 10 to 15 percent of remaining practices are attractive, and the 30 percent local ceiling binds.
  • Australia: 3,600 to 3,800 practices, 80 to 85 percent independent; about 40 percent of 1,100 metropolitan practices meet CVS's criteria; at £50m a year the runway is a decade or more (C-16).
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers

C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

D5619th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. CVS's organic cycle troughed in H1 FY2025 (LFL -1.1 percent) and has been recovering slowly; the turn is real but shallow and price-led, and on a two-year view it improved through FY2026 rather than slowing as the headline suggests. It is not yet a turn towards the 4 to 8 percent target. The acquisition cycle is turning up more clearly (UK reopened, Australia accelerating). Expect FY2027 LFL of about 2.5 to 3.5 percent, H2-weighted, with the November trading update and the 4 Mar 2027 interims both reading against a tough 2.7 percent comparative.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The LFL path: FY2023 7.3 percent; FY2024 2.9 percent (cyber attack and system migration); H1 FY2025 -1.1 percent; FY2025 0.2 percent; H1 FY2026 2.7 percent; H2 FY2026 about 1.5 percent; FY2026 2.1 percent. Two-year stacks of about 1.6 then 3.0 percent (derived) show the improvement.
  • The drivers of the turn are price (larger summer 2026 increases), easier comparatives in H2 FY2027, Healthy Pet Club Advanced and digital booking, and, later, the ageing cohort. The drag is household deferral of routine care.
  • Management's informal read: positive early LFL growth into FY2027, trading in line with expectations.
  • Revenue growth (reported) has run at 5.4 to 8.5 percent for four years and sits at trend; the organic part is what is below the company's normal rate.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F4324th of 30

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The external picture is a modest net headwind to organic earnings over the next 6 to 12 months, clearly less adverse than the last 12 to 18 months because the regulatory tail has gone, labour availability has turned and the comparatives ease in H2 FY2027, with a slow structural tailwind building for FY2028 onwards. External forces will permit about 2 to 4 percent organic growth; they will not drive the case. The case therefore rests on internal capital allocation and on whether price holds against a squeezed consumer.

Each driver in turn.

  • Pet humanisation and the ageing cohort: a slow tailwind, weak now, strengthening from FY2028. Value-chain level: demand.
  • The UK consumer squeeze: a moderate headwind, strongest in calendar 2027, acting on routine visits, wellness diagnostics and membership. Company level.
  • Consolidation: neutral in the UK (plateau at about 60 percent corporate, fewer attractive targets, lower vendor prices); a strong tailwind in Australia (15 to 20 percent consolidated) with rising competition for assets. Industry level.
  • Regulation: a large headwind removed; a small residual drag from transparency and compliance. Industry level.
  • Labour: from strong headwind to mild headwind; availability improved, statutory cost steps continue. Value-chain level (supply).
  • Online and digital disintermediation: a mild headwind on medicines, a mild tailwind from AI on cost. Value-chain level.
  • Private-market valuations: a tailwind to equity value, not to earnings. Sector level.
  • Rates and the market regime: a mild headwind via the discount rate, offset by sector-level preference for defensives. Sector level.

Taken together. At value-chain level the demand side is weak and the supply side improving; at industry level the structure is rational and supportive; at sector level the market favours the group. The headwinds are moderate and identifiable; the tailwinds are slow or financial.

Internal change forces/risks

F5525th of 30

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

CVS is a company changing its capital allocation rather than its operations. The buyback, the acquisition step-up across Australia and a reopened UK, the refinancing and the higher leverage are real, funded and already in the share count and the revenue line; together with tighter clinical rostering and procurement, they make 8 to 10 percent adjusted EPS growth in FY2027 close to arithmetic. The operating changes (Healthy Pet Club Advanced, digital booking, the national brand, nurse roles) are sensible but small and untested, guidance has not been raised, and the change that would most alter the trajectory, a new chief executive, is pending, with a reset of the medium-term targets the most likely first act. For the model: carry the capital-allocation mechanics in full, do not credit the organic initiatives with more than a point of LFL until they show in membership and volume, and treat buyback renewal after the current programme as uncertain.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

C-6013th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Standards are measurably improving in cost discipline, clinical staffing and governance, and that improvement is what held the margin flat through an £11m payroll-tax step and flat UK volumes. It has defended the margin, not raised it, and two measures went the wrong way in FY2026: cash conversion and, on the conflicting figures, colleague engagement in the second half.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Cost discipline: clinical staff costs 34.3 percent of revenue in H2 FY2026 against 36.0 percent; non-clinical cost of sales 21.4 percent against 21.6 percent after retendering; restructuring costs nil in H1 FY2026; capital expenditure cut to £36.4m and guided to about £30m.
  • Clinical operations: vet vacancy rate 5.8 percent (6.5 percent); attrition down; client Net Promoter Score 78.9 in 2025 (68.0 in 2024); Provet Cloud across the UK estate; standardised clinical protocols and a Life Stage Assessment framework.
  • Governance: Main Market listing (29 Jan 2026), FTSE 250 inclusion (March 2026), fully independent committees, about 70 percent board independence, a third-party whistleblowing system, a controls declaration under Provision 29 being prepared for January 2027, refinancing on better terms.
  • Against: adjusted operating cash conversion fell to 70.6 percent from 76.9 percent (working-capital outflow of £12.8m, timing of R&D credit receipts and new buying terms); the employee Net Promoter Score is reported at +10.0 for December 2025 and +5.8 for FY2026, which, if the measures are comparable, means engagement fell in the half in which the CEO's retirement and rostering changes landed; exceptional costs rose to £10.6m; executive base salaries rose 14 to 19 percent from 1 Jul 2026.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

D3321st of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The negatives are a cluster of individually tolerable weaknesses that together describe a roll-up whose earnings per share have not compounded for three years: the leadership vacancy, a price-led organic engine losing volume, falling cash conversion while leverage rises, a wide and recurring gap between adjusted and reported profit, executive pay rises after poor returns, and a target that has quietly slipped. The plausible negatives are a new-chief-executive reset, a halted buyback and a policy change to R&D credits.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Present: the CEO vacancy (since 30 Mar 2026); members -2.1 percent and UK practice volumes falling; cash conversion 70.6 percent; leverage 1.63x; adjusted profit before tax £84.9m against reported £32.0m (amortisation of acquired intangibles, contingent consideration, £10.6m of exceptional costs); about £16m of R&D tax credits inside EBITDA; base salaries up 14 to 19 percent from July 2026 (CEO to £570,000) after a -22.1 percent three-year total shareholder return, with a proposed rise in the maximum bonus to 150 percent of salary; engagement apparently lower in H2 FY2026; the doubling target moved back a year; Animed's price-testing losses in H1; capital expenditure cut to about 4 percent of revenue, which the risk register flags as a possible under-investment "false friend" if sustained.
  • Plausible: a reset of targets and a kitchen-sink of costs by a new chief executive; a halted or unrenewed buyback (about 2 percent of EPS); restriction of the R&D credit regime (about £11m to £12m after tax, about 17 percent of EPS, derived); a repeat cyber incident (the 2024 attack cost about £7m of revenue and £4.2m to £5m of EBITDA).
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

F3826th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement. The culture is improving but is not a holistic, self-reinforcing system of the Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair kind. Clinical standards, research and governance are strong; employee engagement has swung widely and may have fallen again in the second half of FY2026; the corporate model loses senior clinicians after acquisitions; and alignment between goals (growth), incentives (EBITDA-weighted bonuses) and outcomes (flat EPS) is incomplete.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Engagement: employee Net Promoter Score +14.6 (June 2023), +13.9 (March 2024), -2.8 (June 2024, cyber attack and system migration), +3.1 (FY2025), +10.0 (December 2025), +5.8 (FY2026); attrition falling.

  • Incentives: bonus 80 percent adjusted EBITDA and 20 percent sustainability; long-term incentives 50 percent EPS growth above inflation and 50 percent relative shareholder return against the FTSE 250.
Group judgement

Risks

B+604th of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile has improved: the company-breaking regulatory risk has gone, the balance sheet is comfortable, and the downside to a fair trough multiple is about 13 to 19 percent with a private-market value well above the price. What remains is a slow risk and two sharp ones. The slow risk is that acquired growth keeps masking a UK practice engine that grows about 1 percent, so EPS growth stays arithmetic and the multiple stays low. The sharp ones are a loss of the R&D credit (about 17 percent of EPS) and the reception of the new chief executive. For the model: include explicit sensitivity lines for UK practice volume, wage inflation, the R&D credit and the share count, because those four explain most of the plausible range.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

B+565th of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. The one shock that fitted this shape, a structural CMA remedy, has gone. What remains are medium-magnitude shocks, of which two deserve explicit stress lines in the model: a restriction of the R&D tax credit (low to medium plausibility, about 17 percent of EPS) and a further statutory payroll step (high plausibility each year, a few percent of EBITDA per step). The others are low-probability, medium-magnitude operational risks.

Each risk (derived where marked).

  • R&D tax credit restricted or removed: about £16m in FY2026 EBITDA; after tax about £11m to £12m, about 17 percent of EPS; plausibility low to medium (policy and HMRC scrutiny; no change in our files); sudden at a fiscal event.
  • Payroll legislation step: the April 2025 step cost about £11m (about 7.6 percent of EBITDA unmitigated); the 28 Oct 2026 Budget added none per our Geography Digest; each April's wage-floor rise costs a few million pounds; plausibility high, magnitude low to medium.
  • Cyber attack repeat: the April 2024 attack cost about £7m of revenue and £4.2m to £5m of EBITDA; systems now cloud-based; low plausibility, medium magnitude.
  • Medicines supply disruption: two contracts supply all medicines; alternatives exist; low plausibility, medium short-term magnitude.
  • Clinical exodus after the leadership change: senior vets or acquired founders leaving; low to medium plausibility; medium magnitude for UK volume.
  • Australian shock: an economic downturn, a regulatory change under the new merger regime, an integration failure or a goodwill impairment; Australia is about 16 percent of pre-central EBITDA; low plausibility.
  • Renewed regulatory or political intervention on vet prices: low plausibility for the horizon after binding orders; high magnitude.
  • Covenant or refinancing: very low (1.63x against 3.25x; facility to 2030).
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

A741st of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. Concentrated, and concentrated in the right things for predictability: few, mechanical and monitorable. The forecasts differ from FY2020 to FY2026 in one large respect, adjusted EPS compounding at 9 to 11 percent a year against a decline of 3.6 percent a year from FY2023 to FY2026, and in two smaller ones, free cash flow roughly doubling in FY2027 and leverage peaking at 1.85x. Revenue growth (6.2 to 7.0 percent against 5.4 to 8.6 percent) and the headline margin (19.7 to 20.3 percent against 19.7 to 20.0 percent on the adjusted basis) barely change. Four drivers explain about 85 percent of the EPS difference: the share count (new), the pace and price of acquisitions (continuing, but at 6x to 7.5x rather than the UK's historical 10x), the absence of the shocks that hit FY2024 to FY2026 (cyber, the payroll-tax step, CMA costs) and the R&D credit stepping down rather than up. The UK organic core, which drove FY2020 to FY2023 and whose stall drove the decline since, is held unchanged and so explains little of the difference. The concentration cuts two ways. It makes the forecast simple and the monitoring list short. It also means the forecast's risk is concentrated in two decisions (deal pace, buyback renewal) that pass to an unnamed chief executive, in one policy item (the R&D regime) and in one behavioural spread (price against wages net of volume) whose outcome will only be visible twice a year.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The difference, line by line (model against FactSet history and the brokers'-basis actuals). Revenue growth: FY2021 to FY2026 averaged about 8.6 percent including the pandemic year, 6.5 percent excluding it; the forecast is 6.2 to 7.0 percent. Adjusted EBITDA margin: 19.7, 20.0, 19.9 percent for FY2024 to FY2026; 19.7 to 20.3 percent forecast. EBITDA growth: about 6 and 5 percent in FY2025 and FY2026; 5.2 percent in FY2027 then 7.3 to 7.6 percent. Adjusted EPS: 95.5p, 86.6p, 80.1p, 85.6p for FY2023 to FY2026; 93.3p, 102.1p, 112.9p, 125.6p, 139.8p forecast. Free cash flow on our basis: about £29m in FY2026 (derived); £60m in FY2027 and £86m by FY2031. Net interest: £8.6m (FY2020) to £16.8m (FY2026) on FactSet's basis; £18.1m to £20.4m forecast, a much slower rise. Shares: 70.6m (FY2020) to 71.7m (FY2025), then 69.2m (FY2026), 66.3m (FY2027) and 61.9m (FY2031).
  • The EBITDA bridge FY2026 to FY2031, +£56.8m (model). Australia +£34.0m, UK deals +£9.4m, UK organic +£12.0m, Laboratories and Animed +£7.0m, central costs −£2.6m, R&D credit −£3.2m. About 70 percent of EBITDA growth is bought; about 21 percent is UK organic, and that organic piece is price on falling-then-flat volume. By comparison, FY2020 to FY2023 EBITDA growth of about £60m (FactSet operating) was mostly organic on 7 to 10 percent LFL; FY2023 to FY2026 growth of about £15m was acquisitions net of shocks and the Crematoria sale.
  • The EPS bridge FY2026 to FY2027, +7.7p. Share count about +4.7p; acquisitions after interest about +2.3p; organic, Laboratories and Animed about +2.5p; R&D credit −3.4p; costs below EBITDA and tax about −1.4p. Three items (share count, acquisitions, R&D) are 80 percent of the gross movement. In FY2028 to FY2031 the share count's contribution falls to about 1 to 2 points a year and the acquisition and PBT-leverage contributions rise; the composition shifts but stays concentrated.
  • Why the concentration is mechanical. The share count for FY2027 is 95 percent known (66.73m voting rights at late October, programme to finish in November). The FY2026 Australian deals (14 sites) roll over by arithmetic; two post-year-end deals and two signed add £8.9m of consideration; the £15m UK deal is signed. Net interest is set by a facility whose margin and swap are disclosed. The R&D step-down is set by an accounting method the company has already changed. What is not mechanical is the price-volume outcome in the UK, worth about ±3p of FY2027 EPS for each point of volume and ±6p for each point of price, and the pace of new deals, worth about 0.5p per £10m of deployment in the first year.
  • Against the past, the drivers have become more concentrated. In FY2020 to FY2023 the difference from history came from a diffuse, market-wide demand surge (pet ownership, pandemic deferral, pricing) across hundreds of practices. In FY2024 to FY2026 it came from three discrete shocks. From here it comes from a capital-allocation programme run from the centre. A central programme is easier to forecast and easier to stop: one board decision on buybacks or one regulatory decision on R&D credits moves EPS by 2 to 14 percent.
  • The concentration of risk. The R&D credit: about 13p of FY2027 EPS if the regime were lost, with HMRC enquiry windows open on £38.3m of claims and a recognition discount now lower than VetPartners' (OWN-1). The chief executive: deal pace and buyback renewal are his or her first decisions; the OWN-4 precedents show new chief executives at Vimian, Restore, Pets at Home and Terveystalo changed targets, took charges or re-cut capital returns within twelve months, while Mears did not. The price-wage spread: visible only at the interims through the clinical staff ratio. Australian multiples and the ACCC: visible deal by deal.
  • How peers compare. Medicover's forecast difference rests on diffuse organic growth across many countries; Spire's rested on a single cost programme and a bid; Pets at Home's on a retail turnaround plus vet joint-venture maturation. CVS sits nearer Spire: a few central levers, an activist, and a private-market value above the public one.

Implication for predictability. A concentrated, mechanical driver set is positive for forecasting the next 18 months and for monitoring: four lines (share count, deals done, clinical staff ratio, the R&D line in the interims) carry most of the information. It is negative for resilience, because the forecast has few independent sources of error to offset each other and two of its drivers can be switched off by a single decision. On balance positive, because the two switch-off risks are low probability within 18 months and the FY2027 part of the programme is already executed.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

F4230th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The defining change is a vacancy, not an arrival: Chief Executive Richard Fairman announced his retirement on 30 Mar 2026 and, seven months on, no successor is named. Around it, almost everything else has moved: a new Chief Operating Officer from IVC Evidensia, a Chief Veterinary Officer on the board, the Deputy Chief Executive gone, a first Australian Managing Director, a Chief Client Officer being recruited, three new non-executive directors and a new Senior Independent Director, and an activist on the register. The signal is a board shifting from a finance-led allocator towards wanting an operator ("requisite operational skills"), and from share issuance to buybacks under activist pressure. Until a name is announced the change caps the multiple; once announced it is a binary event, and a new chief executive will very probably reset the medium-term targets within 6 to 12 months.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The chief executive: a chartered accountant (EY, PwC; finance roles at Virgin Money and RAC) who moved from CFO to CEO in 13 months in 2019; his tenure delivered the Australian entry (July 2023), the sale of the Netherlands, Ireland and Crematoria, the cyber recovery and the CMA navigation, alongside a three-year total shareholder return of -22.1 percent to June 2025 and adjusted EPS falling from 95.46p (FY2023) to 85.6p (FY2026). A succession sub-committee formed on 22 Jul 2026 and an external search firm is engaged; he stays until a successor is appointed.
  • Continuity: CFO Robin Alfonso (since November 2019; strong cash discipline) anchors the finance function; the research calls him isolated after the Deputy CEO's departure.
  • The board: Jane Bednall and Helen Keays joined on 1 Apr 2026 and Laura Hagan on 1 May 2026 (consumer, retail and technology experience); Deborah Kemp retired on 30 Jun 2026; independence about 70 percent.
  • Outside actors: Converium Capital (2 percent economic stake) demanded a £100m buyback in May 2026 and threatened to nominate directors; the board launched £50m thirteen days later. Kelso Group welcomed it. Swedbank Robur holds 3.01 percent.
  • What a new chief executive is likely to do: the "double adjusted EBITDA by FY2028" ambition (originally FY2027, set at the November 2022 Capital Markets Day) is out of reach on consensus (FY2028 EBITDA £158m against a doubling of any FY2022 base, which on FactSet's operating basis was £95m), and the 4 to 8 percent LFL range has been missed for three years. A successor is likely to rebase both, review the low-margin online business and the laboratories, and set a new capital-return policy. The rebase would mainly affect medium-term targets, because consensus already assumes only about 2.5 to 3 percent organic growth.

Implication for the model. Hold the operating path unchanged through the vacancy; carry a "new-CEO reset" event in H2 FY2027 to H1 FY2028 as a risk to targets and to buyback renewal rather than to consensus EBITDA.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F5225th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The strategy is a reaffirmation with re-weighted priorities, not a change. Capital has pivoted to Australia since 2023 and UK acquisitions restarted in September 2026; organic priorities have moved from clinical capacity (the old bottleneck) to the client (the new one): Healthy Pet Club Advanced, online booking, a national "CVS Vets" brand, a Chief Client Officer and wider nurse roles. It is clearly articulated and specifically resourced on the capital side, thinner and untested on the organic side, and likely to change again under a new chief executive.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Articulation: a stated framework (4 to 8 percent LFL, 19 to 23 percent adjusted EBITDA margin, cash conversion above 70 percent, leverage below 2.0x, capital expenditure of about £30m, acquisitions of about £50m a year deployed "dynamically" across both countries); a "pet health in your pocket" digital vision.
  • Resourcing: a £350m facility to 2030 with over £150m of undrawn facilities and cash; the capital-expenditure cut from £36.4m to about £30m frees cash for acquisitions and buybacks.
  • Organic levers in flight: Healthy Pet Club Advanced (about 14,500 clients in three months; trial practices showed better volume and EBITDA); over 150,000 online appointments, 15 percent out of hours; online membership sign-up from August 2026 taking 50 percent of new registrations out of hours; joint branding on over 80 percent of UK companion practices to enable national marketing; nurses doing dental work to release vet time; an AI scribe trial.
  • What is missing: a dated Australian milestone (the £105m to £135m-plus long-term EBITDA ambition has no date) and any answer to UK volume that has been tested at scale.

The medium-term framework was reaffirmed after three years below its LFL range, FY2027 was pointed at consensus rather than above it, and the Capital Markets Day ambition to double adjusted EBITDA was quietly moved back a year to FY2028, where it is now out of reach. A departing chief executive is unlikely to raise anything; the next change to targets will come from the successor and is more likely down than up.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

A723rd of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Australia is a sensible, disciplined and so far successful entry, and the company's one genuine growth engine: a fragmented market, entry at about 6x EBITDA against about 10x historically in the UK, acquired practices at EBITDA margins of 25 percent-plus, and a platform built to 61 sites in three years. The runway is long (a decade or more at the current pace), so it is a steady compounding driver rather than a near-term step change. New product markets are limited to extensions of the subscription model.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Scale and economics: Australian revenue £79.1m in FY2026 (+51.8 percent from £52.1m), about 11 percent of group revenue and about 16 percent of group EBITDA before central costs (about £20m); 35 practices on 57 sites at June 2026, 61 sites by September; six practices (14 sites) bought in FY2026 for about £43m to £46m of initial consideration; two more completed post year-end for £4.9m and two signed for £4.0m.
  • Market: about A$5.7bn across about 3,600 to 3,800 practices, 15 to 20 percent corporate-owned; VetPartners 7 percent, Greencross 6 percent, CVS 1 to 2 percent; about 40 percent of 1,100 metropolitan practices meet CVS's criteria.
  • Discipline: about 19 percent of leads converted; target practices of four or five vets; about 80 percent of consideration paid up front, 20 percent deferred; an immediate move to a single preferred wholesaler and preferred laboratory and crematoria partners; local clinical advisory committees.
  • Risks: private-equity competition for targets and a disputed entry multiple; synergies that come mainly from procurement while clinical wage savings are slow (Vets Central CFO); landlord-transfer friction; local vet shortages; unhedged translation; contingent consideration expensed outside adjusted profit.
  • The arithmetic of the runway: reaching the long-term ambition of £105m to £135m of Australian EBITDA from about £20m needs roughly £85m to £115m more, or about £500m to £700m of capital at 6x (derived): a decade at £50m a year, so Australia adds about £6m to £8m of EBITDA a year, roughly 4 to 5 percent group EBITDA growth.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F4224th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Price rises are real in the sense of being applied and sticking (about 3 to 5 percent a year, with larger rises in summer 2026), but they are nominal, not real, against the company's own cost inflation, and they are being bought with volume: members, preventive visits and wellness diagnostics are falling while price rises. Management increasingly names price as its first lever. The FY2027 test is whether the larger summer increases hold volume now that every price is published online.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Applied: UK practice revenue roughly flat on falling volume implies about 3 to 5 percent price (derived); Healthy Pet Club revenue per member up about 5 to 6 percent (derived); Deutsche Bank says CVS implemented larger service price increases in summer 2026 and expects price to contribute more to organic growth; an IVC Evidensia director says corporate price reviews continued at historical rates through the CMA investigation.
  • Not above cost inflation: CPI 3.5 percent; wage floors rising about 4 percent or more a year; employment costs rose as a share of revenue (52.2 to 52.3 percent). Real pricing power (2 percent a year or more above cost inflation) is not demonstrated.
  • Erosion signs: members -2.1 percent; owners declining preventive care and switching to generics; independents about £20 to £25 cheaper per consultation; an expert's "perceived ceiling" for elective services; transparency now live.
  • The comparison that matters: Pets at Home's joint-venture practices, priced near independents, grew vet consumer revenue 5.0 percent in FY2026 against CVS's 1.0 percent practice LFL, which suggests part of CVS's volume loss is the price of its price.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

A702nd of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Acquisitions are stepping up, sensibly and with discipline: spend rose to £45.8m in FY2026 from £30.9m, the target is about £50m a year across both countries, and the UK has reopened after a pause of over two years. Integration in Australia is ahead of the business cases. Each year's £50m at about 6x adds about 4 percent to adjusted EPS on a full-year basis (derived), less at UK prices. The cautions are that the adjusted line excludes real costs of the model (contingent consideration and acquisition fees), and that acquired growth masks a flat organic engine.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Pace and price: Australia about 6x EBITDA (company, Deutsche Bank); the first UK deal, two sites and nine vets, £15m initial, said to be below the group's implied multiple; management will not return to UK multiples of about 10x (peers paid up to 15x historically).
  • Accretion (derived): £50m at 6x buys about £8.3m of EBITDA; less about £1.5m of depreciation and lease cost and £2.8m of interest at about 5.6 percent gives about £4.0m of profit before tax and about £2.8m after 30 percent tax, about 4.2p a share (about 4 percent of FY2027 EPS) once annualised. At 9x to 10x in the UK, about 2 to 3 percent.
  • Return on capital: about a 17 percent pre-tax EBITDA yield at 6x, about 10 to 13 percent after tax and maintenance spend before synergies, against a cost of capital of about 8 to 8.5 percent (Jefferies uses 8.5 percent); management's hurdle is an internal rate of return above 10 percent and it expects acquired Australian practices to reach a 19 to 23 percent return on capital in three to five years.
  • Integration: practices ahead of plans; single-wholesaler synergies immediate; procurement deals for laboratories and crematoria.
  • Costs outside the adjusted line: business combination costs of £8.9m in H1 FY2026, of which £6.8m contingent consideration expensed because it is tied to vendors' continued employment.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

A723rd of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Yes, materially and in both directions at once. The company returned £31.7m by buyback in FY2026 after none in the previous four years (the cash-flow history shows small issuance in FY2022 to FY2025), has about £21m of its £50m programme still to buy, raised leverage from 1.18x to 1.63x, refinanced, and says it will exceed its 2.0x guardrail temporarily for acquisitions. The buyback is accretive at today's price. Its weakness is that it was activist-prompted and has no stated policy beyond the current programme, so renewal is a choice for the next chief executive.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Programmes: £20m completed January 2026 (partly to absorb AIM-holder selling); £50m launched 26 May 2026; 2.3m shares bought for about £28.8m by early September 2026; diluted shares 67.8m against 71.7m at FY2025.
  • Accretion: at 12.4x 24-month-forward earnings the buyback buys an earnings yield of about 8 percent with debt costing about 4 percent after tax; completing it takes the share count to about 66.2m (derived), worth about 2 to 3 percent more on FY2027 EPS than a static count.
  • Balance sheet: net bank debt £199.6m; £350m facility to May 2030 at a margin 20 basis points lower, with an option to 2031; covenant 3.25x; dividend progressive (9.0p from 8.5p).
  • Pressure: Converium wants £100m; Jefferies prefers acquisitions to buybacks; Deutsche Bank sees leverage rising comfortably towards 2.0x as about £50m a year is deployed.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Several smaller positive forces are present, each modest alone; together they are the company's organic answer to flat volumes. The two with real potential are Healthy Pet Club Advanced (a mechanism to convert deferred routine care into recurring paid visits) and laboratory internalisation and diagnostics. The plausible larger forces are corporate actions: a renewed buyback, a laboratory sale, or a bid.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Present: Healthy Pet Club Advanced (about 14,500 clients in three months; trials showed better volume and EBITDA); digital booking and online sign-up; the national CVS Vets brand; laboratory momentum (third-party volumes +4.3 percent, desktop analysers, acquired practices moved onto CVS laboratories); Animed's return to profit in H2 FY2026 on its new platform (Subscribe & Go about 8 percent of monthly sales); nurses taking on dental procedures; the Main Market listing and FTSE 250 inclusion broadening the shareholder base.
  • Plausible: a second buyback after the £50m completes; a laboratory sale (over £100m per Deutsche Bank); Veterinary Surgeons Act reform widening nurse roles; a private-equity approach, given the valuation gap and the sector's take-private wave.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

B+624th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. No actor controls CVS (free float about 99.4 to 100 percent; insiders about 0.6 percent), but an activist has already changed its capital allocation and has signalled it wants more: a larger buyback, lower capital spending and board representation. The pressure is on capital returns, not operating strategy, and it raises the probability of a renewed buyback, a sale of a non-core asset or a bid if the discount persists. It is supportive to the equity and neutral to the business.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Converium Capital (Montreal): a 2 percent economic stake disclosed in May 2026; a public letter urging a £100m buyback, criticising capital spending of about 6 percent of revenue and citing three- and five-year shareholder returns of -49 percent; a threat to nominate directors; it disclosed it may trade options or short, so its interests may diverge from long-term holders.
  • Others: Kelso Group (about 250,000 shares; publicly welcomed the buyback and refinancing); Swedbank Robur 3.01 percent (June 2026).
  • Board response: the £50m buyback thirteen days after the letter; capital expenditure guided down to about £30m; refinancing.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

D6316th of 30

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The positive corporate changes are strong, funded and executing on capital allocation, and narrow and untested on operations. Three financial forces (acquisitions in two countries, a buyback of about 8 percent of the shares, deliberate use of the balance sheet) plus genuine cost discipline will deliver near-mechanical EPS growth of about 6 to 8 percent a year before any organic recovery. What they do not do is change the operating trajectory: no guidance has been raised, the organic initiatives are small and new, and the most important change, the chief executive, is still a vacancy whose direction is unknown.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Resourcing: free cash flow of about £70m to £77m a year (company basis and consensus), over £150m of undrawn facilities and cash, a guardrail with headroom to 2.0x and a covenant at 3.25x.
  • Mechanics (derived): the buyback adds about 3 to 4 percent to FY2027 EPS on the average count; £50m a year of acquisitions adds about 3 to 4 percent a year once annualised; price-led organic growth roughly offsets wage inflation.
  • The swing factor: the successor's profile and first decisions (targets, buyback, laboratories, Animed).
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

F4223rd of 30

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. The staircase is dense with dated events but has few novel positive steps, and the two that are novel are binary. Within six months the market gets the four-month trading update, the probable chief executive appointment, the completion of the buyback and the interims of 4 Mar 2027, which read against the hardest comparative of the cycle. In months 6 to 18 the new chief executive's strategy, the easy H2 FY2027 comparative and evidence on Healthy Pet Club Advanced are the steps that could re-rate the shares. In months 18 to 36 the ageing pet cohort and Australian scale are the well-founded structural steps, and a bid or a laboratory sale are the plausible corporate ones.

Within 6 months (to April 2027).

  • Four-month trading update with the AGM, late November 2026: the first read on FY2027 LFL after the summer price rises and the Healthy Pet Club Advanced launch. Moderately novel; well-founded as an event; likely to show positive but low-single-digit LFL.
  • Chief executive appointment: the most novel step, timing unknown (the search has run since July 2026), direction unknown.
  • Buyback completion (about £21m left) and a possible new programme: familiar; a renewal would be novel and activist-supported.
  • Deals: completion of the £15m UK practice; further Australian deals (two completed and two signed post year-end); familiar.
  • CMA order implementation passing without visible margin damage: quietly positive.
  • Half-year trading update (January) and interims (4 Mar 2027): LFL against a 2.7 percent comparative; first disclosure of Advanced membership numbers. Read-across: Pets at Home's results in late November.

Months 6 to 18 (to April 2028).

  • The new chief executive's strategy and targets (probably a capital markets event in 2027): novel; could be a reset (negative for the narrative, neutral for consensus) or a credible plan (positive).
  • FY2027 trading update (July 2027) and results (September 2027): H2 laps about 1.5 percent and a heat-hit fourth quarter, so the best chance of a 3 percent-plus print.
  • Healthy Pet Club Advanced scale and membership turning up: the organic proof the market wants; moderately founded on trial results.
  • The April 2027 wage-floor step; the swap expiry in February 2028: negative, known.
  • Australia at about 70 sites; a steady UK deal run-rate; possible Veterinary Surgeons Act proposals: incremental.

Months 18 to 36 (to October 2029).

  • The pandemic pet cohort entering higher-need ages: well-founded biologically; timing of the revenue effect uncertain.
  • Australia approaching a fifth of group EBITDA: well-founded on the current pace; a mix re-rating case.
  • The FY2028 "double EBITDA" deadline: likely missed or replaced; negative if still standing.
  • Corporate action (a bid, a laboratory sale, an activist escalation) if the discount persists: plausible, undated.

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

C-6011th of 30

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

C-6011th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry structure is CVS's strongest external foundation and its value chain its weakest. The UK is a concentrated, rational, margin-first oligopoly where prices rise through volume declines and no rival wants a price war under the regulator's eye; Australia is early and fragmented, a long runway for a disciplined buyer. But inside that structure the advantaged model is the joint-venture network, not the fully corporate one; customers are deferring routine care; transparency and online pharmacies are eroding the medicine margin; insurers are tightening; and Australian bidding is rising. For the model this means: a margin that can be defended by price and procurement, a UK organic share that drifts slightly down without acquisitions, medicine revenue growing slower than service revenue, and acquisition economics in Australia that hold at about 6x to 7x for the next two years with upward pressure.

Value chain dynamics

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?F4526th of 30full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F4029th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. CVS sells direct to owners through its practices, so intermediaries matter only at two points, and both are turning slightly against it. Insurers, which have buffered price rises, are capping costly diagnostics while uptake falls on premium inflation; online pharmacies have been inserted by the regulator as a mandated alternative route for medicines. Its own digital route (online booking and sign-up, Animed Direct) is a modest help.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Insurance: about 50 percent UK penetration (4.6m policyholders) against about 15 percent in Australia; coverage limits on MRI and similar; reduced uptake (expert, September 2026).
  • Online pharmacies: mandatory disclosure of prescription rights; Molecule Group building direct links into independents' systems.
  • Own channels: over 150,000 online appointments; 50 percent of online membership sign-ups out of hours; Animed Direct relaunched with Subscribe & Go and next-day delivery.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

D5016th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Corporate competitors' behaviour helps CVS (rational pricing, no price war, compliance burdens that push independents to sell), while the better-structured competitor, Pets at Home's joint-venture network, and resurgent independents hurt it at the margin by taking clinicians and price-sensitive clients. CVS's position is moderately robust: it will not lose share quickly, but it is losing the organic share contest slowly and needs acquisitions to stand still.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Pets at Home: vet consumer revenue +5.0 percent, joint ventures +6.3 percent, clinician turnover 27 percent, about 50 percent of clients on care plans, priced near independents; but its vet growth also slowed to +1.3 percent in its last quarter.
  • IVC Evidensia: larger procurement scale and better supplier terms (expert).
  • Independents: experts expect them to take share on trust and price; the CMA burden falls harder on them.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?D5019th of 30full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

C5212th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Disruption is acting mainly on demand and on regulation-driven channel choice, mildly on competition in CVS's favour, and hardly at all on suppliers. It leaves CVS's position moderately robust: the service core is untouched, the medicine margin erodes slowly, and scale operators gain from technology.

Each actor.

  • Demand: online pharmacy and generics pull medicine spend out of the clinic; the regulator's disclosure rule accelerates it; consultations are untouched.
  • Competition: AI scribes and cloud systems favour scaled operators over legacy independents; CVS migrated its UK estate to Provet Cloud.
  • Regulators and political actors: the remedies are themselves a disruption designed to shift medicine purchasing and make prices comparable; the Veterinary Surgeons Act reform could re-cut clinical roles.
  • Suppliers: manufacturer direct-to-clinic and white-label contracts bypass wholesalers in CVS's favour; nothing disruptive against it.
  • Other actors (insurers): coverage limits on expensive diagnostics are the one to watch for referral and laboratories.
