C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

D5522nd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. In UK first-opinion veterinary care CVS is a top-three corporate operator with about 8 to 9 percent of practices, a long way behind the clear number one, IVC Evidensia, and roughly level with Pets at Home's joint-venture network, which has more practices and more consumer revenue. The research's label of "second-largest" holds by some counts and not others; the Consumer should treat CVS as number two or three, not a leader. In Australia it is a small third corporate at 1 to 2 percent. In UK reference laboratories it is a meaningful player (three laboratories, an overnight courier fleet, half its volume from external clinics); in online pet pharmacy it is a minor one.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • UK: CVS about 423 UK practices, about 8.4 to 9 percent; IVC Evidensia about 1,000 UK practices (expert) and about £720m of EBITDA across over 2,500 sites in 20 markets, roughly five times CVS's group EBITDA; Pets at Home 455 practices (87 percent joint ventures) with vet consumer revenue of £688m against CVS UK practice revenue of about £569m (derived) and about 19 percent of UK vet share by its own measure; VetPartners (about 10 percent among the unlisted), Medivet and Mars-owned Linnaeus complete the corporate tier.
  • Australia: VetPartners 7 percent, Greencross 6 percent, CVS 1 to 2 percent; 84 percent independent.
  • Distance to the top: closing the gap to IVC by acquisition is neither possible (the CMA's 30 percent local ceiling, few attractive targets) nor the strategy.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C7012th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The UK market is concentrated at the top and fragmented in the tail: corporates own about 60 percent of practices (from 10 percent in 2013), the top five about 45 percent of clinics, well above the 30 percent test, and six groups hold the corporate tier, so the curve is steep at the top (the ratio of the top five to numbers six to ten is not measured, but the next tier is small regional groups, so it is very likely above five times). Australia is the opposite, at 15 to 20 percent corporate, which is the opportunity.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • UK: top five about 45 percent of registered clinics and over 3,000 sites; one expert puts corporate share at about 80 percent; six corporate groups over 60 percent of clinics.
  • Australia: two private-equity leaders at 7 and 6 percent; CVS 1 to 2 percent.
  • Local markets matter more than national: the CMA's 30 percent local share ceiling binds in some towns and is the effective constraint on UK consolidation.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

D5617th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Corporate share rose fast for a decade and has now plateaued; within the corporate tier, shares are stable except for Pets at Home's joint-venture model, which has taken about 6 points since 2017. CVS's own share has been stable at 8 to 9 percent through a two-year acquisition pause, which means its organic share has probably drifted slightly down given its below-market practice growth. New entrants, chiefly de novo independents, are gaining at the edges but no study measures it. Online pharmacies are the entrant that has clearly taken share, in medicines.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Corporate share 10 percent (2013) to 60 percent (2026); "peak maturity" per experts; only 10 to 15 percent of remaining practices are attractive.
  • Pets at Home gained about 600 basis points of UK vet share since 2017; its joint-venture practices grew consumer revenue 6.3 percent in FY2026 against CVS practice LFL of 1.0 percent.
  • Independents re-forming and marketing local ownership (expert calls, February to March 2026).
  • Online: Chewy and Molecule Group (Hyperdrug) scaling; most chronic-medicine sales have already moved online (expert, September 2026).
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

F4224th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Share consolidated strongly towards the largest groups for a decade by acquisition; it is now consolidating organically towards the most advantaged model, which is Pets at Home's joint-venture structure (owner-vets, near-independent pricing, lower clinician turnover), and towards integrated platforms with laboratories and referral. CVS is among the largest but not among the most advantaged: its integrated laboratory and referral chain is an advantage, but its fully corporate model is the one the three-speed market is squeezing. In Australia, where consolidation is early, scale and procurement still decide, and CVS is gaining.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Pets at Home: vet group profit before tax +10.4 percent to £83.8m, gross margin 55.7 percent, clinician turnover down from 37 to 27 percent, practices maturing from £1.1m to £1.5m average revenue; the CMA singled out its joint ventures as pricing near independents.
  • Corporate model: experts say retention is harder in corporates; a former CVS director (July 2025) said CVS had lost its cultural reputation among vets.
  • CVS's integrated assets: laboratories with over 30 percent EBITDA margins, referral hospitals, procurement scale; Deutsche Bank names laboratory and referral integration as CVS's moat.
  • The regulatory ceiling (30 percent local share) caps further concentration towards the largest in the UK.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

A823rd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Competitors put margin first and are benign: the corporate tier prices centrally, competes on procurement rather than price, and has raised prices by about 5 percent a year through three years of falling volume. Industry prices do not fall when volume falls, which is the core support for CVS's margin. The pressure on price comes from the regulator, online pharmacies and price-conscious owners, not from rivals. The exception is the online channel, where price competition is intense.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Prices rose through the volume decline (expert and Deutsche Bank evidence; the CMA found large corporates about 18 percent above independents).
  • Consolidators drive profitability through drug supply terms (expert); pricing set in percentile bands.
  • No evidence of loss-making for share in clinical services; Animed Direct at about a 3 percent EBITDA margin shows the contrast online.
  • Owners of the corporates are long-term private equity (EQT, CVC, BC Partners and others) and Mars, all focused on EBITDA and exit value.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

A762nd of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The attitude is stable and has not changed in the corporate tier over three years; six groups under the same regulatory spotlight have behaved alike, and a corporate price war while the CMA's transparency order bedded in would be irrational. A shark attack on clinical prices is unlikely. Two changes are visible at the edges: independents' attitude has hardened (proudly local and cheaper), and private-equity bidding for Australian practices has intensified, which is competition for assets, not customers. A plausible future instability is a highly levered private-equity owner cutting price or buying share if its own volumes fall, but nothing in the evidence suggests it.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Corporate behaviour: unchanged pricing reviews through the investigation (IVC Evidensia director).
  • Independents: competing on local trust and lower consultation fees (£50 against £70 to £75).
  • Australia: multiples quoted at 6x, 5x to 8x and 9x to 10x; the Vets Central CFO says CVS's entry pushed prices up.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C6611th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The structure is supportive: a concentrated, rational UK oligopoly in which prices rise through volume declines, plus an early, fragmented Australian market where a scaled buyer can compound at 6x. The support is moderately fragile at three edges, each slow-moving rather than sudden: transparency that makes the corporate price premium visible and nudges medicine sales online, independents and joint-venture models taking talent and trust at the margin, and rising bidding in Australia. The structure protects the base of the thesis (recurring, high-gross-margin service revenue) more than its growth.

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

D5217th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. For consultations, surgery, diagnostics and emergencies there is no substitute; that is about 70 percent of practice revenue and the reason the business is defensive. For medicines the substitute (online pharmacy at 50 to 60 percent less, and generics) is real and strengthening with regulatory help. The most important "substitute" is not a product but behaviour: owners deferring routine care, which has risen with the squeeze and should ease as the cohort ages.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Fees £473.1m against clinical goods £200.1m (FY2025).
  • Online price gap 50 to 60 percent; the transparency order effective 22 Sep 2026.
  • Generic switching: 47 to 48 percent of US practices surveyed have switched or plan to switch for Convenia and Cerenia (Morgan Stanley, October 2026).
  • Deferral: routine and preventive visits weak; wellness laboratory testing declining (US and UK expert calls).

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

B+684th of 30

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

D5020th of 308 answers
Judgement49C-4152C-4255C-4360C-4448C-4547C-4635C-4750
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D5020th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

CVS's advantages are real, specific and ordinary: a subscription that locks in routine care, a laboratory and referral chain with genuine switching costs, procurement scale and a proven acquisition capability in Australia. None is unique, the larger rival and the joint-venture model each beat it on one dimension, and its pricing power is the kind that passes cost through at the expense of volume rather than the kind that expands margins. For the model this means margins held around 20 percent by price, procurement and mix, not expanded by moat; organic share drifting slightly down in the UK; and a weak mechanical tailwind from Australian mix and laboratory utilisation worth roughly 0.1 to 0.2 points of margin a year.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

F5023rd of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. CVS holds real but ordinary advantages for a scaled consolidator: none is unique, and the other corporates hold each in their own form. The strongest and widening is the laboratory network with its own overnight courier fleet; the most valuable commercially is the Healthy Pet Club subscription, stable in revenue and slowly narrowing in members; procurement scale is moderate and widening in Australia; local clinician trust is strong but belongs to the vet, not the company. The UK advantage set is stable to slightly narrowing (transparency, the joint-venture competitor, talent leakage); the Australian one is widening.

Each advantage.

  • Customer: Healthy Pet Club (moderate; members narrowing, revenue widening). Over 40 percent of active patients; £95.5m of recurring revenue (+3.5 percent); about £15 a month by direct debit; members 508,000 (-2.1 percent); a premium tier with unlimited consultations from July 2026. It converts deferrable spend into contracted spend and routes members into the clinic.
  • Customer: local trust and clinical quality (strong locally, not owned). Physical switching is low and rests on the individual clinician; client Net Promoter Score 78.9 in 2025 (68.0 in 2024); clinical research (over 100 peer-reviewed papers a year). It walks out with the vet, which is why retention matters.
  • Production: laboratories and referral (moderate to strong; widening). Three reference laboratories, a door-to-door overnight courier fleet across England and Wales, half the volume from external clinics, EBITDA margins over 30 percent; FY2026 revenue +11.5 percent, volumes +4.3 percent, EBITDA +25.6 percent. Referral hospitals keep complex cases in-house and train staff.
  • Scale: procurement (moderate; widening in Australia). Manufacturer rebates direct, white-label medicines, the Vet Direct buying operation (over 5,000 items) and buying-group services to third parties; non-clinical cost of sales 21.4 percent of revenue; Australian practices moved to a single wholesaler. IVC Evidensia, larger, gets better terms (expert).
  • Capability: acquisition sourcing and integration (moderate; proven in Australia): 61 sites in three years at about 6x, ahead of business cases.
  • Regulatory: none of its own; a mild relative advantage because CMA compliance costs fall harder on independents.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

C-5212th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. CVS is large relative to independents and to most suppliers' other customers, mid-sized among UK corporates and small against IVC Evidensia. Its scale confers purchasing terms, laboratory utilisation, the ability to absorb compliance and technology costs, cheap acquisition finance and national marketing; it does not confer cost leadership, because a bigger rival exists, nor pricing leadership, because the regulator and transparency cap the corporate premium.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Size: revenue £712.8m; about 9,000 staff, 2,532 vets (average) and about 3,300 nurses and patient-care assistants; about 475 UK practices plus 61 Australian sites.
  • Relative: IVC Evidensia about £720m of EBITDA (about five times CVS's £141.5m); Pets at Home vet consumer revenue £688m against CVS UK practice revenue of about £569m (derived); Australian leaders at 7 and 6 percent against CVS's 1 to 2 percent.
  • What scale buys: the £350m facility at SONIA plus 1.25 to 2.50 percent; Provet Cloud across the UK estate after the 2024 cyber attack; laboratories fed half by internal volume; national CVS Vets marketing; compliance absorbed at about £5m one-off.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

F5224th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. CVS's surplus utility is high in consultations, surgery, diagnostics and emergencies, where there is no substitute and its clinical standards and integration are good; it is inferior on medicines, where online pharmacies are 50 to 60 percent cheaper; and it is contested in routine wellness, where owners defer and independents are cheaper. Overall it is not demonstrably inferior, but it is not leading, and transparency is now putting the price side of the value equation in front of every client.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Not inferior: client Net Promoter Score 78.9 (up from 68.0); Healthy Pet Club revenue still rising; referral demand robust; complaints rose under regulatory scrutiny without visible financial damage.
  • Price disadvantage: consultations about £70 to £75 at corporates against about £50 at independents; the CMA found large corporates about 18 percent above independents while Pets at Home's joint ventures sat near parity.
  • Risks to perceived value: mandatory ownership branding (an IVC Evidensia director says corporate signage may cost local trust), clinician turnover breaking continuity of care.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

C4711th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. CVS has and uses pricing power in the nominal sense, demonstrated by application: prices have risen about 3 to 5 percent a year through three years of falling volume, and larger rises were put through in summer 2026. But the erosion of relative utility is measurable rather than minimal: members are falling, routine visits are being deferred and the price gap to independents is now published. This is moderate pricing power, sufficient to pass through cost inflation, not enough to expand margins without volume.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Applied: UK practice revenue roughly flat on falling volume (derived price of about 3 to 5 percent); Healthy Pet Club revenue per member up about 5 to 6 percent (derived); Deutsche Bank expects price to contribute more to organic growth after the summer 2026 increases.
  • Erosion: members -2.1 percent; preventive care declined "because price increases have outpaced wage growth" (expert); wellness laboratory tests declining; employment costs rising slightly as a share of revenue, so price is not running ahead of cost.
  • Where it is strongest: complex, non-shoppable diagnostic and surgical services, where comparison is hard (expert).
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

D3019th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. It largely does not. Procurement and scale savings are retained to defend the margin against wage inflation, and corporate prices sit above independents'. The partial exceptions are the subscription (bundled routine care at a monthly price) and buying-group services sold to independent clinics. This matters because the competitor that does share (Pets at Home's joint ventures, priced near independents) is growing faster, and transparency now rewards sharing.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

C5612th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. The gravitational pull is weakly positive and is mostly financial and geographic rather than competitive. Each year the Australian mix lifts the group margin, the laboratories gain internal volume from acquired practices, procurement rebates scale with volume, and, while it runs, the buyback lifts EPS; from FY2028 the ageing pet cohort should add biological demand. Against that, a treadmill of annual wage-floor rises, amortisation, interest on debt-funded growth and talent leakage from the corporate model absorbs most of it, which is why EPS did not compound from FY2023 to FY2026.

Reasoning and evidence (derived where marked).

  • Australian mix: at margins of 25 percent-plus against a group 19.9 percent, each 3 points of revenue shifting to Australia adds about 0.15 to 0.2 points of group margin (derived); Australia went from about 3.5 to 7.7 to 11 percent of revenue over FY2024 to FY2026.
  • Laboratories: revenue +11.5 percent became EBITDA +25.6 percent.
  • The treadmill: about £3.7m of cost per point of employment-cost inflation (derived); adjusted EPS 95.46p (FY2023) to 85.6p (FY2026) despite revenue growth of about 21 percent over the period.

Group judgement

Company - Management

F4426th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is a disciplined, honest, finance-led allocator that has run the business through three external shocks without breaking it, and has not made it grow per share. Its strengths are capital decisions (entry prices, disposals, refinancing, now buybacks) and clear operating disclosure; its weaknesses are organic delivery, a thin top team, a culture still recovering, pay decisions that sit poorly with the record, and shareholder-friendly actions that needed an activist. The chief executive is leaving, so the forward question is the successor: an operator with clinical credibility would address the main gap and probably reset targets; a caretaker or another finance-track appointment would not. For the model: trust the company's capital-allocation execution and its near-term guidance once set; do not trust its medium-term organic targets.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

D4222nd of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Ambition is large on paper and modest in behaviour. The stated goals (double adjusted EBITDA, Australia eventually rivalling the UK, 4 to 8 percent organic growth) are big; the decision style is measured and low-velocity, and two of the three goals have slipped or been missed. Against peers, CVS is less aggressive than the private-equity consolidators (IVC Evidensia, VetPartners) and less inventive than Pets at Home (joint ventures, insurance, subscriptions), but more disciplined on price paid than either. A new chief executive will define the next ambition.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Australia: £105m to £135m-plus of EBITDA "eventually", undated, from about £20m.
  • Behaviour: a two-year UK acquisition pause during the CMA investigation; selective four-vet-plus criteria; a 19 percent conversion of Australian leads; buybacks only under activist pressure.
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

F5523rd of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. The strategy is smart on capital and ordinary on operations. Recycling 10x disposals into 6x Australian purchases, pausing UK deals while the regulator decided, refinancing early and buying shares at about 12x earnings are right and robust across environments. The operating priorities (client engagement, subscriptions, digital, nurse roles) are sensible responses to flat volume rather than moat-widening moves, and the strategy has no answer to the joint-venture model's advantage in clinician retention and price perception. It is dynamic in where it deploys capital, not in how it runs clinics.

Reasoning and evidence. The Crematoria sale at about 10x (May 2025); the Netherlands and Ireland exits; Australia at about 6x with 25 percent-plus margins; the UK restart at below-group multiples; Healthy Pet Club Advanced, online booking, the CVS Vets brand, the Chief Client Officer; capital expenditure cut to about £30m.

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

But capital expenditure is being cut to about 4 percent of revenue from 5 to 6 percent while cash goes to buybacks, which the company's own risk register flags as a possible false friend if sustained; the leadership bench is thin; and the culture is recovering from a low.

C-61

How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

The Investment Analyst's heading How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking?

Its outlier is capital discipline (entry multiples), not operating execution.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

D4518th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Governance is prudent in its scaffolding and mixed in its behaviour towards shareholders. The structures are sound and were strengthened before they were required (Main Market listing, independent committees, a 200 percent-of-salary shareholding rule, malus and clawback, post-vesting holding, a third-party whistleblowing system, an early controls declaration). But three behaviours put management close to or ahead of shareholders: the buyback came only after an activist's public letter; base salaries rose 14 to 19 percent after a -22 percent three-year return, with a higher bonus cap proposed; and the incentive design rewards adjusted EBITDA, which the acquisition model can buy. The evidence suggests a board that does the right things when pressed, which is why the activist matters.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • For: zero long-term incentive vesting on poor returns; the CEO holds over 200 percent of salary; five insiders bought shares in twelve months and none sold (small sums, under £20,000 on average); single-class shares, no poison pills; about 70 percent board independence.
  • Against: the £50m buyback thirteen days after Converium's letter, after four years of small share issuance; salary rises from 1 Jul 2026 (CEO 16 percent to £570,000, CFO 14 percent to £385,000, CVO 19 percent to £345,000); a proposed maximum bonus of 150 percent of salary with deferral; auditor tenure of nine years; intercompany recharges rising; a related-party acquisition in 2022 (the Deputy CEO's spouse's practice, at arm's length).
C-64

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

D3820th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

CVS's economics are mid-quality and stable rather than strong and improving: high gross margins, a flat 20 percent EBITDA margin with a policy-exposed credit inside it, a 10 to 11 percent EBIT margin, a pre-tax return on capital of about 16 percent, good cash conversion on the right measure, comfortable leverage, a narrow perimeter, and a long runway to deploy capital at returns above its cost. The weaknesses are a wide and recurring gap between adjusted and reported earnings, a low blended return on the capital deployed since FY2023, and operating leverage that cuts both ways on a volume-sensitive, wage-heavy base. For the model: forecast adjusted EPS (the market's basis) but carry reported EPS and cash; model price and volume separately, because price drops through almost fully and volume about half; carry Australian mix as the main margin driver; and show the R&D credit as a separate line so its loss can be stressed.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

D4418th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. CVS is generating mid-to-high single-digit adjusted EPS growth on mid-single-digit revenue growth, after three years of decline, and the growth is mostly arithmetic: the buyback and acquired EBITDA supply most of it, organic price roughly offsets wage inflation, and below-the-line costs absorb a little. FY2027 growth of about 10 percent is durable because it is largely mechanical; FY2028 and beyond are less durable, because the share-count contribution ends unless the buyback is renewed and the organic contribution depends on volume.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • History (adjusted, brokers' basis): 95.46p (FY2023), 86.6p (FY2024, cyber attack), 80.1p (FY2025, higher interest and payroll taxes), 85.6p (FY2026, +6.9 percent on revenue +5.9 percent).

  • FY2027 composition (derived): EBITDA +6.5 percent (acquired roughly 3 to 4 points, organic roughly 2.5 to 3 points); depreciation and interest slightly faster than EBITDA (about -1 point); average share count down about 3.5 to 4.5 percent.
  • Durability: acquired growth lasts while about £50m a year is deployed at 6x to 7x and leverage stays below about 2.0x; the share-count effect is one-off unless renewed; the organic effect needs price to hold volume.
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

A663rd of 30

There is clear scope to deploy about 11 percent of sales a year (about £50m of acquisitions and £30m of capital expenditure) at returns above the cost of capital for five years and more, mainly in Australia. The returns are good rather than high (about 10 to 13 percent after tax at 6x before synergies, nearer 15 percent with them; lower in the UK), which supports steady compounding and a fair multiple somewhat above today's, but not a premium compounder multiple while the organic engine is flat and the deployment is partly debt-funded.

  • Fair multiple: the market pays about 12.4x 24-month-forward earnings; private buyers pay 9x to 14x EBITDA for comparable assets; the gap is the market's discount for flat organic growth and a pending chief executive.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

F1825th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. The earnings base is not clean. Adjusted profit before tax is about 2.7 times reported; the adjusted line excludes items that recur with the acquisition model (contingent consideration expensed for continued employment, deal fees, amortisation of acquired intangibles) and includes a policy-exposed R&D credit worth about 11 percent of EBITDA; exceptional costs are recurrent in practice; and the reported tax rate is 44 percent. The market prices adjusted EPS, so the model should forecast it, but should also carry reported EPS and a cash measure, because a market that re-bases to reported or cash earnings would see a much higher multiple.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The gap: adjusted profit before tax £84.9m against reported £32.0m (FY2026); adjusted EPS 85.6p against reported 24.4p.
  • Recurring items outside "adjusted": business combination costs £8.9m in H1 FY2026 (contingent consideration £6.8m, fees £2.1m); amortisation of acquired intangibles (the difference between FactSet total depreciation and amortisation of £64.7m and adjusted depreciation of about £41m, derived); exceptional costs £10.6m in FY2026 (CMA remedies £5.1m, the listing move and investigation costs), after £4.2m of cyber costs in FY2024.
  • Inside "adjusted": R&D tax credits of £12.8m (FY2024), £15.1m (FY2025) and about £16m (FY2026, derived) within EBITDA; removing them would cut EPS by about 17 percent (derived, after tax).
  • Tax: adjusted about 29 percent (above the 25 percent UK rate; Australian profits taxed at 30 percent, so mix pushes it up); reported 44.4 percent (non-deductible amortisation and costs).

Pricing power

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

C4510th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Price will contribute about 4.0 percent to UK practice revenue in FY2027, 3.5 percent in FY2028 and about 3.1 to 3.3 percent a year thereafter. That is about £20m to £23m of revenue a year, nearly all of it margin before costs. It will add almost nothing net to the UK margin, because it is matched by wage inflation of about 3.4 to 3.7 percent on a staff base worth about 54 percent of revenue, and by drug and other cost inflation of about 3 percent.

It also keeps costing volume: we assume −2.5 percent in FY2027 and −1.5 percent in FY2028, recovering to flat only by FY2030. UK practice LFL is therefore about 1.4 percent in FY2027, 2.0 percent in FY2028, 2.6 percent in FY2029 and about 3 percent by FY2030.

Price adds to group margin only where it is not yet published and compared:

  • in Australia, through fee regularisation after acquisition and procurement (one Adelaide practice went from a 34.2 to a 44.2 percent margin by its second year);
  • in the laboratories.

The asymmetry has changed against CVS since the first set:

  • prices have been published online since late 2025;
  • since 22 Sep 2026 clinics must tell owners they can buy medicines elsewhere;
  • the 18.3 percent premium of corporate groups over independents is now visible;
  • the UK consumer's weakest year is 2027.

The penalty for pushing price is now larger than it was in 2022 to 2023, when corporates put through rises above 10 percent. Our base assumes CVS knows this and moderates from the larger summer 2026 increases. If it instead holds price at 4 percent and volume falls 3.5 percent, FY2027 EPS is about 3 percent lower. If competitive pressure forces price down to 3 percent with no volume gain, EPS is about 6 percent lower.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • What has been applied.
  • UK practice revenue rose about 0.9 percent in FY2026 on volume down about 3 to 4 percent, so price was about 4 to 5 percent (derived).
  • Healthy Pet Club revenue rose 3.5 percent on members down 2.1 percent, so revenue per member rose about 5 to 6 percent.
  • The CEO said on the FY2026 call that summer 2026 rises were "slightly above historical rates", which implies negative practice volume.
  • Jefferies (4 Jan 2023) recorded corporate rises above 10 percent in the 2022 to 2023 inflation spike. That was the start of today's consumer tightening.
  • Why price no longer adds to margin.
  • The employment cost ratio rose from 52.2 to 52.3 percent in FY2026 despite rostering.
  • The April 2026 wage-floor step was about 4 percent and April 2027's is 3.9 percent.
  • Vet salary inflation is easing as vacancies fall (6.5 to 5.8 percent), but salary expectations are sticky (prospectus, January 2026).
  • Excluding the R&D credit, the margin fell 0.75 points over three years of 3 to 5 percent price rises.
  • The new constraints.
  • The CMA found five corporate groups priced 18.3 percent above independents, with Pets at Home's joint ventures near independents (final report, 24 Mar 2026). Consultations cost about £70 to £75 at corporates against about £50 at independents.
  • Mandatory ownership branding removes the appearance of local independence.
  • Morgan Stanley's October 2026 survey has 76 percent of US vets citing higher clinic prices as a driver of falling footfall. US clinic visits fell 2.2 percent in Q3 2026 (Vetsource).
  • Against this, one expert (Julian Samuelson, October 2025) expects some independents to raise prices towards corporate levels. The US hospital transparency precedent shows prices converging on the average rather than collapsing.
  • Competing subscription offers are being bundled more aggressively.
  • IVC Evidensia's Pet Health Club has 1m UK members. Its Plus tier offers unlimited consultations and 20 percent off selected medicines.
  • Medivet's Extra plan gives 10 percent off all bills, and Medivet markets lifetime vaccination bundles at £100.
  • CVS's Healthy Pet Club Advanced (launched 1 Jul 2026, about 14,500 clients in three months, about 3 percent of members) is a volume defence. It dilutes price per visit, and its churn and mix effects will not be visible until FY2027 interims.
  • The medicine margin at risk (our estimate, inside the volume assumption).
  • Clinical goods were about £200m against fees of £473m (FY2025), so about 30 percent of UK practice revenue is goods.
  • Online pharmacies are 50 to 60 percent cheaper. The written-prescription fee cap of £21 plus £12.50 is about neutral against the market average.
  • Migration is slow: 26 percent of owners buy in clinic for immediacy and 28 percent to support their vet, and after a decade of Chewy, US vets still dispense 63 percent of prescriptions (Morgan Stanley). An expert says most chronic medicines have already moved online.
  • We assume about 3 to 4 percent of in-clinic medicine revenue leaves each year for three years. That is about £1m of gross margin a year, partly recaptured by Animed and prescription fees.
  • It is inside our UK volume assumption, not a separate line.
  • Insurance. UK penetration is about 50 percent, and insured dogs spend 93 percent more and visit twice as often (expert, April 2026). That buffers price, but premiums are driving lapses and insurers are capping diagnostics.
  • In Australia, penetration of about 15 percent makes owners more price sensitive, but acquired practices still get fee regularisation and procurement uplift. That is why we hold the Australian margin at about 25 percent while assuming only 2.5 percent organic growth.
  • Sensitivities (FY2027, model). Each point of UK price is worth about £5.8m of EBITDA and 6.2p of EPS. Each point of volume is worth about £2.9m (3.1p). The combination that matters is price minus wage inflation minus half of any volume loss. That is about +0.4 points in FY2027 before productivity and about zero in FY2028 to FY2029.
5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7550P-0147
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

D4520th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Operating leverage is material in the short term and moderate over a year, and it depends sharply on whether revenue moves through price or volume. Price drops through almost entirely; volume drops through at roughly half in the first months and less once rosters flex, as FY2026 showed. Around consensus, each point of LFL moves FY2027 EPS by about 3.5 percent if it comes from volume and about 6 percent if it comes from price, and each point of wage inflation moves it by about 4 percent the other way (all derived). A plausible FY2027 LFL range of 1.5 to 4 percent therefore gives adjusted EPS growth of roughly 5 to 15 percent around a central 10 percent.

Reasoning and evidence (derived).

  • Price: 1 percent on UK practice revenue of about £569m is about £5.7m, almost all margin: about 4 percent of EBITDA, about 6p (6 percent) of EPS after tax.
  • Volume: 1 percent is about £5.7m of revenue; with salaried clinical staff fixed in the short term, drop-through of about 50 percent gives about £2.9m of EBITDA (2 percent) and about 3.5 percent of EPS.
  • Wages: 1 percent of employment cost is about £3.7m, about 2.6 percent of EBITDA and about 4 percent of EPS.
  • Evidence of leverage working both ways: FY2025 LFL of 0.2 percent with the payroll-tax step produced EPS -7.5 percent; the laboratories turned +11.5 percent revenue into +25.6 percent EBITDA; in FY2026 rostering (clinical staff 36.0 to 34.3 percent of revenue) offset flat UK volume.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

F4225th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Fixed-cost operating leverage is real at CVS but narrower than the "high fixed cost" label suggests, and over the forecast period it will hold the UK practice margin flat rather than lift it. Under IFRS 16, rent sits below EBITDA. The EBITDA cost base of the UK practices is therefore about two-thirds staff and one-quarter drugs and consumables. Staff are fixed for three to six months and flexible over six to twelve months through rostering. Drugs and consumables vary with volume.

Two consequences follow for the line items.

  • The UK practice margin stays flat. Excluding the R&D credit it holds at about 18.1 to 18.3 percent through FY2029 and reaches about 18.3 percent by FY2031. The reason is that price (about 3.5 to 4 percent) only offsets wage inflation (about 3.5 percent, net of 0.3 to 0.6 percent productivity) while volume is still falling. The leverage turns positive only when UK volume turns positive, which we place in FY2030.
  • The group margin rises for a different reason. It rises by 0.1 to 0.2 points a year (19.7 percent in FY2027 to 20.3 percent in FY2031) because of mix: Australia at about 25 percent and the laboratories at 33 to 36 percent with incremental margins of about 45 percent. Operating leverage in the core plays no part.

Against FY2020 to FY2026 this is a different regime.

  • In FY2020 to FY2023 leverage worked upward on 7 to 10 percent LFL.
  • In FY2024 to FY2026 it worked against the company. Excluding the R&D credit, the margin eroded from 18.4 to 17.65 percent. The headline was held at 19.7 to 20.0 percent only by a credit rising by £6m, the exits from the Netherlands and Ireland, and rostering.
  • The R&D crutch now stops rising and steps down in FY2027. The headline margin must therefore be carried by mix alone.

The width of outcomes comes almost entirely from UK volume and the spread between price and wages, which is why the cone for FY2027 EBITDA is about £138m to £160m.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The UK practice EBITDA cost base, FY2026 (derived). Revenue is about £569m and EBITDA excluding the R&D credit about £103m, which leaves costs of about £466m:
  • staff about £305m (about 54 percent of revenue; the group figure is 52.3 percent);
  • drugs and consumables about £120m (non-clinical cost of sales is 21.4 percent of group revenue);
  • other costs about £41m.
  • Rent is outside EBITDA. Bank-test EBITDA of about £122.5m (£199.6m ÷ 1.63x) against IFRS 16 EBITDA of £141.5m implies about £19m of rent and lease cost (derived).
  • How each kind of revenue change drops through to EBITDA.
  • Service price drops through at about 95 to 100 percent.
  • On medicines, price tracks drug cost, so it adds margin only to the extent of the mark-up.
  • Lost volume drops through at about 75 percent in the first months, because only drugs and consumables flex. It falls to about 50 percent once rosters adjust.
  • FY2026 shows that the adjustment can be fast. Clinical staff costs fell to 34.3 percent of revenue in H2 from 36.0 percent, and to 34.0 from 35.7 percent in H1. An Instinct Science survey (11 Mar 2026) found fewer than 10 percent of practices still on fixed full-time schedules.
  • We reject the AlphaSense downside arithmetic. It says each 1 percent of volume costs 180 to 220 basis points of practice margin. With 1 percent of UK practice revenue at about £5.7m, that would require a drop-through of more than 300 percent. Its derived scenario of "22 percent below consensus EPS" stacks that error on LFL of −2 percent and an employment cost ratio of 54 percent. We treat it as an extreme tail, not a scenario.
  • Precedents, corrected.
  • H1 FY2020 (LFL 7.4 percent, margin 14.1 to 18.6 percent) is mostly IFRS 16 (finding 2).
  • H1 FY2023 is the more instructive case: practice margin fell from 21.4 to 21.0 percent on strong revenue growth because wage inflation outran price.
  • FY2025 is the clearest downward case: LFL of 0.2 percent plus an £11m payroll-tax step took EPS down 7.5 percent.
  • The lesson is that wage inflation, not volume, is usually what swamps the leverage at CVS. With the April 2027 step known and modest (3.9 percent), FY2027 to FY2028 is a milder wage period than FY2025.
  • The laboratories are the one segment with clear positive leverage.
  • In FY2026 revenue rose 11.5 percent and EBITDA 25.6 percent, an incremental margin of about 64 percent.
  • We use about 45 percent from here, on 6 to 7 percent revenue growth (volumes up 4 to 5 percent plus price). The margin rises from 32 percent to about 36 percent by FY2031, adding about £1m of EBITDA a year.
  • Vimian's diagnostics segment, which converted 13 percent organic growth into a 7.2 percent EBITA margin, is not a read-across. It is a product business still investing.
  • Animed Direct has thin operating leverage. We take the margin from 2.7 percent to 4.5 percent by FY2029, worth about £0.3m to £0.5m a year. Price tests that cut prices lost margin without adding revenue (CFO, 26 Feb 2026).
  • Australia: we claim no operating leverage. The segment margin is held at about 25 percent. Its growth is bought, and procurement synergies are part of the acquired margin.
  • Central costs are negative leverage. They rose 13.8 percent to £9.9m in FY2026. We carry 5 to 6 percent a year for the chief executive search, the Chief Client Officer, executive salary rises of 14 to 19 percent and controls work under Provision 29.
  • How the leverage shapes the line items, FY2026 to FY2031 (model).
  • Revenue compounds at about 6.5 percent a year; EBITDA at about 7 percent.
  • The group margin rises 0.45 points in total. Excluding the R&D credit it rises about 1.4 points, all from mix.
  • The UK practice margin excluding the credit goes 18.1, 18.15, 18.1, 18.1, 18.2 and 18.3 percent.
  • Across FY2023 to FY2026, by contrast, revenue grew about 21 percent while EBITDA rose about 17 percent and the margin excluding the credit fell 0.75 points.
  • Sensitivities for FY2027 (model). £1m of EBITDA is worth about 1.06p of EPS.
  • 1 point of UK price is about £5.8m of EBITDA, or 6.2p (6.6 percent of EPS).
  • 1 point of UK volume is about £2.9m after rosters flex (3.1p), or £4.3m before (4.6p).
  • 1 point of UK wage inflation is about £3.1m on practice staff (£3.7m across the group), or 3.3p to 3.9p.
  • The cone for FY2027 (model scenarios). EBITDA ranges from about £138m (18.6 percent) to about £160m (20.9 percent).
  • The downside assumes UK volume −3.5 percent on price of 3.5 percent, wages of 4 percent and an R&D credit of £10m.
  • The upside assumes volume −1.5 percent on price of 4.5 percent and a repeat R&D release.
  • The range is roughly symmetric. The near end is skewed slightly down because H1 FY2027 laps 2.7 percent LFL.

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7658P-0258
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?C6411th of 30full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

B+684th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Financial leverage will add about 3 points to FY2027 EPS growth and about 1.5 to 2.5 points a year thereafter, while bank leverage stays in a band of 1.45x to 1.85x.

  • FY2027: completing the buyback cuts average diluted shares by about 5 percent; net of the interest on the cash used, that is +3 percent.
  • Thereafter: the gain comes from acquisitions funded partly by debt at entry yields of 13 to 16 percent before tax, against an all-in bank cost of about 6 percent, and from modest renewed buybacks.

The balance sheet absorbs this comfortably.

  • Net bank debt rises from £199.6m to about £240m by June 2027, the peak at about 1.85x, as the £38m left of the buyback and £56m of acquisitions land in the same year.
  • It then plateaus at about £245m to £255m while EBITDA grows, so leverage falls to about 1.45x by FY2031. This happens because free cash flow on our basis rises from about £29m (FY2026) to £60m (FY2027) and £86m (FY2031).

The risk side changes in February 2028, when the £100m swap at 3.83 percent expires. From then on all of the roughly £240m to £250m of bank debt floats. A 1-point move in SONIA would then cost about £2.4m before tax, about 2.5 percent of EPS, against about £1.3m now.

Financial leverage cannot rescue a weak organic year. In our downside case leverage sits at about 2.0x for three years with no new buyback, the guardrail binds, and the board must choose between deals and returns.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Buyback arithmetic, FY2027 (derived).
  • Of the £50m programme, about £11.7m fell in FY2026 (FY2026 buyback cash of £31.7m less the £20m programme completed in January 2026), leaving about £38.3m in FY2027.
  • Shares go from 69.2m at June 2026 to 66.73m at late October and about 66.3m once the programme ends in late November.
  • FY2027 average diluted shares are about 67.3m against about 71.0m in FY2026: +5.5 percent to EPS.
  • The interest on about £50m of buyback cash at about 6 percent, after tax, costs about 2.6 percent of net income.
  • The net effect is about +3 percent, consistent with the first set's 3 to 4 percent.
  • Acquisition arithmetic, per year from FY2028 (model).
  • About £42m of initial cash plus a 20 percent deferral is about £53m of total consideration. At 6.6x to 7.5x that buys about £7.5m to £8m of EBITDA.
  • Less about £1.5m of depreciation and lease cost, about £3.2m of interest and tax at 29 percent leaves about £2.2m to £2.4m of net income, or about 3.3 to 3.6p (3 to 3.5 percent of EPS).
  • This is adjusted accretion. About £2m a year of each cohort's cost sits outside adjusted EPS as contingent consideration tied to employment (£10.5m a year in total in FY2026). Reported accretion is therefore about a third lower.
  • The facility and rates.
  • The facility is £350m (a £125m term loan and a £225m revolving facility) to May 2030, with an option to 2031. The margin is 1.25 to 2.50 percent over SONIA and averaged 1.6 percent in FY2026.
  • Gross drawn debt is £218m, of which £100m is swapped at 3.83 percent to February 2028.
  • Bank Rate is expected at 4.00 percent on 5 Nov 2026 and about 4.25 percent by mid-2027 (Office for Budget Responsibility, Geography Digest). The swap is therefore slightly valuable to CVS now, and its expiry is roughly neutral if SONIA is near 3.75 to 4.0 percent in 2028.
  • We carry an all-in cost of about 6 percent on bank debt, including fees, plus 4.3 percent on leases.
  • Net financial items (model). £15.6m in FY2026, then £18.1m, £19.5m, £19.7m, £20.2m and £20.4m.
  • FY2027 rises because the buyback and acquisitions are front-loaded into a higher-rate year.
  • Interest cover (EBITDA over net interest) stays at about 8x to 10x. The company's covenant measure is 13.1x.
  • Leases. Liabilities are £106.2m, or £128.7m undiscounted. They grow by about £3m a year with acquired sites. We assume lease principal payments of £14m to £18m a year and lease interest of about £4.6m to £5.0m inside net financial items.
  • All-in leverage including leases, on FactSet's basis, goes from about 2.2x now to about 2.4x at the FY2027 peak and about 1.9x by FY2031.
  • Headroom. The covenant is 3.25x and the guardrail is below 2.0x, with a stated willingness to exceed it temporarily for acquisitions. Deutsche Bank expects leverage to rise comfortably towards 2.0x. Our path stays below that because free cash flow recovers. The upside case falls below 1.0x by FY2031, which would invite a larger return of capital or a bigger deal.
  • Balance sheet shape. Equity grows through retained adjusted earnings less buybacks; consensus book value per share is £4.22 in FY2027. Goodwill and intangibles grow by about £45m to £55m a year with deals. There is no refinancing event before 2030.

Cost-out scope NTM

C-7750P-0346
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C4811th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. The cost base is reducible at the margin, not cuttable in size: perhaps 1 to 1.5 points of margin over three years from rostering, nurse substitution, front-office automation and procurement, without harming capacity. Large cuts would cut revenue, because clinical labour is the product. No cost programme is announced; a new chief executive could launch one.

Each part (shares of FY2026 revenue).

  • Clinical staff (34.3 percent): rostering and lower locum use already took 1.7 points; nurses doing dentals; a further 0.5 to 1 point plausible over three years; the Veterinary Surgeons Act reform could widen nurse roles later.
  • Other employment (about 18 percent, derived from 52.3 percent total): reception and administration are exposed to online booking (over 150,000 appointments), AI scribes and two-way messaging; a gradual reduction.
  • Non-clinical cost of sales (21.4 percent): procurement, white label, retendering; 0.2 to 0.5 points.
  • Central costs (about £21m): limited.
  • Capital expenditure: already cut from £36.4m to about £30m; further cuts would risk the estate.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

F3526th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Little net cost elimination will reach the line items in the base case: about £2m to £3m a year of productivity and procurement, which the April wage-floor rises absorb in full. No cost programme is announced, and the large rostering gain has already been banked. Clinical staff costs fell by about 1.7 points of revenue in FY2026, about £10m. H1 FY2027 now laps a 34.0 percent ratio, so the incremental gain roughly halves.

What does reach the accounts is mostly outside EBITDA or in cash:

  • capital spending cut from £36.4m to about £30m;
  • exceptional costs falling from £10.6m to about £4m;
  • Australian procurement synergies, which arrive inside each acquired practice's 25 percent margin rather than as a separate line.

The one plausible step-change is a review by the new chief executive in H2 FY2027 to FY2028. It could target £8m to £12m (1 to 1.5 points of margin) across the support office, procurement, Animed and pricing architecture, at an exceptional cost of £4m to £8m and with 12 to 24 months to deliver. That would add about 6 to 9 percent to EPS by FY2029. It is a scenario, not the base. We carry it as upside and note that a new chief executive is equally likely to reinvest any saving in pay and client initiatives.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • What is cuttable, by line (shares of FY2026 revenue).
  • Clinical staff (34.3 percent in H2). Rostering and lower locum use took about 1.7 points. Further gains are 0.3 to 0.6 percent of staff cost a year from nurses doing dental work, AI scribes and lower vacancies reducing locum cover. This line is the product, and cutting it cuts revenue.
    • An IVC Evidensia area director (18 Mar 2026) says nurses are scarce and hard to retain, which limits substitution.
    • Reform of the Veterinary Surgeons Act would widen nurse roles, but it is years away.
  • Other employment (about 18 percent). Reception and administration are exposed to online booking (over 150,000 appointments, 15 percent out of hours) and messaging. The savings are real but small and gradual. Experts call software returns "grey and hard to measure".
  • Non-clinical cost of sales (21.4 percent, from 21.6 percent). Retendering, white label and the single Australian wholesaler can save 0.1 to 0.2 points a year. Against this, manufacturers are raising prices and cutting rebates (Zoetis named), and the company relies on two or three supply contracts.
  • Central costs (£9.9m, +13.8 percent). These are rising, not falling, during the succession.
  • Capital spending. It is already cut to about £30m (4 percent of revenue). This is a cash saving of about £6m a year, not an EBITDA saving. Cutting further risks the estate; the company's own risk register warns of under-investment.
  • What we put in the model. Productivity of 0.6 percent of UK practice staff cost in FY2027 (about £1.8m), 0.5 percent in FY2028 and 0.3 percent a year thereafter, plus 0.1 point of procurement. Against this sit wage inflation of 3.6, 3.7, 3.5, 3.4 and 3.3 percent and drug and other cost inflation of 3 percent.
  • Productivity offsets about one-sixth of wage inflation in FY2027 and about one-tenth later.
  • The net effect is visible in the flat UK margin excluding the R&D credit (P-01).
  • The precedent for a programme. Pets at Home announced a £20m saving in support-office overheads in November 2025 against £48m of cumulative regulatory wage headwinds. It cost £6m to £8m in non-underlying charges and was due for completion by the end of FY2026, with the full benefit in FY2027: about 12 to 18 months.
  • Spire Healthcare offset the 2025 employer cost step through self-help.
  • CVS has no such programme. A new chief executive with operating experience is the most likely sponsor, and the timing would be FY2028 benefit at the earliest.
  • Timing of what does reach the line items.
  • FY2027: rostering carry-over and procurement about £2m; exceptional costs down £6.6m (outside adjusted); capital spending down about £6m (cash).
  • FY2028 onwards: about £1.5m to £2m a year inside EBITDA. Acquired Australian practices bring immediate procurement and laboratory synergies inside each deal; vet wages and rent do not fall (Jefferies, December 2023).
  • The R&D credit is not cost elimination, but it moves the same line. It falls by £3.2m in FY2027 in our base. That is larger than the year's net efficiency gain.
5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4750P-0553
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?C5612th of 30full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

D5016th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Two loops will dominate the line items, and they work at different levels of the income statement.

The dominant loop is a reinforcing capital-allocation loop. It is strengthening, and it shapes revenue, EBITDA, the share count and EPS:

  • recovering free cash flow and leverage headroom fund about £50m a year of acquisitions at 6x to 7.5x EBITDA, plus buybacks;
  • each year's deals add about £7.5m to £8m of EBITDA;
  • that raises free cash flow and bank-test EBITDA, which funds the next round.

In our base it supplies about 70 percent of EBITDA growth from FY2026 to FY2031 (about £40m of £57m). It lifts Australia from about 13 percent of EBITDA before central costs to about 26 percent, and from 11 to 22 percent of revenue.

The second loop is a balancing UK price-and-volume loop. It is active and moderate, and it shapes UK LFL and margin:

  • price rises lead to deferred routine care and subscription churn;
  • volume falls about 2 to 3 percent;
  • with fixed staff, the pressure to raise price again builds;
  • published prices and the CMA's disclosure rules make the loop more elastic.

It caps UK practice LFL at about 1.5 to 2.5 percent until FY2029. It is why the UK margin excluding the R&D credit stays at about 18 percent.

Three slower loops modify these two:

  • Demographic (positive, reinforcing from FY2029). The pandemic cohort reaches higher-need ages, which lifts diagnostics and chronic care, laboratory volumes and average transaction value. It is the main reason our UK volume path goes from −2.5 percent to flat by FY2030.
  • Staffing (positive, maturing). Lower vacancies mean fewer locums and better continuity. Its cost benefit was largely banked in FY2026; what remains is retention and volume.
  • Valuation (reinforcing for EPS while the shares stay cheap). A low multiple makes buybacks accretive and keeps the activist engaged. It weakens automatically if the shares re-rate.

The loops to watch for reversal are three:

  • the capital loop through rising Australian multiples, ACCC scrutiny or founder departures;
  • the staffing loop through clinician departures around the leadership change;
  • the price-volume loop tipping into a vicious circle if Healthy Pet Club membership falls faster than about 2 percent a year.

Reasoning and evidence, loop by loop.

  • The capital-allocation loop (reinforcing; strength high; operating now).
  • Australia has gone from entry in July 2023 to 61 sites by September 2026.
  • FY2026: six practices with 14 sites for £43.3m to £45.4m at about 6x (company, Deutsche Bank). Post year-end, two deals completed for £4.9m and two were signed for £4.0m.
  • Acquired practices run at 25 percent-plus margins, ahead of their business cases. The single-wholesaler, laboratory and crematoria synergies are immediate.
  • The loop feeds itself through cash. Our-basis free cash flow goes from about £29m to £60m to £86m, so the same leverage supports more deals each year.
  • What strengthens it: a reopened UK pipeline (practice valuations down from about 3.5x to 2.5x revenue; compliance burdens push independents to sell).
  • What weakens it:
    • private-equity bidding (VetPartners Australia was taken at about 10.7x by EQT; a former CVS director quotes 9x to 10x for premium practices);
    • Coles abandoning a A$4bn Greencross deal in July 2026 on "pet market peaking" feedback, which may cool platform prices but not small-practice prices;
    • the ACCC's Phase 2 review of a Vets Central deal (August 2026);
    • the leverage guardrail.
  • How we carry it: multiples drift from 6.2x (FY2027) to 7.5x (FY2031), and margins on new Australian deals are 24 percent.
  • The UK price-and-volume loop (balancing; strength moderate; operating now).
  • The evidence: members fell from 519,000 to 508,000; UK practice LFL was about 1 percent on about 4 to 5 percent price; an expert says owners decline preventive care "because price increases have outpaced wage growth"; wellness laboratory tests are falling; US visits fell 3 to 4 percent (a chief medical officer, 26 Oct 2026).
  • What counters it:
    • subscriptions: Pets at Home has more than 50 percent of vet clients on care plans, and plans drove 9 percent of its vet revenue growth; CVS's Advanced tier is new;
    • inelastic reactive and referral care.
  • What would turn it vicious:
    • members falling faster than 2 percent while revenue per member rises more than 5 percent;
    • a visible switch to independents after branding.
  • This loop sets our UK price and volume inputs (P-04).
  • The demographic loop (reinforcing; strength low now, rising).
  • UK dogs rose 13.4 percent from 2021 to 2023 (Kelso, February 2026), and about 2m lockdown pets are four to six years old.
  • Spend rises from about age five (Pets at Home), and about 60 percent of senior pets have active disease at examination (Jefferies, 30 Sep 2026).
  • Laboratory volumes rose 4.3 percent in FY2026. The loop is visible there before it shows in practice volumes.
  • We give it UK volume of −0.7 percent in FY2029 and −0.2 percent in FY2030, versus −2.5 percent in FY2027.
  • The staffing loop (reinforcing; maturing).
  • The vacancy rate went 9.3 percent (FY2024), 6.5 percent (FY2025), 5.8 percent (FY2026). Locum availability is up and EU vets are returning. The Royal College's modelling shows no medium-term shortage.
  • The cost gain shows in the clinical staff ratio and is mostly banked.
  • What would reverse it: clinician exits around the change of chief executive, and the corporate model's weaker retention against joint ventures (clinician turnover at Pets at Home is 27 percent).
  • The transparency loop (balancing against CVS; slow).
  • Independents win share on price at the margin (the "three-speed market", according to Dr Cees Bennett).
  • Counter: some independents converge upwards.
  • We fold this into UK volume rather than model it separately.
  • The valuation loop (reinforcing for EPS).
  • At 12.4x 24-month-forward earnings the buyback buys an earnings yield of about 8 percent with debt costing about 4.3 percent after tax.
  • Converium's letter (13 May 2026) brought a £50m programme in 13 days. EV/EBITDA is about 7x against private transactions at 9x to 14x.
  • The loop justifies our renewed buybacks from FY2028 (£10m to £25m a year). It also implies corporate-action optionality that we do not model.
  • The net effect on the line items (model, FY2026 to FY2031).
  • Revenue +£264m: acquired about 62 percent, organic about 38 percent.
  • EBITDA +£57m:
    • Australian rollover, deals and organic growth +£34m;
    • UK deals +£9m;
    • UK organic, excluding the R&D credit, +£12m;
    • the laboratories and Animed +£7m;
    • central costs −£2.6m;
    • the R&D credit −£3.2m.
  • EPS: the capital loop plus the valuation loop supply about 60 percent of growth.

C-68

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

D4517th of 30

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement. For FY2027 and FY2028 the company gives no numeric guidance of its own; it points at consensus and says it is comfortable with it, so near-term guidance equals consensus (FY2027 adjusted EBITDA of about £149m to £152m). Its medium-term framework sits above consensus on organic growth (4 to 8 percent LFL against the roughly 2.5 to 3 percent that consensus revenue implies) and at consensus on margin (the bottom of 19 to 23 percent against 20.1 percent). The gap exists because management anchors on its pre-2023 history and on the ageing-pet narrative, while analysts have discounted three years of misses; the market prices consensus, not the framework.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Statements at the FY2026 results (24 Sep 2026): trading in line with market expectations, positive early LFL growth; comfort with consensus (Jefferies: FY2027 EBITDA £149.0m to £151.7m, average £150.4m; EPS about 94.9p); the framework reaffirmed.
  • Consensus now (FactSet, 25 Oct 2026): FY2027 revenue £751m, EBITDA £151m, EPS 95.0p; FY2028 £787m, £158m, 104p.
  • Other quantified guidance: capital expenditure about £30m; acquisitions about £50m a year; cash conversion above 70 percent; leverage below 2.0x.
C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

D4721st of 30

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement. Near-term guidance (consensus) is moderately prudent; medium-term guidance is not conservative. FY2027 consensus needs about 2.5 to 3 percent LFL plus acquired EBITDA and the buyback, against a two-year trend that is improving and change forces that are less adverse than in FY2024 to FY2026 (no regulatory tail, labour availability improved, easier comparatives, larger price rises), so it is achievable with modest room. The 4 to 8 percent LFL range would need UK volume to turn positive; against three years at 2.9, 0.2 and 2.1 percent and a 2027 consumer squeeze, the bottom of the range is plausible by FY2028 to FY2029, not in FY2027.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The past five years: LFL about 7 to 10 percent in FY2021 to FY2023 (pandemic demand); 2.9, 0.2, 2.1 percent in FY2024 to FY2026.
  • Change forces better than the past: the CMA concluded; vacancy 5.8 percent; summer 2026 price rises; H2 FY2027 comparative about 1.5 percent; the pet cohort approaching higher-need ages.
  • Change forces worse than the past: the 2027 UK squeeze, transparency, the online medicine shift, annual wage-floor rises, a cut to capital expenditure.
  • The margin target: 19 to 23 percent; delivered at 19.7 to 20.0 percent with about 2 points of R&D credit inside; the bottom of the range is the honest guide.

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?
