Group judgement

Risks

B+644th of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecasts for the next 18 months are predictable in their level and unpredictable in their presentation. Everything that matters for FY2027 EPS is either transacted (the buyback, the FY2026 and post-year-end deals), contractual (the facility, the swap, the April wage step) or already decided by the company's own accounting (the R&D method), and the two sets' answers that fed the model are coherent and lean the same way. That gives a cone for FY2027 adjusted EPS of about 82p to 104p around 93p, a 60 percent chance of landing within 5 percent of the base, and a driver set concentrated in four monitorable lines. Against that, the three prints before April 2027 will show group LFL of about 1.5 percent and a flat-to-down reported margin, because of the comparative and the R&D release, while the EPS arithmetic shows up only at the full year; the company's disclosure does not let the organic story be read before March; the stated medium-term framework cannot be traced to EPS; and the share price, which moves 87 percent on its own news, will be priced off those prints and off the appointment rather than off the forecastable earnings. Two stress lines sit outside the cone and must be shown separately: the R&D credit (about 13p of EPS; HMRC windows open on £38.3m) and a volume shock from clinician departures around the leadership change. For the Consumer: present FY2027 and FY2028 EPS with confidence and modest downside against consensus; present the operating metrics as likely to disappoint the headline through March; keep the capital-allocation levers as the source of the growth; and separate the predictable earnings leg of the return from the multiple leg, which depends on events this set cannot forecast.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

B+802nd of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The expected change in the financials, adjusted EPS growth of about 9 to 11 percent a year after three years of decline, is highly plausible on strengths the business has demonstrated, because it does not need the strength it lacks. Of FY2027's 7.7p of EPS growth on our base, about 4.7p is the share count (buyback executed), about 2.3p is acquired EBITDA after interest (deals done or signed, integration ahead of plan), and the organic UK practices contribute about nothing net of the R&D step-down. Each of those is a capability CVS has shown in the last 18 months: it executed two buybacks, a refinancing, a listing move, 61 Australian sites at about 6x with practices ahead of their business cases, a UK restart, CMA compliance ahead of deadline, and a 1.7-point reduction in clinical staff costs through rostering. What CVS has not demonstrated since FY2023 is organic volume growth, clinician retention comparable to the joint-venture model, and real pricing power (price above cost inflation without volume loss). None of those is required for FY2027 or FY2028 consensus. They are required for the medium-term framework (4 to 8 percent LFL), for the FY2028 doubling ambition, and, most importantly for the investor, for the re-rating. So the thesis splits cleanly: the earnings thesis rests on demonstrated strengths; the valuation thesis rests on a strength that would have to be acquired. The precedents say that split is common and that it ends one of two ways: an organic re-acceleration or a bid.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The composition of the expected change (model, derived). FY2027 adjusted EBITDA +£7.3m: Australia +£6.9m (rollover +£3.3m, new deals +£3.1m, organic +£0.5m), UK deal +£1.3m, Laboratories +£1.1m, Animed +£0.5m, UK practices organic +£1.4m, central costs −£0.6m, R&D credit −£3.2m. Adjusted profit before tax +£3.0m, worth about 3.0p at the old share count; the share count (71.0m to 67.3m average) adds about 4.7p. The part of the growth that depends on UK volume is the +£1.4m, which is 1.5p of EPS, and it is itself a price effect (4 percent price on −2.5 percent volume).
  • FY2028 shifts the weight but not the kind. EBITDA +£11.3m (acquisitions and rollover about £7.5m to £8m, Laboratories and Animed about £1.4m, UK organic about £2.5m, central −£0.5m); the share count adds only about 1 percent. The organic UK contribution rises to about a fifth of EBITDA growth and still assumes falling volume. Consensus FY2028 (104p) needs about 3 percent organic growth and a held margin; it does not need volume to turn.
  • Demonstrated strengths, with the evidence. Acquisition sourcing and integration: Australia from entry in July 2023 to 35 practices on 57 sites by June 2026 and 61 by September, six deals for £45.4m in FY2026 at about 6x, practices ahead of plan, single-wholesaler synergies immediate (OWN-2); the first UK deal since the pause at a multiple below the group's. Capital returns: £20m completed January 2026, £50m launched May 2026 and about three-quarters executed by late October (voting rights 66.73m). Cost control: clinical staff 36.0 to 34.3 percent of revenue; non-clinical cost of sales 21.6 to 21.4 percent; capital expenditure guided from £36.4m to about £30m. Laboratory integration: revenue +11.5 percent, EBITDA +25.6 percent. Each of these is in the FY2026 accounts, not in a plan.
  • Strengths that would need to be acquired, with the evidence of absence. Organic volume: LFL 2.9, 0.2 and 2.1 percent against 4 to 8 percent; Healthy Pet Club members 519,000 to 508,000; UK practice volume about −3 to −4 percent in FY2026 (derived); US clinic visits −2.2 percent in Q3 2026 (Vetsource via Morgan Stanley) and Piper Sandler's UK visit forecast of −1 to −2 percent for 2026 and flat to −1 percent for 2027. Clinician retention: Pets at Home's joint-venture turnover 27 percent against a corporate model experts describe as losing culture and talent; CVS does not disclose turnover. Pricing power above cost inflation: the margin excluding the credit fell 0.75 points over three years of 3 to 5 percent price rises.
  • Has CVS ever expanded its margin excluding the credit? Yes, in FY2020 to FY2023 on 7 to 10 percent LFL (FY2023 operating EBITDA margin 21.2 percent on FactSet's basis), and the second set shows that the H1 FY2020 jump was mostly IFRS 16. Since FY2023 it has not. The capability exists when volume grows; it is dormant, not absent.
  • How often roll-ups in this position have delivered. The record (second set, P-06; the t-d1 report) is that forecasts resting on acquired growth plus buybacks have been met within about ±3 percent once consensus had been cut, while forecasts resting on organic recovery above about 3 percent have been missed for three years. The peer precedents point the same way: Medicover delivered margin expansion through inflation because it had 11 percent organic growth to lever (22 Oct 2026); Monash IVF's profit fell 41 percent when volumes turned down on a fixed base (Jefferies, 31 Aug 2026); AddLife's return on capital fell below 8 percent on acquisition-led growth (RedEye, 27 Oct 2026); CVS's own return on invested capital fell from 13.2 percent (FY2017) to 9.1 percent (FY2020) in its last acquisition-led phase (Jefferies). The pattern is that acquisition-led EPS growth is deliverable and is not rewarded with a multiple until organic growth re-appears; the 2019 to 2021 re-rating of CVS came with high-single-digit LFL, and the 2018 de-rating came with a wage spike on a fixed cost base.
  • Where the demonstrated strengths could fail. Australian entry multiples (company 6x; a former director 9x to 10x for premium assets; a Vets Central CFO 5x to 8x and rising since CVS's entry; VetPartners Australia taken at about 10.7x) and ACCC scrutiny of clustered deals (the Vets Central Phase 2 referral, 18 Aug 2026; 410 of 486 waivers granted in nine months, so clean deals pass). Founder departures after acquisition (the prospectus names it). A new chief executive who prefers reinvestment to buybacks (Jefferies' preference). Each would slow the mechanical growth rather than reverse it; none is in evidence yet.
  • The weakest link is not a strength but a crutch. The one element of past delivery that will not repeat is the rising R&D credit (£9.6m, £12.8m, £15.1m, £15.7m); it supplied the FY2025 beat and held the FY2026 margin. Removing it from "demonstrated delivery" is what takes FY2027 from "95p with upside" to "93p at the bottom of the comfort range".

Implication for predictability. High for the level of FY2027 and FY2028 EPS, because the inputs that matter are transacted or contractual; low for the trajectory of the operating metrics the market reads first, because those depend on the undemonstrated strength. The forecasts have been built so that the demonstrated part does the work, which is the right construction for an 18-month horizon.

D-03In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?A741st of 30full answer under 2a Internal risks
D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

B+654th of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The cone for the financials is narrow for a UK consumer-facing mid-cap and about as wide as for a good-quality healthcare-services operator: FY2027 adjusted EPS of roughly 82p to 104p around a base of 93p (−12 to +12 percent), with about 60 percent of the probability inside 90p to 97p, and FY2028 of roughly 81p to 121p around 102p. FY2027 adjusted EBITDA runs about £138m to £160m around £149m. The cone is tilted down for the next six to nine months and roughly symmetric over 18 months. The near-term tilt comes from three things that are facts rather than forecasts: H1 FY2027 laps the hardest LFL comparative (2.7 percent); the halves of FY2026 carried an R&D credit that will not repeat in full, so reported margins will look flat to down through March 2027 and down in H2 FY2027 even if the business does what we expect; and the company's record is to land within about 3 percent of a consensus that has already been cut, missing more often in first halves (H1 FY2026 −2.7 percent) than beating. The symmetry over 18 months comes from the easy H2 FY2027 comparative, the arithmetic of acquisitions annualising, and the upside options (buyback renewal, a repeat provision release, a credible chief executive) that are as plausible as the downside options (a further volume step down, a target reset accompanied by a kitchen-sink). Two tails sit outside the cone and should be carried as explicit stress lines rather than inside the range: a loss or clawback of the R&D credit (about 13p of EPS, low probability within 18 months) and a volume shock from clinician departures around the leadership change (medium magnitude, low probability). So the case is predictable in its earnings and unpredictable in its operating optics and its multiple; for the memo's purposes, the probability that FY2027 EPS lands within 5 percent of 93p is about 60 percent, which is high for this sector.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The sensitivities that set the width (second set, model; FY2027). £1m of EBITDA is about 1.06p of EPS. One point of UK price: about £5.8m and 6.2p. One point of UK volume: about £2.9m and 3.1p once rosters flex (£4.3m and 4.6p before). One point of UK wage inflation: £3.1m to £3.7m and 3.3p to 3.9p. The R&D credit: each £1m is 1.06p; a repeat of the £6.6m release is worth about 7p; total loss about 13p. Each 1 point of tax: 1.3p. Each £10m of buyback: about 0.75m shares and about 1p once annualised. Each 1 point of SONIA: about 0.9p now, about 1.8p after February 2028. Each £10m of acquisitions at 6.5x: about 0.5p in the first year, 1p annualised.
  • The plausible combinations. Downside (about 82p): UK volume −3.5 percent on 3.5 percent price, wages 4 percent, an R&D credit of £10m, deals at 6.5x to 8x and £25m a year, no new buyback. Upside (about 104p): volume −1.5 percent on 4.5 percent price, the release repeats, deals at 6x to 7x, £30m a year of buybacks. Neither requires anything outside recent experience: FY2025 produced a volume outcome near the downside; FY2026 produced a credit outcome at the upside.
  • A rough probability distribution for FY2027 adjusted EPS (our judgement). Below 88p: 15 percent. 88p to 92p: 25 percent. 92p to 96p: 40 percent. 96p to 100p: 15 percent. Above 100p: 5 percent. The mean is about 93p, the mode 93p to 94p, and the probability of landing below the 95p consensus is about 60 percent. For FY2028 adjusted EPS: below 95p 25 percent; 95p to 108p 50 percent; above 108p 25 percent; mean about 102p against 104p consensus.
  • What history says about dispersion. Against consensus at the start of each year, CVS's EBITDA landed −2.2 percent (FY2024, cyber), +2.7 percent (FY2025, with the credit up £2.3m), and in line (FY2026), with H1 FY2026 −2.7 percent; consensus EPS fell 15 percent over FY2026's forecast life and 11 percent over FY2027's, almost all of it in 2024 and 2025 when the shocks arrived. The 2018 wage spike produced a fall in H1 FY2019 EBITDA on revenue growth of 23.7 percent; the cyber attack cost about £5m of EBITDA. So the dispersion is about ±3 percent once the year's shocks are known, and 10 to 15 percent when a shock arrives during the year. Over the last six months, FY2027 EPS consensus has moved within a 1 percent band, which is the sign of a year whose shocks are, so far, known.
  • Why the near-term tilt is down (derived). H1 FY2026 EBITDA was £67.7m on a 19.0 percent margin including about £7.1m of R&D credit (17.0 percent excluding it); H2 FY2026 was £73.8m on 20.7 percent including about £8.6m (18.3 percent excluding it). Our H1 FY2027 is £72.0m on 19.1 percent with about £6.25m of credit, which needs ex-credit EBITDA growth of about 8.5 percent on revenue growth of 5.7 percent; the Australian rollover supplies most of that, but any slippage in UK practice LFL below about 1.5 percent in the four months to October takes the H1 margin below 19.0 percent. Our H2 FY2027 is £76.8m on 20.2 percent against 20.7 percent. The market will see group LFL of about 1.5 percent in November and January and a flat-to-down H1 margin in March; the acquisitions and the share count show up only in the full-year EPS. That is the shape of a year in which the first three data points disappoint the headline and the fourth lands the number.
  • Why 18 months is symmetric. H2 FY2027 laps about 1.5 percent LFL and a heat-hit fourth quarter (an easy comparative); the FY2026 and FY2027 deals annualise; the April 2027 wage step is known and modest (3.9 percent); the R&D step-down is in our base; and the two visible upside options (a renewed buyback and a credible chief executive plan) are at least as likely as the two visible downside options (a target reset with a kitchen-sink of exceptional costs, which hits reported not adjusted earnings, and a further point of volume loss as published prices bite). Consensus FY2028 is attainable with 3 percent organic growth and a held margin.
  • The tails outside the cone. The R&D credit: HMRC enquiry windows are open on claims of up to £38.3m; the auditor made the valuation a key audit matter; CVS's unsubmitted-claim discount (about 7.6 percent on the FY2026 claim) is far below VetPartners' 50 percent; peers in UK clinical sectors report rising HMRC scrutiny (Compass Pathways, Autolus; OWN-1). A disallowance would be a cash and reported item first and an adjusted-EBITDA item only if the policy changed; we put the probability of a material adjusted-EBITDA hit inside 18 months at about 10 percent, and of a cash clawback at about 15 percent. A clinician exodus around the leadership change: medium magnitude for UK volume, low probability, not visible until the interims. A second cyber incident: low probability, about £5m of EBITDA. None of these is in the ±12 percent cone, and the memo should show them as separate lines.
  • What would narrow the cone, and when. The four-month trading update (late November 2026) narrows the FY2027 EBITDA range by about a third if it gives LFL and confirms "in line"; the interims (4 Mar 2027) narrow it to about ±4 percent by giving the H1 margin, the H1 R&D credit, membership including the Advanced tier and the clinical staff ratio; the chief executive's appointment removes the target-reset uncertainty from the narrative but not from the numbers until the first strategy update.
  • Peers for scale. The t-d2 report puts the 18-month range at −10 to −15 percent below consensus in its downside and +15 to +20 percent EPS growth in its upside, and calls it negatively skewed; Terveystalo's 2027 to 2028 estimates were cut 13 percent in a single October 2026 note on volume weakness, Spire's shares fell about 25 percent on a trimmed outlook in March 2025, and Medicover's were raised 12 percent on a divestment. CVS's cone is narrower than Terveystalo's or Spire's because so much of its growth is transacted, and its skew is milder because its shocks of 2024 to 2026 are in the base.

Implication for predictability. Good for the number (FY2027 EPS within 5 percent of 93p with about 60 percent probability), poor for the path (three soft-looking prints before the number lands), and dependent on two stress lines the memo must show separately. The predictability is higher than the dashboard's Case Riskiness D implies for the earnings and about as low as D implies for the share price.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

D4521st of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. Moderately helpful: CVS discloses more operating detail than most UK consolidators, but on a semi-annual rhythm, with the two most important splits missing (price against volume, and acquired against organic revenue) and with the decisive item for FY2027 (the R&D credit recognised) visible only at the interims on 4 Mar 2027. Over the next six months the Consumer gets three company data points (the four-month trading update in late November 2026, the half-year trading update in January 2027 and the interims in March) and must fill the gaps with external monitors (Pets at Home's H1 results in late November and its Q3 update in January, the quarterly Morgan Stanley survey, IDEXX and Zoetis in early February, the ACCC's merger register, Molecule Group's trading, Converium's filings, and the chief executive announcement). The trading updates will report group LFL, which understates the practice trend as Animed and the Laboratories normalise, so the headline is the wrong number to anchor on. The interims are the first point at which the four lines that carry the forecast (clinical staff ratio, R&D credit recognised, Healthy Pet Club members including the Advanced tier, Australian sites and consideration) can all be checked. Disclosure is therefore good enough to confirm or refute the FY2027 base by March, and not good enough to do so before then.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • What each company event will show, and what it will not.
  • Four-month trading update with the AGM, late November 2026 (last year 18 Nov 2025: total sales +5.7 percent, adjusted EBITDA +6.2 percent, LFL, acquisitions, "in line"). Expect: revenue growth (about 5.5 to 6 percent on our base), group LFL (about 1.5 percent), an "in line with expectations" statement, deals completed, buyback completion (about 66.2m to 66.3m shares) and a word on the search. Will not show: practice LFL separately (the January update last year did give the practice trend through Deutsche Bank's reading, so a split is possible but not assured), price against volume, the margin, the R&D credit, Advanced tier numbers.
  • Half-year trading update, January 2027 (last year 23 Jan 2026: revenue +5.8 percent, LFL +2.7 percent, EBITDA £67.7m, "in line"). Expect: revenue, LFL, an H1 EBITDA figure or range, acquisitions. This is the first point at which an H1 EBITDA number appears, and the first reading of the R&D credit's effect on the margin, though not itemised.
  • Interims, 4 Mar 2027. Segment revenue and EBITDA, UK against Australia practice counts and sites, Healthy Pet Club members and revenue (and, probably, Advanced tier sign-ups), clinical staff and non-clinical cost of sales as a percentage of revenue, the R&D credit recognised in H1 (in the notes), exceptional costs, business combination costs, cash conversion, bank leverage, the buyback outcome and a statement on capital allocation. This is the first full check on the base case. It is also likely to coincide with or follow the chief executive announcement (the OWN-4 precedents put a listed peer's search at three to six months; the sub-committee formed on 22 Jul 2026).
  • What the company discloses well. LFL by half; Healthy Pet Club members and revenue; clinical and non-clinical cost ratios; segment revenue and EBITDA; Australian sites, consideration and the average multiple; cash conversion; bank-test leverage; the R&D credit's gross, net, in-year and prior-year composition and the open claims (in the annual report, in unusual detail); contingent consideration and its anniversary schedule; the swap's size, rate and expiry.
  • What it does not disclose, and why each gap matters. Price against volume (the single most important split for the UK thesis; we derive it from LFL and membership); acquired against organic revenue in the trading updates (so the organic trend must be inferred from LFL); Australian LFL except annually (disclosed at +3.0 percent for FY2026); the expected R&D credit (so the biggest swing in FY2027 EBITDA is unguided); clinician turnover and locum use (so the staffing loop can only be read through the cost ratio); the Advanced tier's churn and cannibalisation (new; first data at the interims); and any EBITDA or EPS guidance, so "in line" is the only calibration and consensus is the yardstick.
  • The headline that misleads. Group LFL of 2.1 percent in FY2026 contained about 1.1 points from Animed and the Laboratories (second set, finding 8). If the practices improve from 1.0 to about 1.4 percent while the small segments normalise, group LFL prints at about 1.5 percent in the first half. A Consumer who reads the November headline against the FY2026 figure of 2.1 percent would conclude the business is slowing when it is not. The right comparison is practice LFL against 1.0 percent, if given, or group LFL against the two-year stack.
  • External monitors that fill the gaps, with dates. Pets at Home's H1 FY2027 results (late November 2026) for UK vet volumes and care-plan take-up, and its Q3 update (late January 2027); Morgan Stanley's quarterly "Animal Tracks" survey (next due about January 2027) for visit trends and price sensitivity; IDEXX (early February 2027) and Zoetis (mid-February) for US clinic visits and diagnostics utilisation; the Bank of England decision (5 Nov 2026) and the Office for Budget Responsibility for the consumer backdrop; the ACCC's public register for any CVS filing or waiver; Molecule Group's trading updates for online medicine migration; Converium's and Kelso's filings for capital-return pressure; the FTSE 250 quarterly reviews for flows; and the RNS feed for the appointment, which is the one disclosure that cannot be scheduled.
  • How peers compare. Pets at Home reports quarterly with vet revenue, care-plan members and clinician headcount; Medicover reports quarterly with organic growth by division; Spire reported half-yearly with a mid-year trading update. CVS's cadence is at the low end of its listed peers and its content at the high end.
  • What the Consumer should write down now as the test for each event. November: group LFL below about 1.5 percent, or any wording weaker than "in line", moves FY2027 EPS toward 90p; above 3 percent moves it toward 96p. January: H1 EBITDA below about £70m is a miss against our £72m and probably against consensus; the market will read the margin. March: a clinical staff ratio above 34.5 percent, an H1 R&D credit above £7m (a repeat release) or below £5.5m, members below 500,000, Advanced tier sign-ups below about 25,000, and Australian consideration since July below about £15m are each a signal that an input is wrong.

Implication for predictability. The case can be tracked, but not before March with any precision, and not through the headline. The next six months are a period in which the Consumer will know less than the model implies and the market will read numbers that understate the base.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

C509th of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The capital side traces clearly and the operating side does not. What the company says it will achieve on capital (about £50m a year of acquisitions at about 6x in Australia, about £30m of capital expenditure, leverage below 2.0x with temporary excursions, cash conversion above 70 percent, the £50m buyback) maps onto identifiable lines with known arithmetic: each £50m at 6x adds about £8m of EBITDA and about 3 to 4 percent to EPS once annualised; the buyback adds about 3 percent to FY2027 EPS net of interest. What the company says it will achieve on operations (4 to 8 percent LFL, a 19 to 23 percent margin, "pet health in your pocket", Healthy Pet Club Advanced, online booking, the CVS Vets brand, nurse roles) has no quantified building blocks: no price-volume split, no target for members or visits, no expected contribution from the initiatives, no outlook for the R&D credit that sits inside the margin target. So the stated medium-term framework cannot be traced to EPS and should be treated as a direction, not a forecast; our model gives the initiatives about half a point of LFL inside the volume path for want of anything better. The external transmission is clearer than the internal one, because each value-chain force has a quantified channel: the household squeeze reaches routine visits and members (about 3p of EPS per point of volume); wage legislation reaches the staff ratio (about 3.5p per point; the April 2027 step is 3.9 percent); the CMA order reaches medicines and elective work through published prices (inside our volume path at 3 to 4 percent of in-clinic medicine revenue a year); Australian multiples and the ACCC reach acquired accretion (about 0.5p per turn of multiple on £50m); rates reach interest (about 0.9p per point now, 1.8p after February 2028). The weak link in both chains is the R&D credit, a £12m to £16m item that the company neither guides nor explains in its margin target and that converts one-for-one into adjusted EBITDA.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The internal chain, stated and traceable. Acquisitions: about £50m a year, both countries, "dynamically" (company); Australia at about 6x with 25 percent-plus margins (company, Deutsche Bank); the first UK deal at below the group's multiple; 80 percent paid up front, 20 percent deferred. Each tranche traces to revenue (about £30m to £40m a year), EBITDA (about £7.5m to £8m), D&A and lease cost (about £1.5m), interest (about £3m at 6 percent) and about 3.3p to 3.6p of EPS annualised (second set). Buybacks: £50m programme, voting rights 66.73m at late October, about 66.3m at completion; FY2027 average 67.3m against 71.0m; about +5.5 percent gross and +3 percent net of interest to EPS. Capital expenditure about £30m, leverage below 2.0x and cash conversion above 70 percent trace to free cash flow and net debt without ambiguity.
  • The internal chain, stated and not traceable. The 4 to 8 percent LFL range has been missed for three years and has no stated building blocks; the 19 to 23 percent margin has been delivered at its bottom with about 2 points of R&D credit inside it; the ambition to double adjusted EBITDA by FY2028 (from about £107m on the company's FY2022 basis or £95m on FactSet's) would need about £190m to £215m against a consensus of £158m, and nobody treats it as live. The initiatives are described but not sized: Healthy Pet Club Advanced (about 14,500 clients in three months, trials showed "better volume and EBITDA", no figures); over 150,000 online appointments; online sign-up with 50 percent out of hours; joint branding on over 80 percent of practices; nurses doing dental work; an AI scribe trial. The company gives no expected visit, member or revenue effect for any of them. The route from these to EPS runs through UK practice volume, and the company's own disclosure does not let anyone, including the company in public, measure it.
  • The lines a Consumer can watch, and the measure for each. UK practice LFL (half-yearly; the trading updates give group LFL); Healthy Pet Club members and revenue per member (half-yearly); clinical staff as a percentage of revenue (half-yearly; 34.3 percent in H2 FY2026); non-clinical cost of sales (21.4 percent); Australian sites, consideration and multiple (each update); the R&D credit recognised (half-yearly, in the notes); bank-test leverage and cash conversion (half-yearly); the share count (monthly, through the buyback announcements and the total voting rights notice).
  • From EBITDA to EPS: where clarity is lost. Four steps between adjusted EBITDA and adjusted EPS are opaque to an outsider. The R&D credit sits inside EBITDA without a forecast. Contingent consideration tied to employment (£10.5m in FY2026) and deal fees (£4.3m) sit outside adjusted earnings but are recurring costs of the model, so adjusted EPS overstates the economic earnings by roughly a third of the acquisition accretion (second set). The adjusted tax rate the company states (27.4 percent) does not reconcile to adjusted EPS without minorities (about £0.8m, derived), which the company does not break out. And the share count that brokers use is not uniform (consensus net income over EPS implies about 68.5m for FY2027 against 67.3m), so two brokers with the same profit forecast can differ by 2 percent on EPS. The Financial Analyst's model summary adds its own basis breaks (FY2026 EPS 0.56, "+68.7 percent" growth, the EBITA step) that the memo must not quote (Reader's key).
  • The external chain, force by force, with the channel and the sensitivity.
  • UK household income and confidence reach routine and preventive visits, elective procedures and membership, with a lag of one to two quarters and no monthly reading. The Office for Budget Responsibility's 2027 (growth 1.0 percent, inflation near 3.75 percent, Bank Rate about 4.25 percent, the energy cap up about 16 percent in January) is the weak year. Channel to EPS: about 3.1p per point of UK practice volume.
  • Wage legislation reaches the staff ratio each April; the 2027 step is 3.9 percent to £13.21 (Rathbones, Morgan Stanley), worth about £3m, against an unmitigated cost of about £3.7m per point of group wage inflation. Channel: about 3.5p per point.
  • The CMA order (22 Sep 2026) reaches medicines (written-prescription disclosure, a £21 cap that is neutral) and elective work (published prices, estimates above £500) and adds administrative cost; experts say more than half of long-term medication sales have already moved online, so the remaining leakage is 3 to 4 percent of in-clinic medicine revenue a year on our assumption (about £1m of gross margin a year), and the elective effect is inside the volume path. Channel: about 1p a year.
  • Australian deal competition and the ACCC reach the acquired contribution through the multiple (each turn above 6x on £50m is about £1.2m of EBITDA and about 0.5p) and the pace (each £10m not deployed is about 0.5p in year one). The Vets Central Phase 2 referral shows that clustered local deals are reviewed; CVS's 1 to 2 percent share limits the risk.
  • Rates reach interest on about £118m of floating debt now and about £240m from February 2028 (about 0.9p and 1.8p per point).
  • Tax policy reaches the R&D credit (about 13p of EPS at risk in total) and the corporation tax rate (about 1.3p per point); the 28 Oct 2026 Budget changed neither.
  • Pet insurance reaches average transaction values and diagnostics (claims inflation about 5 percent in 2026, Jefferies; insurers capping some diagnostics); no quantified channel, inside the price assumption.
  • How clear is the chain compared with peers. Pets at Home gives vet revenue, care-plan members, clinician headcount and quarterly cadence, so its chain is clearer; Medicover gives organic growth by division and a dividend policy; Spire gave explicit EBITDA guidance. CVS gives no profit guidance and the richest cost-ratio disclosure of the group, so it is clearest on costs and least clear on demand.

Implication for predictability. Positive for the capital-driven two-thirds of the EPS bridge, which traces line by line; negative for the organic third, which does not trace at all and for which the company's stated ambition is not a usable input; and a specific negative for the R&D credit, which is unguided and large. The external transmission is clear enough that the Consumer can state, for each macro or regulatory development, which line it reaches and by roughly how much, which is more than can be said for most UK mid-caps.

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

Judgement. The main sentiment risks are read-across from the UK's other listed vet operator, the reception of the chief executive appointment, and a market regime change that rotates money out of defensives. None is likely to be extreme for a FTSE 250 stock with a full free float and about $5m to $6m traded daily, but each can move the shares 5 to 10 percent in a day without any change at CVS.

Each risk.

  • UK policy and media: a spring fiscal event with broad tax rises; media coverage of vet prices that transparency will sustain.
  • Activist dynamics: Converium may trade options or go short; an activist exit could weigh on the shares.
  • Sector flows: take-private bids for Spire and others lift the cohort; a broken deal or a French budget shock can hit sector funds.

C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

F2424th of 30

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement. There is no upgrade record. The company sets near-term numbers close to consensus and meets them, usually after the consensus has been cut; it reaffirms medium-term targets it is missing rather than raising or formally lowering them; and it lets ambitions slip quietly (the doubling target moved a year). This is the "honest tracker" the research describes, not a sandbagger and not an upgrader. The departing chief executive will not change that; the successor may reset rather than raise.

Reasoning and evidence. FY2024 EBITDA £127.3m against £130.1m consensus (a 2 percent miss, cyber); FY2025 £134.6m against £131.0m (a 2.7 percent beat, after Deutsche Bank cut its FY2025 EPS 6 percent and with the R&D credit up £2.3m); H1 FY2026 £67.7m against £69.6m (a 2.7 percent miss); FY2026 £141.5m against £141.6m to £141.7m (in line, after a 12 percent cut to FY2026 EPS by Deutsche Bank in October 2025).

C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

F3425th of 30

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement. Over one year: met, after cuts. Over three years: one miss, one beat after cuts, one in line after cuts, and the organic target missed every year. Over five years: strong delivery in the pandemic years (FY2021 to FY2023), then three years of downgrades. In the comparable environment, a squeezed UK consumer with a labour-cost step (FY2025), CVS missed badly on organic growth (LFL 0.2 percent) but protected the margin, partly with a rising R&D credit. The lesson for FY2027: profit estimates set near consensus are usually met; organic growth assumptions above about 3 percent are usually missed.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • One year (FY2026): revenue £712.8m against £714.2m (-0.2 percent); EBITDA £141.5m against £141.6m to £141.7m; EPS 85.6p (85.8p brokers' basis) after the FY2026 consensus fell from about 101p to 85.8p over its forecast life (FactSet range); H1 FY2026 EBITDA missed by 2.7 percent.
  • Three years: FY2024 EBITDA -2.2 percent (cyber), FY2025 +2.7 percent, FY2026 in line; LFL 2.9, 0.2, 2.1 percent against 4 to 8 percent; adjusted EPS from 95.46p to 85.6p.
  • Five years: revenue growth 19.2 percent (FY2021), 8.6 percent (FY2022), 6.3 percent (FY2023); LFL about 7 to 10 percent; margins expanded.
P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

D5017th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. The forecasts should carry high weight for FY2027, medium weight for FY2028 and low weight for FY2029 to FY2031, and they should lean differently by line item.

FY2027 (high weight, about 70 percent of the forecast EPS growth already in hand).

  • The buyback is nearly complete, the UK deal and four Australian deals are done or signed, and the rollover of FY2026 deals is arithmetic.
  • Lean slightly down on EBITDA:
  • the R&D credit has no repeat release in full;
  • H1 FY2027 laps 2.7 percent LFL;
  • the company missed H1 FY2026 EBITDA by 2.7 percent and FY2024 by 2.2 percent.
  • Lean down on organic revenue: organic assumptions above about 3 percent have been missed for three years.
  • Neutral on EPS: the share count offsets the rest.
  • Our 93.3p is already 1.8 percent below consensus. We would not lean further.

FY2028 (medium weight).

  • Lean down on UK volume and margin, because the record of organic delivery is poor.
  • Lean up on acquired revenue against consensus, because the record of capital execution is good.

FY2029 to FY2031 (low weight).

  • The shape is more reliable than the levels: acquisition-led growth of 6 to 7 percent, a margin around 20 percent and EPS growth around 10 percent.
  • Discount our EPS levels by about 5 percent for execution risk: a reset by the new chief executive, rising Australian multiples, and policy risk on the R&D credit.

Trust the company on: capital actions, near-term profit set close to consensus, and cost control.

Distrust it on: medium-term organic targets (4 to 8 percent LFL), the margin range (19 to 23 percent), any ambition with a date (doubling EBITDA by FY2028), and any margin held up by the R&D credit.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The profit record, EBITDA against consensus at the time.
  • FY2024: −2.2 percent (the cyber attack).
  • FY2025: +2.7 percent, after Deutsche Bank had cut EPS by 6 percent, and with the R&D credit up £2.3m. The credit made the beat.
  • H1 FY2026: −2.7 percent.
  • FY2026: in line, after a 12 percent cut to FY2026 EPS by Deutsche Bank in October 2025.
  • Profit lands within about ±3 percent of consensus once consensus has been cut. In the one year it beat, the R&D credit supplied most of the beat. That tool now works in reverse (P-08), so the FY2027 risk is a small miss, not a beat.
  • Consensus over forecast lives. FY2026 EPS went from 101p to 85.8p (−15 percent). FY2027 went from 107p to 95p (−11 percent). The last six months were flat to −1 percent, and estimates are now realistic, not inflated.
  • The organic record. LFL was 2.9, 0.2 and 2.1 percent against a 4 to 8 percent range. Management's informal tone (for example "high confidence" in a return to 4 to 8 percent) has been a poor predictor of organic delivery and a fair one of near-term profit.
  • The capital record. Every stated capital action in the last 18 months was executed: the Crematoria sale at about 10x, two buybacks, the refinancing, the move to the Main Market, Australian deals ahead of business case, the UK restart and CMA compliance ahead of deadline. This supports full weight on the acquired-revenue and share-count inputs.
  • The medium-term ambitions record. The ambition to double EBITDA moved quietly from FY2027 to FY2028 and is now out of reach. Our FY2028 EBITDA is £160m against about £190m needed on FactSet's operating FY2022 base. The 19 to 23 percent margin has been delivered only at its bottom, and only with the R&D credit.
  • The change of chief executive adds a specific downward lean in H2 FY2027 to FY2028: a reset of targets and a kitchen-sink of exceptional costs. The kitchen-sink would hit reported earnings, not adjusted earnings. It is a risk to targets and narrative more than to consensus EBITDA.
  • How we have already leaned.
  • Our UK price and volume path is below the company's tone.
  • The R&D credit is cut by £3.2m.
  • Productivity is halved against FY2026.
  • Australian multiples rise.
  • The net result: FY2027 to FY2028 EPS is 1.8 percent below consensus, and FY2029 is 0.8 percent above consensus only because we model acquisitions that consensus omits.

Forecasts and upgrades

7

Forecasts with good growth rates and higher growth/margins than past

B7518th of 30

Memo topic 6, Forecast financials: the Rater's score

7a

Growth rate

4 answers
C-4952Judgement54P-0856P-1055
C-49What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?D4418th of 30full answer under 4c Financial profile
Group judgement

Financial forecasts

D4820th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The forecasts tell one consistent story. CVS's earnings per share will compound at about 9 to 11 percent a year over the next four years, against three years of decline. The causes are capital allocation and the absence of repeated shocks, not a better operating business.

Where the growth comes from. About 70 percent of EBITDA growth comes from acquisitions, mainly in Australia at 6x to 7.5x and 25 percent margins. A falling share count and costs below EBITDA that grow more slowly than EBITDA add 2 to 4 points of EPS growth a year on top.

What does not change. The UK practices, three-quarters of profit, remain a price-led business:

  • price of about 3.5 to 4 percent offsets wage inflation and no more;
  • volume keeps falling until the pandemic cohort ages into higher need around FY2029;
  • the margin excluding the R&D credit is flat at about 18 percent;
  • operating leverage stays dormant until volume turns.

The near-term weak point. The one new finding that matters most for the next 18 months is that the FY2026 R&D credit contained about £6m of non-recurring release. FY2027 EBITDA is therefore more likely to land at the bottom of management's comfort range (£148.8m) than at consensus. The share count offsets most of that at EPS, so FY2027 EPS of about 93p is 1 to 2 percent below consensus. FY2028 is similar.

How much weight to give the numbers. Give FY2027 high weight, FY2028 medium weight, and treat FY2029 to FY2031 as a shape rather than a level. The record says capital execution is reliable and organic delivery is not. The forecasts have been built so that the reliable part does the work.

What this means for the Consumer.

  • Predictability is good for EPS and free cash flow over 18 months: much of the growth is mechanical and already transacted.
  • Predictability is poor for the headline operating metrics the market reads first (LFL and margin). These will look flat to soft through the next three prints.
  • The four drivers that explain the cone are UK volume, the price-wage spread, the R&D credit and acquisition pace and price. Each can be monitored half-yearly.
  • Downside levels: about 82p for FY2027 EPS and about 81p for FY2028.
  • Upside levels: about 104p for FY2027 EPS and about 121p for FY2028.
P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

B+665th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. From FY2027 the costs below EBITDA and the share count turn from a drag into a support for adjusted EPS. This reverses FY2020 to FY2026.

  • In FY2020 to FY2026, interest more than doubled (£7m to £17m), depreciation grew with leases and capital spending, and the share count drifted up (70.6m to 71.7m). Adjusted EPS fell from 95.5p (FY2023) to 85.6p (FY2026) even as EBITDA rose about 17 percent.
  • From here, the sum of D&A and net interest grows about 5.5 percent a year, from £56.6m to about £74m by FY2031, more slowly than EBITDA at about 7 percent. Adjusted profit before tax therefore rises from 60 percent to 63 percent of EBITDA.
  • The adjusted tax rate drifts up about 0.2 points a year with the Australian mix.
  • The share count falls 1 to 2 percent a year: −5 percent on average in FY2027, then renewed buybacks at modest scale.
  • The result: adjusted EPS grows about 2 to 4 points a year faster than EBITDA. FY2027 is +9.0 percent on EBITDA +5.2 percent. FY2028 to FY2031 is +9 to +11 percent a year on EBITDA +7.3 to +7.6 percent.

Net income and free cash flow tell a lower, lagging story that the Consumer must carry alongside:

  • Reported EPS rises from 24.4p to about 42p in FY2027 and about 82p by FY2031 (derived). The roll-up keeps generating amortisation of acquired intangibles (£25m to £31m), business combination costs (£14.5m to £15m a year, including about £10.5m of employment-linked contingent consideration) and exceptional costs (£4m in FY2027 and £2m to £3m after). The reported tax rate is 33 to 36 percent.
  • Free cash flow on our basis goes from about £29m (FY2026) to £60m (FY2027), then grows about 9 to 10 percent a year to £86m. About half of the FY2027 jump is non-recurring: working capital swings from a £12.8m outflow to a £2m inflow, exceptional costs fall £6.6m and capital spending falls £6m.

Two items below EBITDA could move EPS by more than 5 percent and are not in the base:

  • an HMRC disallowance of R&D claims (up to £38.3m of cumulative claims are open);
  • a large reset by the new chief executive.

Reasoning and evidence, line by line.

  • Depreciation and amortisation (adjusted, excluding acquired intangibles).
  • Derived history: about £38.7m (FY2025), £41.0m (FY2026).
  • Model: £42.8m, £45.3m, £47.9m, £50.5m, £53.2m, which is 5.65 percent of revenue falling to 5.45 percent.
  • The drivers are acquired right-of-use assets and fixed assets, with capital spending flat at £30m to £34m.
  • Consensus carries £41.1m in FY2027 and FY2028 and £22.7m in FY2029, a thin-coverage artefact from one broker. The consensus EBIT median is not consistent with its EBITDA and D&A medians. Read the consensus gap on profit before tax instead: £59.9m of costs below EBITDA against our £60.9m in FY2027.
  • Net financial items (adjusted). £15.6m, then £18.1m, £19.5m, £19.7m, £20.2m and £20.4m (P-02).
  • We exclude the unwinding of discount on deferred consideration. FactSet's reported net interest of £16.8m against the adjusted £15.6m suggests about £1.2m.
  • The interest sensitivity doubles after February 2028.
  • Tax.
  • The adjusted rate implied by 85.6p is about 28.5 percent; the company states 27.4 percent (FY2025: 26.9 percent). Read the company's figure as on a different basis, perhaps before diluted shares or minorities.
  • We use 28.5 percent in FY2027, rising 0.2 points a year to 29.3 percent, because Australia's share of profit (taxed at 30 percent against the UK's 25 percent) rises from about 13 percent (FY2026, derived) to about 17 percent (FY2027) and 26 percent (FY2031) of EBITDA before central costs.
  • Each 1 point of tax is about 1.3p of EPS.
  • The reported rate (44.4 percent in FY2026) stays high because amortisation and contingent consideration are not deductible.
  • One-offs and items outside adjusted earnings (our estimates).
  • Exceptional costs: £4m in FY2027 (the tail of CMA compliance, the change of chief executive, controls work under Provision 29), then £3m and £2m.
  • Business combination costs: about £14.5m to £15m a year while £50m a year is deployed.
  • Amortisation of acquired intangibles: about £25m rising to £31m.
  • The reset risk is a £5m to £15m kitchen-sink in FY2028 (reported only).
  • The R&D risk. A full loss of the credit would cost about 14 percent of FY2027 EPS, or about 13p. Separately, a retrospective HMRC disallowance would be a cash and reported item of up to the open £38.3m. We carry neither.
  • Minorities and associates. Minority equity is £5.5m and there are no associates. This is immaterial.
  • Buybacks and share issuance.
  • FY2027: −£38.3m completes the £50m programme, and shares reach about 66.3m.
  • FY2028 to FY2031: −£10m, −£20m, −£25m, −£25m. This is a capacity-based, probability-weighted assumption. A new chief executive with leverage falling to about 1.7x is more likely than not to renew a programme, but at a size the £50m-a-year acquisition commitment allows.
  • Share issuance for incentive schemes is about 0.1m to 0.2m a year.
  • FY2026's £31.7m was the first buyback in five years; FY2022 to FY2025 saw small net issuance (£0.1m to £2.3m a year).
  • Dividends. 9.0p in FY2026, then 9.5p, 10.0p, 10.5p, 11.0p and 11.5p. That is a payout of about 10 percent and £6.6m to £7.3m a year, which does not constrain anything.
  • Working capital.
  • It was 2.7 percent of revenue at June 2026 against 1.3 percent at June 2025. The FY2026 outflow came from the timing of R&D credit receipts and new buying terms.
  • We assume a partial reversal to 2.2 percent at June 2027 and flat thereafter.
  • It swings by about £40m between half-years (8.9 percent at December), so interim free cash flow will always look weak.
  • Deferred and contingent consideration. About £8m to £9m of cash a year. It sits in our acquisitions line, not in free cash flow.
  • Versus the last six years (derived).
  • From FY2020 to FY2026, net interest rose from £8.6m to £16.8m on FactSet's basis.
  • The cost of the 2024 cyber attack, the payroll-tax step and a rising share count turned 21 percent revenue growth (FY2023 to FY2026) into −10 percent EPS.
  • None of the three repeats in the base.
P-10

Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

D4821st of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Our base case is a disciplined roll-up compounding on a stalled organic core.

  • UK practices: price about 4.0 percent falling to 3.1 percent, volume −2.5 percent improving to flat by FY2030, and LFL 1.4 percent rising to 3.1 percent. The margin excluding the R&D credit is flat at about 18 percent.
  • Group LFL: about 2.0 to 2.3 percent in FY2027 (H1 about 1.5 percent, H2 about 2.6 percent), rising to about 3 percent by FY2030.
  • Australia: revenue goes from £79m to about £107m in FY2027 and about £214m by FY2031, at about 25 percent margins, on about £33m a year of initial consideration at multiples rising from 6.2x to 7.5x.
  • UK acquisitions: £12m to £15m a year at about 8x.
  • The laboratories: revenue up 6 to 7 percent at incremental margins of about 45 percent.
  • Animed: margin recovering to 4.5 percent.
  • The R&D credit: £12.5m a year, flat.
  • Central costs: up 5 to 6 percent a year.

These give:

  • revenue of £757m, £810m, £863m, £919m and £976m;
  • EBITDA of £148.8m, £160.1m, £171.9m, £184.8m and £198.3m (margin 19.7 to 20.3 percent);
  • adjusted EPS of 93.3p, 102.1p, 112.9p, 125.6p and 139.8p;
  • free cash flow on our basis of £60m to £86m;
  • leverage peaking at 1.85x and falling to 1.45x.

The next 18 months are the least favourable part of the path.

  • H1 FY2027: revenue about +5.7 percent; margin 19.1 percent against 19.0 percent; EBITDA about +6 percent.
  • H2 FY2027: revenue about +6.7 percent; margin 20.2 percent against 20.7 percent, because the R&D release normalises; EBITDA about +4 percent.
  • H1 FY2028: revenue about +7.1 percent; margin about 19.3 percent.

The market will therefore see LFL of about 1.5 to 2.5 percent and flat-to-lower margins in the November, January and March prints. The arithmetic of acquisitions and the share count shows up in full-year EPS rather than in the headline operating metrics.

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

F5728th of 30

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. There is plausible scope to lift the adjusted EBITDA margin by about 1 point to about 21 percent by FY2029 (consensus 20.2 percent) and the EPS growth rate by 2 to 3 points a year, from one big area and several small ones, all congruent with the strategy. The big area is Australian mix; the small ones are laboratories, Animed's recovery, Healthy Pet Club Advanced and UK acquisitions. Peer precedent (IVC Evidensia at about 21 percent; CVS's own 21.2 percent operating EBITDA margin in FY2023) says 21 percent is achievable; the joint-venture margins at Pets at Home are a different structure and not a precedent.

Reasoning and evidence (derived where marked).

  • Australia: from 11 percent to perhaps 16 to 18 percent of revenue by FY2029 at 25 percent-plus margins adds about 0.4 to 0.6 points of group margin (derived).
  • Laboratories: fixed-cost leverage and internal volume; 0.1 to 0.2 points.
  • Animed: from break-even in H1 FY2026 towards 4 to 5 percent EBITDA; about 0.1 point.
  • Volume: Healthy Pet Club Advanced and the ageing cohort turn volume flat-to-positive by FY2028 to FY2029, which with operating leverage is the largest swing.
  • Offsets: wage floors, transparency, and the R&D credit.
C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

F4224th of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. The organic engine is running well below the company's own normal rate (2.1 percent LFL against a 4 to 8 percent target and a pre-pandemic 5 to 6 percent average), but it did not decelerate over the twelve months in underlying terms: the year-on-year LFL rose from 0.2 to 2.1 percent, and the apparent second-half slowdown is mostly a comparative and weather effect. The input force itself is mixed: price is accelerating, volume is still falling but more slowly, membership is falling, and the acquisition engine is accelerating. Reported revenue growth (5.9 percent) is a point below the five-year rate (6.9 percent) because acquired growth was lower than in FY2024.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Organic: FY2025 0.2 percent, FY2026 2.1 percent; two-year stacks about 1.6 percent (H1 FY2026) and about 3.0 percent (H2 FY2026, derived).
  • Below normal: the company's pre-pandemic five-year LFL average was 5 to 6 percent; FY2023 was 7.3 percent.
  • Inputs: members 519,000 to 508,000; acquisitions £30.9m to £45.8m; Australian sites 52 (late 2025) to 61.
  • The false-friend risk: acquired revenue (£32.8m in FY2025; Australia +£27m in FY2026) masks a UK practice engine growing about 1 percent; the risks report ranks this its second-place threat.

Judgement. Consensus is credible for FY2027 and broadly balanced for FY2028 to FY2029. It assumes revenue growth slightly below history (less acquired growth), a flat margin, and EPS growth well above the last three years because of the buyback and acquisitions rather than any operating step-change. P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

D5020th of 30

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. The net impact is a business whose reported growth looks better than in FY2023 to FY2026 while its organic core looks the same.

Revenue. Growth steps up from 5.4 and 5.9 percent (FY2025 and FY2026) to 6.2 to 7.0 percent a year.

  • Acquisitions supply about 3.5 to 4.5 points: Australia about 3.5 points and the UK about 1 point from FY2028.
  • Organic growth supplies about 2.0 to 3.0 points.
  • FY2020 to FY2023 was the reverse: about 7 to 10 percent organic and little acquired.

EBITDA margin. It dips to 19.7 percent in FY2027 as the R&D credit normalises, then rises 0.1 to 0.2 points a year to 20.3 percent by FY2031.

  • Excluding the R&D credit, the margin turns from three years of erosion (18.4 to 17.65 percent) to a rise of about 0.25 points a year (18.0 percent in FY2027 to 19.0 percent in FY2031).
  • All of that rise is mix (Australia, the laboratories, Animed). The UK practice margin excluding the credit stays at about 18.1 to 18.3 percent.

EBITDA. Growth is about 5 percent in FY2027 and about 7.5 percent a year after that, against about 6 and 5 percent in FY2025 and FY2026.

Adjusted EPS. Growth is about 9 percent in FY2027 and 9 to 11 percent a year to FY2031, against −3.6 percent a year from FY2023 to FY2026. EPS rises from 85.6p to 93.3p, 102.1p, 112.9p, 125.6p and 139.8p.

Free cash flow on our basis. About £29m in FY2026, then £60m in FY2027, rising to £86m by FY2031.

Net bank debt. It plateaus at about £240m to £255m, and leverage falls from a 1.85x peak to 1.45x.

Reported EPS rises to about 42p (FY2027) and about 82p (FY2031), but it stays about 40 to 55 percent below adjusted EPS throughout.

How the difference from the past is built. Five things explain it:

  1. the payroll-tax step, the cyber attack and the CMA costs do not repeat;
  2. the share count falls instead of rising;
  3. acquisitions run at 6x to 7.5x in Australia rather than about 10x in the UK;
  4. the R&D credit stops rising and steps down, the one item that turns from tailwind to headwind;
  5. the UK organic core is unchanged: price matches wages and volume is negative until about FY2029.

How concentrated the case is. Four drivers explain about 85 percent of the difference between the forecasts and the last four years:

  • the pace and price of Australian acquisitions;
  • the share count;
  • the UK spread between price and wages net of volume;
  • the R&D credit.

The skew. Mildly negative in the next 6 to 9 months (H1 FY2027 and the R&D credit). Mildly positive over 18 months to four years, because consensus omits unannounced acquisitions and buyback renewal.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The EBITDA bridge, FY2026 to FY2027 (model), +£7.3m in total.
  • Australia +£6.9m: rollover of FY2026 deals about +£3.3m, new deals about +£3.1m, organic about +£0.5m.
  • UK practices organic, excluding the credit, +£1.4m.
  • UK deal +£1.3m.
  • The laboratories +£1.1m and Animed +£0.5m.
  • Central costs −£0.6m.
  • R&D credit −£3.2m.
  • The EBITDA bridge, FY2026 to FY2031 (model), +£56.8m in total.
  • Australia +£34.0m.
  • UK deals +£9.4m.
  • UK organic +£12.0m.
  • The laboratories +£5.5m and Animed +£1.5m.
  • Central costs −£2.6m.
  • R&D credit −£3.2m.
  • The EPS bridge, FY2027 against consensus (95.0p).

  • Higher D&A plus interest (£60.9m against £59.9m): −1.1p.

Forecasts and upgrades

8

Probably upgrades this financial year to

D3618th of 30

Memo topic 7, Scope for upgrades over 6 to 12 months: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

D3619th of 30

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Our assessment is already most of the way into guidance, estimates and the narrative, and the part that is not yet in them is unfavourable first and favourable later. Guidance is consensus, and consensus already assumes the modest organic growth we assume; our FY2027 is 1 to 2 percent below both because of a fact in the annual report that no covering house in our files has written about, the £6.6m R&D release, and because the first half laps the hardest comparative. So the next three events (November, January, March) are more likely to produce small cuts and soft optics than upgrades, the breadth of revisions is more likely to tip negative than positive, and the medium-term framework is more likely to be reset than met. The favourable parts of the gap are real but slower: the share count is under-modelled, the out-year consensus omits deals the company will do, buyback renewal is unpriced, the laboratories and take-private options are discussed by one house, and the sell-side's ratings (73 percent Buy, targets 22 percent above the price) already lean toward a re-rating the estimates do not yet support. The configuration is of a stock whose earnings will be delivered roughly as expected, whose near-term prints will be read as disappointing, and whose upgrade cycle, if it comes, starts with the FY2027 results and a new chief executive's plan in the second half of 2027. For the Consumer: there is no cushion of conservatism in guidance or estimates to be unlocked over the next six months; the case for months six to eighteen rests on the H2 FY2027 comparative, the appointment and the out-year arithmetic becoming visible, and the memo should say so plainly rather than present the gap as favourable throughout.

Guidance

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

F3224th of 30

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement. CVS gives no numeric profit guidance; its near-term "guidance" is a statement of comfort with consensus (FY2027 adjusted EBITDA £149.0m to £151.7m, EPS about 94.9p) and its medium-term guidance is a framework (4 to 8 percent LFL, 19 to 23 percent margin, cash conversion above 70 percent, leverage below 2.0x) plus an ambition to double adjusted EBITDA by FY2028. Over the next 18 months the near-term statement is most likely to be met and almost certain not to be raised: we put the probability that FY2027 lands inside or within 2 percent of the comfort range at about 60 percent, that it is trimmed to the bottom of or slightly below the range at some point before September 2027 at about 30 percent, and that it is raised at about 10 percent. The eight events in the window break into three kinds. The three before April 2027 (the four-month update in late November, the half-year update in January, the interims on 4 Mar 2027) are the most likely to produce "in line" wording with soft optics (group LFL about 1.5 percent, a flat H1 margin), and they carry the small risk of a "slightly below" if the four months to October show practice LFL under 1 percent. The two in mid-2027 (the July trading update and the September results) are where the number lands; our base is £148.8m, so "met" means at the bottom. The three after that (November 2027, January 2028, March 2028) will be set by a new chief executive and read against FY2028 consensus of £158m, which we think is attainable, with the April 2027 wage step and the swap expiry (February 2028) as the known headwinds. For the medium term, the probability that the framework is reset by the new chief executive within twelve months of appointment is about 60 percent for the LFL range (to something like 3 to 5 percent or to different measures), about 80 percent that the FY2028 doubling ambition is dropped or replaced, and about 30 percent that the margin range is narrowed to its bottom half; the probability that any medium-term target is raised is below 10 percent. A reset would nominally be "guidance reduced" but would mostly affect the narrative rather than consensus, which already assumes 2.5 to 3 percent organic growth, and if paired with a cost programme or a buyback policy it would probably be received well. The drivers of each outcome are listed below; the single most important is the R&D credit recognised in H1 FY2027, which decides whether the March interims show a margin that holds or slips.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • What the company has said, and its record of saying it. At the FY2026 results (24 Sep 2026): trading in line, positive early LFL growth, comfort with the consensus range, framework reaffirmed, doubling ambition carried to FY2028. The record: FY2024 EBITDA −2.2 percent against consensus (cyber); FY2025 +2.7 percent after a 6 percent cut to Deutsche Bank's EPS and with the credit up £2.3m; H1 FY2026 −2.7 percent; FY2026 in line after a 12 percent cut to FY2026 EPS in October 2025. The November 2025 update (sales +5.7 percent, EBITDA +6.2 percent) and the January 2026 update (revenue +5.8 percent, LFL 2.7 percent, EBITDA £67.7m) both said "in line" and the year landed in line. The pattern is an honest tracker: numbers set at consensus and met once consensus has been cut, misses concentrated in first halves, no upgrades, and targets that slip quietly rather than being withdrawn.
  • The event calendar and our probabilities (near-term statement: raised / met / reduced).
  • Late November 2026, four-month update: 10 / 75 / 15. Driver of "met": group LFL 1.5 to 2.5 percent, revenue about +5.5 to +6 percent, "in line". Driver of "reduced": practice LFL under 1 percent after the summer price rises and published prices, or weather and consumer comment that foreshadows a soft H1. Driver of "raised": LFL above 3 percent (unlikely against 2.7 percent) or a large acquisition.
  • January 2027, half-year update: 10 / 70 / 20. Driver of "reduced": an H1 EBITDA indication below about £70m, which happens if the H1 R&D credit falls to the in-year run-rate (about £5.7m) and practice LFL is about 1 percent; the market would read it as a margin miss even though it is a credit effect.
  • 4 Mar 2027, interims: 10 / 65 / 25. The H1 margin (our 19.1 percent against 19.0 percent) and the H1 credit are the swing; also the first Advanced tier numbers and the clinical staff ratio. This is also the likeliest window for the appointment, which could change the tone without changing the number.
  • July 2027, full-year trading update, and September 2027 results: 15 / 60 / 25. H2 LFL against an easy comparative (about 1.5 percent and a heat-hit fourth quarter) is the best chance of a 3 percent-plus print, which supports "met" at the bottom of the range and a confident tone into FY2028; the H2 margin will be down against a comparative that held most of the release, which the company will have to explain. A repeat of a provision release (the remaining provision is £2.9m) is the main route to "raised".
  • November 2027 to March 2028: set by the new chief executive against FY2028 consensus (£158m); the April 2027 wage step (3.9 percent), the swap expiry and any reset decide the tone. Probability of "met" about 55 percent, "reduced" about 30 percent (a kitchen-sink or a reset presented alongside), "raised" about 15 percent (a renewed buyback or faster deals).
  • Why "raised" is so unlikely near term. The near-term statement is consensus itself, so raising it means guiding above consensus, which this management has never done; the departing chief executive has no incentive to raise; the credit is a headwind; and the first half is the hard half. Raising would need a repeat release, a 3 percent-plus LFL in the first four months, or a chief executive who chooses to start with a beat.
  • Why "reduced" is more likely than the record suggests. Our base (£148.8m) sits at the bottom of the comfort range for reasons that are facts (the credit's composition, the comparative); the company's own practice is to let consensus drift to where the number will land rather than to cut it in one step, so a "slightly below" or a "towards the lower end" in January or March is the modal form of reduction, not a profit warning. A formal downgrade would need a second force: volume falling faster than 3 percent, clinician departures, or an HMRC challenge.
  • The medium-term framework: what a new chief executive will probably do, with the precedents. The OWN-4 report's nine transitions at six peers show target resets within the first year at Vimian (new financial targets within ten weeks, with a rights issue), Terveystalo (a reorganisation and a €29m write-down alongside a profit programme), Restore (£5.9m of restructuring charges in the first half-year and a new 20 percent margin target), Pets at Home (a £20m cost programme, a re-based dividend and a £50m buyback) and Spire (a transformation programme and a suspended dividend), against continuity at Mears and Medicover. Boards in healthcare and consumer services hire externally more often than other sectors; CVS's board has said it wants "requisite operational skills" and is recruiting a Chief Client Officer in parallel. Applied to CVS: the 4 to 8 percent LFL range (missed three years running) is the most likely casualty, because no new chief executive wants to inherit a target already missed; the doubling ambition is already dead in practice; the margin range survives because 19.7 to 20.3 percent sits inside it. A reset would be announced at a strategy update or capital markets event in 2027, probably with the FY2027 results or within six months of arrival.
  • Capital-allocation guidance. About £50m a year of acquisitions and capital expenditure of about £30m are the one piece of guidance we expect to be met without qualification, because they are within the company's control and record. Buyback guidance after the £50m completes is absent; we put the probability of a renewed programme of £20m to £30m within twelve months of the appointment at about 55 percent (the activist, the share price at about 12x, leverage falling from June 2027, and the Pets at Home and Restore precedents), which would be a form of "guidance raised" on capital returns.
  • How our assessment compares with guidance, line by line. FY2027 EBITDA: company comfortable with £149.0m to £151.7m; ours £148.8m (at or just below the bottom). FY2027 EPS: company about 94.9p; ours 93.3p (1.7 percent below). Medium-term LFL: 4 to 8 percent; ours 2.0 to 3.1 percent group in every year. Margin: 19 to 23 percent; ours 19.7 to 20.3 percent. Capital expenditure, acquisitions, cash conversion, leverage: in line. Doubling by FY2028: ours £160m, not met. The gap is therefore slightly unfavourable near term (we are below the number the company has endorsed), neutral on the capital framework, and unfavourable on the medium-term operating targets, which we expect to be cut rather than met.

What this means for the Consumer. Do not expect guidance to be the source of upgrades in the next twelve months; expect it to be met in a way that looks soft until September 2027, and expect the medium-term framework to be replaced. The favourable scenario is not a raise but a credible reset: a new chief executive who sets 3 to 5 percent LFL, states a buyback policy and quantifies a cost programme would lower the nominal targets and raise the probability that they are met, which is what the multiple needs.
