I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

D3517th of 30

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement. Consensus for FY2027 (revenue £751m, EBITDA £150.6m, EPS 95.2p mean and 95.0p median) is more likely to drift down by 1 to 3 percent than to be held, and very unlikely to rise, over the next three events; FY2028 (£787m, £158m, 103p to 104p) is more likely to be held than cut, with the dispersion closing from the top; and the out-years (FY2029 at 112p on six brokers, FY2030 at 110p on two) are more likely to be upgraded than cut as acquisitions get built in and the coverage artefacts are cleaned up. Net breadth of revisions, which has been flat to slightly negative for six months (EPS −1 percent over three months, EBITDA 0, sales +1, target price −1, buy share stable at 73 percent after rising 9 points in six months), will most likely tip negative in the November to March window and turn neutral to positive from the September 2027 results if H2 LFL prints at 3 percent or above and the new chief executive's first plan is credible. Our FY2027 EPS of 93.3p is 1.8 percent below consensus and our FY2028 of 102.1p is 1.8 percent below; for FY2029 we are 0.8 percent above and for FY2030 about 14 percent above the two-broker figure. So the gap between our assessment and consensus is slightly unfavourable for the next twelve months, neutral at eighteen, and favourable beyond, with the caveat that the out-year favourability rests partly on brokers choosing to model unannounced deals, which Jefferies refuses to do and Deutsche Bank does.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Where consensus stands and how it is built (FactSet, 25 Oct 2026; nine houses, seven with FY2027 EPS). FY2027 EPS 0.940 (Barclays) to 0.973 (Investec); EBITDA £150m to £152m; revenue £745m (Jefferies, excluding unannounced deals) to £759m (Davy). FY2028 EPS 0.954 (Shore Capital, flat on FY2027) to 1.05 (Davy); FY2029 0.978 (Shore) to 1.13 (Investec, Barclays). Ratings: seven Buy or Overweight, two Hold (Jefferies 1,400p; Shore Capital 1,250p, initiated September 2026); targets 1,250p to 1,900p (RBC), median 1,600p. Net income over EPS implies about 68.5m shares in FY2027, so at least some brokers have not fully reflected the buyback (voting rights 66.73m in late October).
  • The mechanics of the likely drift down in FY2027. Three forces push EBITDA estimates down by about £1m to £3m between now and March: the R&D credit (brokers who carry £14m or more, as Jefferies' margin arithmetic implies, will adjust once the H1 credit prints at about £6m); the H1 LFL print (group LFL of about 1.5 percent against a 2.7 percent comparative will read as deceleration); and the H1 margin optics. One force pushes EPS up by about 1 to 2 percent: the share count, as brokers true up to 66.3m. The net is EPS of about 92p to 94p by March 2027, which is where we are. The dispersion will narrow because the top end (Investec 0.973) falls more than the bottom.
  • Why FY2028 is more likely held than cut. Consensus FY2028 EBITDA of £158m needs about 3 percent organic growth and a held margin on top of acquisitions; our £160.1m is above it because we model the stated deal pace. Brokers who exclude unannounced deals (Jefferies: revenue £770m, EBITDA £158m) will hold unless UK volume worsens; brokers who include them will add as deals are announced. Shore Capital's flat FY2028 EPS (0.954) is the outlier that would be revised up on any evidence of EPS growth. The April 2027 wage step is known; the swap expiry affects FY2028 by only four months (about £0.8m of interest at current rates). The cut scenario for FY2028 is a target reset accompanied by a kitchen-sink that touches adjusted costs (central cost investment, pay) or a volume step down of more than a point.
  • Why the out-years are more likely upgraded. FY2029 rests on four to six brokers and FY2030 on two; the consensus depreciation line (£41.1m, £41.1m, £22.7m) is a thin-coverage artefact, as is the FY2030 EPS fall to 110p. Our FY2029 EPS (112.9p) is in line with consensus and our FY2030 (125.6p) is 14 percent above it; removing all unannounced deals and buybacks would put FY2029 at about 103p (second set), which means consensus FY2029 already assumes either continued deals or margin expansion without saying so. As the pace of about £50m a year is demonstrated each half, brokers who model it will raise out-year revenue and EBITDA, and FY2030 will be re-based upward as coverage broadens. The risk to this view is a chief executive who slows deals in favour of buybacks or organic investment, in which case EBITDA estimates fall and EPS estimates hold.
  • Net breadth of revisions, event by event (our expectation). November 2026: small negative (one or two houses trim FY2027 EBITDA by about 1 percent; target prices hold); January 2027: negative (H1 EBITDA indication); March 2027: mixed (EBITDA down 1 to 2 percent, EPS flat on the share count, out-years up for houses adding deals; the appointment could lift targets); July to September 2027: neutral to positive if H2 LFL is 3 percent or more and the FY2027 number lands at the bottom of the range; late 2027 to early 2028: dependent on the new chief executive's first plan, with upgrades to FY2029 if a buyback policy and continued deals are confirmed and cuts if targets are reset with reinvestment. Across the 18 months, the probability that FY2027 EPS consensus ends below 94p is about 60 percent; that FY2028 consensus ends within 2 percent of today's 104p is about 55 percent; that FY2029 consensus ends above today's 112p is about 55 percent.
  • The dashboard's reading. Sell-side earnings momentum pinned D (28 Oct 2026), revision-momentum score +5 (was −5 in July), buy share 73 percent (+9 points in six months). The ratings are ahead of the estimates: the sell-side has bought the re-rating case (median target 22 percent above the price) without raising numbers. That configuration usually resolves through the estimates catching up or the targets coming down; we expect targets to hold through March on the appointment and to be tested in September.
  • Read-across from peers' events. Pets at Home's H1 (late November 2026) is the main external revision driver: its vet consumer revenue grew 1.9 percent in Q1 FY2027 and it guides low-single-digit vet growth, so a weak vet print would reinforce cuts to UK volume assumptions across both names; a strong care-plan print would support the Advanced tier story. Terveystalo's October 2026 cut (13 percent to 2027 and 2028 operating profit) and Spire's take-out at about 9x show the two ends of the sector's revision behaviour; CVS is nearer the Spire end on earnings quality and the Terveystalo end on volume sensitivity.
  • How our assessment compares with consensus, line by line (FY2027, FY2028, FY2029). Revenue: +0.8, +2.9, +5.3 percent above (acquisitions). EBITDA: −1.0, +1.4, +3.6 percent. Net income: −3.1, −1.9, +0.3 percent. EPS: −0.9 to −1.8, −0.4 to −1.8, +0.8 to +2.2 percent (the model against the Analyst's cross-check). Free cash flow (our basis £60m) is not comparable with FactSet's £76.6m without adding back lease principal and intangible spend; on a like basis we are close to consensus.

What this means for the Consumer. The sell-side is not a source of near-term upgrades; it is a source of small near-term cuts that we already have in our numbers, followed by out-year upgrades as the acquisition pace is built in. The favourable gap is in FY2029 to FY2030 and in the share count; the unfavourable gap is in the FY2027 EBITDA line and the H1 optics. The breadth of revisions will probably be negative into March 2027 and the stock will have to carry that against a D earnings-momentum pin; the turn, if it comes, is the FY2027 results and the new chief executive's plan.

11d

Current multiple

A903rd of 30Memo topic 11, The fair multiple: the Rater's score1 answer
D-0755
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

D4021st of 30

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The share price is overwhelmingly idiosyncratic and the multiple is company-driven. Over the latest six-month window (6 May to 30 Oct 2026) the equal-weighted basket of the dashboard's eleven priced healthcare-services peers explains 13 percent of CVS's daily price variance (correlation 0.36, beta 0.48); 87 percent is CVS's own movement. Over 24 month-end windows the explained share has never exceeded 22 percent and fell to about zero from October 2025 to March 2026, the period of the CMA provisional decision, the AIM-to-Main-Market move and the first buyback, when CVS traded on its own news. No single peer explains more than 12 percent (Clariane) and the two UK names, Spire (6 percent) and, by omission, Pets at Home (not in the basket), do not drive it. The multiple tells the same story: CVS's trailing P/E of 11.6x sits at the 13th percentile of its own ten-year history and its 24-month-forward P/E of 12.4x at the 42nd percentile of five years, while the peer set is scattered from the 5th percentile (Diagnostyka) to the 94th (Emeis), so there is no sector multiple to be "in line" with. CVS's de-ratings (2018 on a wage spike; 2023 to 2025 on the CMA, the cyber attack and payroll taxes) and its one re-rating (2019 to 2021 on high-single-digit LFL) were all company events. For predictability this is double-edged and on balance positive: the share price will respond to the things our answers can monitor (the prints, the appointment, the buyback) rather than to a sector factor we cannot forecast, but it also means that the sector tailwinds in the case (the private-buyer theme, the defensive bid in a risk-off market, Spire's take-out at about 9x) will not lift CVS by osmosis; they reach it only through a bid or a change in its own numbers.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The co-movement table (computed 30 Oct 2026, daily returns, 126-day windows). Latest window: correlation 0.36, beta 0.48, R-squared 13 percent, idiosyncratic share 87 percent; six-month return +16.5 percent for CVS against +12.8 percent for the basket. Month-end history: R-squared 6 to 10 percent in late 2024 and early 2025; 16 to 22 percent from April to September 2025; 0 to 3 percent from October 2025 to March 2026; 17 to 20 percent from April to September 2026; 13 percent now. Beta has ranged 0.12 to 0.77 and is 0.48 now. Per peer, latest window: Clariane 0.35 (12 percent), Emeis 0.31 (10 percent), Spire 0.24 (6 percent), Attendo 0.23, Ambea 0.21, Diagnostyka 0.16, Humana 0.14, Aevis and Fresenius Medical Care 0.10, Terveystalo −0.04, Ramsay Generale de Sante −0.08.
  • What the pattern says. The windows of higher co-movement (April to September 2025 and April to September 2026) coincide with sector-wide moves in European healthcare services (the 2025 take-private wave, the 2026 defensive rotation) and even then the basket explained only a fifth of CVS's variance. The windows of near-zero co-movement coincide with CVS's own regulatory and listing events. The basket's heterogeneity (Nordic care providers up 47 to 72 percent over twelve months, Terveystalo down 31 percent, Emeis down 24 percent, CVS up 3 percent) means the "sector" is not a single factor at all; it is a set of national stories.
  • The multiple against peers (dashboard, 29 Oct 2026). CVS P/E 11.6x, 13th percentile of its ten-year history, valuation pin A; Medicover 23.8x (25th percentile), Spire 14.4x (38th), Attendo 12.6x (45th), Ambea 13.7x (91st), Humana 10.2x (65th), Terveystalo 8.8x (9th), Fresenius Medical Care 8.5x (8th), Diagnostyka 13.6x (5th), Emeis 28.4x (94th). The peer percentiles span the full range, so CVS's low percentile is a statement about CVS (its own 2019 to 2021 peak near 29x forward sets the range), not about the sector. Pets at Home, the only direct UK vet comparator, trades at about 11.5x calendar 2026 earnings with its vet division valued at about 14x by Deutsche Bank; the two UK names are close, which suggests a UK-vet factor exists, but it is not measured in the dashboard basket.
  • The multiple's own history (FactSet, five years weekly). 24-month-forward P/E range 8.03x to 28.69x, median 13.47x, 25th to 75th percentile 11.26x to 19.23x; today 12.44x at the 42nd percentile of five years and the 74th of two years. The ten-year Selector bands: 10.90x "crash", 15.80x "conservative", 18.60x "prudent". The fall from about 29x to about 8x between 2021 and 2024 and the recovery to about 12x were CVS's own regulatory, cyber, payroll and organic story; the European healthcare-services sector did not de-rate by that amount over the period.
  • What drives the idiosyncratic 87 percent, in order. The two data points a year on LFL and margin; regulatory news (the CMA timeline until March 2026; now the ACCC at the margin); the activist and buyback (May 2026); the listing move and FTSE 250 inclusion (January to March 2026); the chief executive vacancy; and read-across from Pets at Home's quarterly statements. Each is a scheduled or semi-scheduled event, which is why the share's path is lumpy around dates and quiet between them.
  • Implications for the multiple's drivers over 18 months. A company-driven multiple re-rates on company events: a credible appointment (worth one to two turns, first set C-84), a 3 percent-plus LFL print (H2 FY2027 is the best chance), a renewed buyback, or a bid or laboratory sale. It de-rates on a soft print, a disappointing appointment or a target reset without a plan. Sector events (a European healthcare take-private, a rotation into defensives) have historically moved CVS by less than half the peer move (beta about 0.5). For the memo's topic #11 the practical consequence is that the fair multiple should be set from CVS's own phase and range, as the guideline does, and the path from today's 12.4x toward 14.9x should be dated to CVS's own events, not to a sector re-rating.
  • A caution on the measurement. The basket is equal-weighted across eleven European care, hospital and diagnostics names, has no price history for Medicover, and excludes Pets at Home; daily correlations across different market hours understate true co-movement a little. Even allowing for that, an explained share below 25 percent in every window is a robust finding.

Implication for predictability. Positive on balance: the share price will track the company's own prints and decisions, which are the things our forecasts and monitoring plan address, and the multiple's low percentile reflects CVS-specific concerns that CVS-specific events can resolve. The negative is that the case cannot borrow momentum from a strong sector, and the sector's current strength (technical momentum B for most peers against CVS's D) is not a leading indicator for CVS.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the eleven ratings page uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

F4026th of 30

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Veterinary and healthcare-services consolidators have outperformed when three things coincided: organic growth at mid-single digits or better, a wide and visible spread between the multiple paid for acquisitions and the multiple the market pays for the group, and a margin rising on scale. They have underperformed when organic growth stalled while acquired growth and leverage kept rising (the roll-up treadmill), when labour costs jumped, and when regulators intervened. CVS currently fits the underperformance pattern on its organic engine and the outperformance pattern on its acquisition spread and private-market value. Historically the re-rating trigger has been an organic re-acceleration or a bid; with organic recovery to 4 percent-plus not in sight for FY2027, the most likely routes to outperformance in 18 months are a well-received chief executive with LFL of 3 to 4 percent (worth one to two turns of P/E) or corporate action.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • CVS's own history (our general knowledge, to be verified): a margin warning in early 2018 on vet recruitment and locum costs led to a deep de-rating; under the present management from 2019 to 2021 LFL ran at high single digits, margins expanded and the shares re-rated to a peak multiple near 29x 24-month-forward earnings (the top of the five-year range in the financials block); from late 2023 the CMA investigation, the cyber attack and payroll taxes took the multiple to about 8x and produced three- and five-year shareholder returns of -49 percent by May 2026.
  • Bids crystallise private values: Mars bought VCA (2017), TPG took Greencross private (2019), private equity owns IVC Evidensia, Medivet and VetPartners (our general knowledge); in 2026 Spire Healthcare accepted a cash offer at about 9x EBITDA and Clariane sold assets at about 14x. Our Sector Digest: "private buyers pay more than the stock market," a story judged early for CVS.
  • Pets at Home as the domestic precedent: its vet joint ventures re-rated it in 2019 to 2021; retail problems de-rated it in 2024 to 2026; brokers now argue the vet division covers its whole market value.
  • Rules of thumb from our digests: on a wage-heavy cost base, mix moves earnings more than sales; where the patient pays, the operator can price, subject here to transparency; a worry clearing at a low multiple can lift a share price without an earnings upgrade (the CMA clearing did not, which says the market's concern moved to the organic engine and the empty chair).
  • Fit: acquired growth masking organic weakness (the risks report's second-place threat), leverage rising, a labour-cost step: the treadmill pattern. A 6x entry against a 12x P/E and a group at 7x EBITDA against private transactions at 9x to 14x: the spread and private-value pattern.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

A703rd of 30

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. With the regulator settled, the fair trough is about 10.9x 24-month-forward earnings (the ten-year 10th percentile, the dashboard's "crash" band), which gives about 1,150p on consensus earnings (-13 percent) and about 1,070p if earnings come in about 7 percent below consensus (-19 percent). A harsher trough at the five-year 10th percentile (9.55x) on lower earnings gives about 940p (-29 percent); the 8x seen at peak CMA fear would give about 790p to 850p (-35 to -40 percent), which we regard as unlikely now that the regulatory tail is gone. The downside is bounded, and it is cushioned by a private-market value well above the price.

Reasoning and evidence (derived).

  • Base: price 1315p; 24-month-forward consensus EPS 105.2p, so 12.5x.
  • Fair trough: 10.9x on 105.2p = 1,147p (-13 percent); on 98p = 1,068p (-19 percent). The 52-week low of 1,062p corroborates.
  • Stress: 9.55x on 98p = 936p (-29 percent); 8.03x on 98p = 787p (-40 percent).
  • Enterprise-value lens: at 6x FY2027 EBITDA (the price CVS pays for small Australian practices) the equity is worth about 1,100p (-16 percent).
  • Cushion: at 9x FY2027 EBITDA, the multiple of the 2026 Spire offer, the equity is worth about 1,770p (+35 percent); at 10x about 1,990p.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

F4223rd of 30

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative ahead is a slow staircase with one or two possible jumps. The regulatory clearing, the step the market waited three years for, has already happened without a re-rating; what remains to look forward to is a chief executive, a first half that will look soft against its comparative, an easier second half, the first evidence from the new subscription tier, and steady Australian accretion. The well-founded steps are mechanical (buyback, acquisitions, cohort ageing); the novel steps (the appointment, an organic inflection, corporate action) are uncertain in timing and direction. For the investor this means the next 6 months are more likely to test patience than reward it, and the re-rating window, if it comes, is H2 FY2027 into FY2028.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–80

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The two sets are highly congruent on the shape of the case and congruent in direction wherever they differ in number. Both describe the same company: a disciplined acquirer and capital allocator whose UK organic engine grows only through price, whose margin is held flat by mix and procurement rather than lifted by leverage, whose EPS growth is close to arithmetic for FY2027, and whose re-rating depends on two events it does not control (the chief executive appointment and a volume turn). Where the second set departs from the first, it does so with new evidence (the composition of the R&D credit, the segment arithmetic of group LFL, the buyback's progress) and always in the same direction: slightly lower near-term EBITDA, slightly higher out-year revenue, and an unchanged EPS path. Differences that all point one way are a sign of a refined view, not a contradiction. The jarring notes are not between the sets but between the sets and some of the AlphaSense reports, which contain several factual errors we have checked and set aside, and between the earnings leg of the case (predictable) and the valuation leg (two-thirds of the modelled return comes from a multiple that depends on the two uncertain events). The Consumer can rely on the sets as one coherent base; the places where we think differently are small and listed below.

The tensions, and how each resolves.

  • Group LFL for FY2027. The first set said 2.5 to 3.5 percent; the second set said 2.0 to 2.3 percent. The second set is right, because about 1.1 points of FY2026's 2.1 percent came from Animed Direct (+11.1 percent) and the Laboratories (+11.5 percent), which normalise, so group LFL can be flat even as UK practice LFL improves from 1.0 to 1.4 percent. The two views agree on the UK practices; the difference is arithmetic the first set did not do. The implication is that the November and January prints will look softer than the underlying practice trend.
  • The R&D credit. The first set carried about £16m as recurring and a 17 percent EPS loss if it went; the second set carried £12.5m and a 14 percent loss. The OWN-1 report confirms the second set and refines it: the FY2026 credit was £16.9m gross, of which £12.2m was the in-year claim, £4.7m prior-year true-ups and £6.6m the effect of the method change, less £1.2m of fees. The second set is right that the headline margin was held by a rising credit, and right to step it down.
  • The acquired contribution to FY2027 EBITDA growth. The first set said 3 to 4 points; the second set 5.5 points. Both are right on their own definitions: the first set counted only new deals at the stated pace, the second set added the rollover of FY2026 deals (about 2.3 points) that are already owned. Use 5.5 points, and note that it is the least uncertain part of the FY2027 bridge.
  • Australian organic growth. The second set assumed 2.5 percent and called it undisclosed, citing Jefferies. The OWN-2 report records that the company disclosed Australian LFL of +3.0 percent for FY2026 and that Deutsche Bank (23 Jul 2026) described it as outperforming the UK. This is a small correction in the case's favour and strengthens the second set's choice to hold the Australian margin at about 25 percent without claiming operating leverage. Davy's back-calculation of an Australian margin near 30 percent (February 2026) is an upside the sets do not use.
  • "95p achievable with modest upside" (first set) against 93.3p (second set). The second set is better founded, because the R&D release is a fact in the annual report. The first set's guidance scores (C-68 at 48, C-71 "moderately prudent") were set before that fact was known; read them a few points lower.
  • The cone's skew. The t-d2 report calls the 18-month range "wide and negatively skewed" with downside of 10 to 15 percent against consensus; the second set calls it "roughly symmetric" at about ±12 percent. We come down between them (D-04): narrow for a mid-cap, tilted down for the next three prints, symmetric over 18 months.
  • The valuation leg. The first set's trough is 10.9x (about 1,150p) and its private-market cushion 9x EBITDA (about 1,770p); the Financial Analyst's model applies the guideline fair multiple of 14.92x at the 18-month column and gets 1,814p and a total return of +37.8 percent, of which +25.3 points is the multiple and +9.3 points earnings. The dashboard's TSR/Valuation pin is F, because the pin and the re-based return disagree by two grades or more. The sets do not contradict each other here, but the Consumer should present the return as an earnings-led component we can defend (about 9 percent a year) plus a multiple component that rests on the chief executive and a volume turn, neither of which our answers can forecast. The guideline multiple of 14.92x sits above the stock's five-year median of 13.47x and its two-year median of 12.16x; our answers to D-07 and the fourth set say the multiple is company-driven and the near-term prints are unlikely to supply the trigger.
  • Errors in the AlphaSense reports, set aside. The t-d2 report says lease liabilities "surged to £199.6m"; that figure is bank-test net debt, and lease liabilities were £106.2m (OWN-5). The t-d1 report's downside arithmetic of 180 to 220 basis points of practice margin per 1 percent of volume implies a drop-through above 300 percent and is rejected, as the second set did. The t-d2 report's "Pets at Home vet revenue +13 percent" is a 2024 figure; the current run-rate is +1.9 percent (Q1 FY2027). The OWN-1 report's claim that CVS's unsubmitted-claim discount is 7.6 percent against VetPartners' 50 percent is correctly derived from the annual report but applies to one year's claim; it supports the view that CVS's recognition is now at the aggressive end of peer practice, which matters for the HMRC tail (D-04).
  • Data conflicts carried from the first set. Australian FY2026 initial consideration is £43.3m in the cash flow (cash paid in year), £45.4m as aggregate initial consideration in the annual report, and £45.6m in the July update; use £45.4m for deal value and £43.3m for cash.

Where we think differently from the sets.

  • Both sets treat the three prints before April 2027 as LFL events; they are also margin events. H1 FY2026 carried about £7.1m of R&D credit and H2 about £8.6m (derived); FY2027's halves carry about £6.25m each on our base. The H1 FY2027 margin will therefore be flat to slightly down on a reported basis even if the business does what we expect, and the H2 FY2027 margin will be down about half a point against a comparative that held most of the release. Neither set says this plainly; it is the main reason we lean the near-term skew down.
  • The second set's buyback renewal of £10m in FY2028 is conservative relative to the evidence in the OWN-6 report (an activist still on the register, a board that has twice responded within weeks, shares at about 12x forward earnings, leverage falling after June 2027) and the OWN-4 precedents (Pets at Home and Restore both launched buybacks within a year of a new chief executive). We would put the probability of a renewed programme of £20m to £30m within twelve months of the appointment at about 55 percent. That is worth about 1 percent of FY2028 EPS and does not move a rounded input, but the Consumer should treat buyback renewal as a mild upside to the base, not a neutral.
  • The first set's reading of competitors (rational, margin-first) is congruent with the second set's price assumptions, but both sets may under-weight how far the published-price regime shifts the price-volume trade-off at the margin for elective work. We keep the inputs and record it as the main reason the volume path could be a point worse than modelled in FY2027.

What the congruence means for predictability. A coherent base narrows the cone: every line of the model traces to a stated judgement in one of the two sets, the judgements lean the same way, and the places where the sets were corrected by new evidence were corrected before the model was built. The residual incongruence is between the earnings case and the valuation case, which is a question for the memo's topics #10 to #12 rather than a flaw in the forecasts.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

C4810th of 30

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. The narrative knows the shape of the case well and the mechanics badly. The sell-side (nine houses; AlphaSense holds research from two, Deutsche Bank and Jefferies, so the narrative we can see is a 25 percent sample weighted to the two most active) articulates the headline dynamics comprehensively: price-led organic growth with weak footfall, the CMA remedies and online medicine leakage, Australia at about 6x as the growth engine, the buyback and the activist, the chief executive vacancy and the risk of a target reset, the ageing pandemic cohort, and the private-market valuation gap and take-private optionality. Both houses strip the R&D credit out of the margin (Deutsche Bank 17.6 percent, Jefferies 17.8 percent), so the credit's existence is known. What is not articulated by any covering house in our files, and is therefore the least known, is the set of items that decide the next three prints and the FY2028 interest line: that the FY2026 credit contained a £6.6m release from a method change that will not repeat; that CVS's recognition discount is now far below VetPartners' and HMRC windows are open on £38.3m; that H1 FY2027's reported margin will be flat to down for that reason alone; that contingent consideration tied to employment (£10.5m a year) is a recurring cost outside adjusted earnings; that the February 2028 swap expiry doubles the interest sensitivity; and that consensus FY2029 only holds if acquisitions continue. The dynamics in the case's favour that are also under-articulated are the share count (consensus net income still implies about 68.5m shares) and the out-year acquisition arithmetic (Jefferies excludes it and values it separately at about 300p a share; Deutsche Bank includes it). Investor commentary that reaches the public record is thin: Converium's letter (13 May 2026) on undervaluation and a £100m buyback, Kelso's purchase (February 2026) on valuation and FTSE 250 inclusion, and nothing public since from either or from Swedbank Robur; we have no evidence from Twitter or investor letters beyond those. For the next 18 months the balance of what is unknown is unfavourable, because the unknown items are mostly negatives that will surface at the interims; for 18 months to three years it is favourable, because the unknown items are mostly the mechanical positives.

Reasoning and evidence, dynamic by dynamic (who discusses it, since when, with what view; "widely" means both houses and company disclosure, "narrowly" means one house, "not at all" means neither house in our files).

  • UK organic growth is price with falling volume. Widely. Deutsche Bank (24 Sep 2026: H2 LFL about 1.5 percent, footfall weak, FY2027 "building gradually"); Jefferies (24 Sep and 30 Sep 2026: weak consumer confidence and weather hit footfall; routine and preventive visits weaker; reactive demand resilient); Morgan Stanley's October 2026 survey (93 percent of vets cite macro, 76 percent cite clinic prices); Piper Sandler (UK visits −1 to −2 percent in 2026, flat to −1 percent in 2027). View: a consumer and cohort problem that eases over time, not a company problem; Jefferies adds that independents and joint ventures are taking share from fully corporate groups. Well known.
  • The CMA remedies and online prescription leakage. Widely, since 2023; Deutsche Bank and Jefferies both call the final order benign and expect fee rebalancing to offset drug-margin loss; expert calls (IVC Evidensia area director, Harrison Family Vets) say more than half of chronic medicine sales have already gone online and leakage will accelerate. The size of the remaining exposure (medicines 15 to 20 percent of practice revenue; our estimate about £1m of gross margin a year) is discussed only in expert calls, not in broker numbers. Known in direction, not in size.
  • Australia at about 6x as the driver, and whether multiples are rising. Widely. Deutsche Bank (21 Oct 2025, 24 Jul 2026: 6x, 16 percent of EBITDA, "confidence building"); Jefferies (31 Jul 2026: primary expansion strategy, £50m a year at 6x worth about 300p NPV, discipline needed as private equity bids up prices). The rising-multiple evidence (5x to 8x per Vets Central's CFO; 9x to 10x for premium assets per a former director; VetPartners Australia at about 10.7x) sits in expert calls only. Deutsche Bank's 23 Jul 2026 note records Australian organic growth outperforming the UK; the company disclosed +3.0 percent. Known; the risk of multiple creep is narrowly known.
  • The buyback has cut voting shares to 66.73m; a further programme. The reduction is public (total voting rights, 30 Oct 2026) and both houses discuss the buyback; Jefferies (23 Jul 2026) says leverage toward 2.0x makes further large buybacks unlikely and prefers acquisitions; the board says buybacks depend on price. Nobody in our files sizes the probability of renewal. The share count's effect on FY2027 EPS appears to be incompletely reflected in consensus (net income over EPS implies about 68.5m). Known as a fact, under-modelled as a number.
  • The R&D credit, its £6.6m release and the HMRC exposure. The credit's existence and its margin effect are widely known (Deutsche Bank 17.6 percent excluding it; Jefferies 17.8 percent; both since October 2025). The composition (£12.2m in-year, £4.7m prior-year, £6.6m from the method change, £1.2m fees), the non-recurrence of the release, the 7.6 percent discount against VetPartners' 50 percent, the £2.9m remaining provision and the open windows on £38.3m are in the annual report and the auditor's key audit matter and in no broker note in our files; the t-i2 report concludes that the sell-side treats the credit as a core underlying benefit. Not articulated. This is the single most important gap for the next three prints.
  • The adjusted-to-reported gap and recurring contingent consideration. Narrowly at best: Deutsche Bank notes the 44.4 percent reported tax rate and acquisition costs; Jefferies notes cash conversion as evidence of quality; the £14.8m of business combination costs a year and the IAS 19 treatment are in the annual report only. Not articulated as a recurring cost of the model.
  • The chief executive succession and targets. Widely: Jefferies (9 Jul and 27 Sep 2026: investors await visibility; targets may be revised); Deutsche Bank (24 Jul 2026: framework intact, strategy clear). The view is split between "overhang" and "continuity"; nobody sizes a reset. Known.
  • The ageing pandemic cohort. Widely and for two years: management, Jefferies (31 Jul 2026 and 20 Oct 2026 on IDEXX), the Paragon orthopaedics head (a second peak in 2025 to 2027); the counter-view (Dr Cees Bennett: minimal uplift) is also in Jefferies' expert series. Known; the timing is contested and nobody attaches a number for CVS.
  • The private-market valuation gap and Laboratories sale. Deutsche Bank only, since October 2025 (a sale could raise over £100m at 10x to 15x; management says too integrated); Spire's recommended offer at about 9x and Qiagen's at 13x are sector reference points in Deutsche Bank and Rothschild notes; Converium's letter raises the take-private risk. Narrowly known on the laboratories; widely known on the gap.
  • The swap expiry in February 2028. Company disclosure only; neither house provides a post-expiry interest sensitivity in our files. Not articulated.
  • Whether out-year consensus includes unannounced deals. Known to specialists: Jefferies states it excludes them (and its revenue is the lowest in the set); Deutsche Bank models continued expansion; the rest are not visible. The consequence, that FY2029 consensus already assumes deals or margin expansion, is not discussed. Narrowly known.
  • The H1 FY2027 margin optics (credit in the halves, the comparative). Deutsche Bank calculated the H1 FY2026 margin excluding the credit (17.0 percent), so the material exists, but no house in our files has projected the H1 FY2027 reported margin on a like basis. Not articulated.
  • Investor commentary. Converium (13 May 2026): shares at 2017 levels despite doubled revenue and EBITDA; £100m buyback; £45m of acquisitions still affordable; threat to nominate directors; may trade options or short. Kelso (February 2026): 130,000 shares at 1,387p on valuation and index inclusion. Nothing public from either since the buyback, and nothing from Swedbank Robur (3.01 percent); the OWN-6 report found no filings and no reports of an approach. No Twitter or investor-letter evidence in our files. The investor narrative is therefore a capital-returns narrative, not an operating one.
  • The sector narrative. Risk-off, rates-led, defensives favoured, UK mid-caps unrewarded for domestic earnings, European healthcare take-privates (Spire, Qiagen, DCC Healthcare, Animalcare, Dechra), media attention to vet prices sustained by transparency, IVC Evidensia weighing a listing (a pricing benchmark if it happens). Investors in the sector are focused on take-outs and on the Nordic care names' earnings momentum (Ambea, Attendo and Humana up 47 to 72 percent in a year); CVS is not where sector momentum is.

Scoring the gap. For the next 18 months, the unarticulated dynamics are net negative for the stock's near-term reception (the release, the H1 margin, the interest line) and net positive for the number (the share count); the articulated dynamics are already in the price at about 12x. For 18 months to three years, the unarticulated dynamics are net positive (acquisition arithmetic in the out-years, buyback renewal, a possible laboratories sale) and the articulated ones (cohort, Australia) are directional positives without numbers. The gap is favourable only for an investor who can hold through the first three prints.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 30 October 2026, 00:29 to 00:49 UK, Opus, richie login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
CVS Group plc (CVSG-GB): answers to the first set of questions, as at 29 Oct 2026

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reader's key: conventions, the base, and the case in one paragraph

The case in one paragraph (our judgement, to steer the model). CVS Group is a UK and Australian veterinary consolidator whose organic engine has stalled in volume and now grows only through price, while its capital-allocation engine (a £50m buyback, about £50m a year of acquisitions at about 6x EBITDA in Australia and a reopened UK pipeline, leverage raised to 1.63x) is producing near-mechanical earnings-per-share growth of about 8 to 10 percent a year. The regulatory tail (the CMA investigation) has gone, labour availability has turned from a constraint to a modest support, and the shares sit on about 12.5x 24-month-forward earnings and about 7x EBITDA, well below private-market prices for the same assets. What the case lacks is a volume recovery and a chief executive. For the 18-month model: treat UK practice volume as falling 1 to 2 percent in FY2027 and price as rising 4 to 5 percent, group like-for-like (LFL) growth of 2.5 to 3.5 percent with the first half the weakest, a flat-to-slightly-rising EBITDA margin around 20 percent, acquired EBITDA of £5m to £8m a year, a share count falling to about 66m, and consensus FY2027 adjusted EPS of 95p as achievable with modest upside from the share count and modest downside from depreciation, interest and the tough first-half comparative.

Conventions.

  • Fiscal year ends 30 June. FY2026 is the year to June 2026, reported 24 Sep 2026. Next: AGM with a four-month trading update expected in late November 2026 (AGM notice posted 23 Oct 2026; last year's update was 18 Nov 2025); a half-year trading update in January; H1 FY2027 results on 4 Mar 2027.
  • Price 1315p on 29 Oct 2026. The last memo (25 Sep, re-rated 1 Oct 2026) priced the case at 1221p, the results-day close; the shares are up 7.7 percent since.
  • Earnings per share (EPS) basis: adjusted, the brokers' basis. FY2026 adjusted EPS 85.6p (company) and 85.8p (FactSet brokers' basis); reported basic EPS 24.4p. Never compute growth across bases.
  • "LFL" means like-for-like sales growth: practices owned for the whole of both periods, constant currency, working-day adjusted.
  • "(derived)" marks our arithmetic from disclosed figures.

The FY2026 base.

  • Revenue £712.8m (+5.9 percent). Group LFL 2.1 percent (H1 2.7 percent; H2 about 1.5 percent). Veterinary Practices LFL +1.0 percent.
  • Segments: Veterinary Practices £648.2m, of which Australia £79.1m (+51.8 percent), so UK practices about £569m against about £564m in FY2025, +0.9 percent (derived); Laboratories £35.0m (+11.5 percent; test volumes +4.3 percent to about 442,000; EBITDA +25.6 percent to £11.3m); Animed Direct online retail £51.0m (+11.1 percent; break-even in H1, profitable in H2).
  • Adjusted EBITDA £141.5m, margin 19.9 percent (20.0 percent in FY2025); 17.6 percent excluding R&D tax credits (17.8 percent in FY2025), so the credit was about £16m in FY2026 (derived) after £15.1m in FY2025 and £12.8m in FY2024.
  • Adjusted profit before tax £84.9m; reported £32.0m; exceptional costs £10.6m; reported tax rate 44.4 percent; adjusted tax rate about 29 percent.
  • Adjusted operating cash conversion 70.6 percent (76.9 percent); company free cash flow £69.2m (£72.2m); FactSet free cash flow £36.7m (a different definition).
  • Net bank borrowings £199.6m, 1.63x bank-test EBITDA (1.18x); FactSet all-in including leases 2.16x. Facility £350m to May 2030; £100m swapped at a 3.83 percent fixed rate to February 2028; covenant 3.25x; board guardrail below 2.0x with a stated willingness to exceed it temporarily for acquisitions.
  • Shares: 71.7m at FY2025, 69.2m at FY2026 year-end, 67.8m diluted at 25 Oct 2026; about £21m of the £50m buyback remained in early September 2026 (derived).

Consensus (FactSet median, 25 Oct 2026, brokers' basis). Revenue £751m, £787m, £820m for FY2027 to FY2029 (+5.3, +4.8, +4.1 percent); EBITDA £151m, £158m, £166m (margins 20.1, 20.1, 20.2 percent); EPS 95.0p, 104p, 112p (+10.7, +9.2, +7.8 percent). FY2029 rests on four to six brokers and FY2030 on two; the FY2029 depreciation and FY2030 EPS fall are coverage artefacts.

Valuation. 11.6x current P/E (13th percentile of its ten-year history); 12.4x 24-month-forward P/E (42nd percentile of five years, 74th of two years; five-year median 13.5x); enterprise value £1,051m, about 7.0x FY2027 consensus EBITDA.

Data conflicts the Consumer should know. Australian FY2026 initial consideration £43.3m (results) against £45.6m (July trading update); employee Net Promoter Score +10.0 at December 2025 against +5.8 for FY2026; return on capital employed 16.4 percent (FY2025, foundations report) against 17.3 percent FY2025 and 16.1 percent FY2026 (results presentation); Australian entry multiples about 6x (company, Deutsche Bank) against 5x to 8x and 9x to 10x (two experts); Crematoria proceeds £42.3m or £42.4m.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: CVS Group plc (CVSG-GB), a UK and Australian veterinary services group: first-opinion and referral companion-animal practices in the UK and Australia (the Veterinary Practices segment, about 88 percent of revenue), UK reference laboratories (Laboratories), and the online pharmacy and pet retailer Animed Direct (Online Retail).

Request: T-P1

Peers: Pets at Home Group (PETS-GB; the listed UK vet network run as joint ventures, the closest listed comparator for UK practice economics); IVC Evidensia (private, EQT-backed; the largest UK and European veterinary consolidator); Medivet (private, CVC-backed UK consolidator); VetPartners (private-equity-owned UK consolidator); Linnaeus (Mars-owned UK first-opinion and referral group); Greencross (TPG-owned; a leading Australian veterinary group); VetPartners Australia (EQT-backed Australian consolidator); Vimian Group (VIMIAN-SE; veterinary products and diagnostics, for laboratory cost structure). Inputs: What share of a UK first-opinion veterinary practice's costs is fixed in the short term (salaried vets and nurses, rent, equipment) against variable (drugs, consumables, locums), and how quickly can staff rosters be flexed when visit volumes fall? How did margins at UK vet groups move in the post-pandemic visit decline of 2023 to 2026, in the vet wage-inflation period of 2018 to 2019, and in the volume surge of 2020 to 2021 (dated figures)? CVS reported clinical staff costs falling to 34.3 percent of revenue in the second half of FY2026 from 36.0 percent (FY2026 results, 24 Sep 2026): what drove the fall, and how much further can rostering, lower locum use and nurse substitution go at CVS and at peers? How much did the April 2025 rise in employer National Insurance and the National Living Wage add to UK vet groups' costs, how was it mitigated, and what is known about the April 2027 wage-floor uprating? Is vet salary inflation easing now that vacancy rates are falling (CVS reported a vet vacancy rate of 5.8 percent in FY2026 against 6.5 percent in FY2025)? What fixed-cost structure explains CVS Laboratories turning revenue growth of 11.5 percent into EBITDA growth of 25.6 percent in FY2026 (results, 24 Sep 2026), and how far does that leverage extend? What savings have vet groups actually realised from online booking, AI scribes, cloud practice-management systems such as Provet Cloud, and two-way client messaging? What did support-office cost programmes deliver against promises (for example Pets at Home's £20m overhead saving announced in November 2025)? After acquisitions in Australia, which costs fall (procurement, laboratory and crematoria contracts) and which do not (vet wages, rent), and over what time? What qualifies for the R&D tax credit that CVS includes in adjusted EBITDA (£15.1m in FY2025, per its annual report), how durable is it, and what policy changes could reduce it? Focus: How much of a change in UK practice visit volumes reaches EBITDA in the first year and after staff rosters adjust, and how much further the clinical and support cost ratios can fall without reducing capacity, given wage floors that rise every April.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Pets at Home Group (PETS-GB; joint-venture practices the CMA found priced near independents); IVC Evidensia, Medivet, VetPartners and Linnaeus (private UK corporate groups that set prices centrally); UK independent practices (the price reference after transparency); Greencross and VetPartners Australia (Australian pricing); Molecule Group (UK online veterinary pharmacy, Hyperdrug); Chewy and Mars-owned Banfield and VCA (US precedents for online medicine shift and clinic pricing). Operating conditions: a UK household squeeze with rising living costs (2025 to 2027); the CMA's transparency regime (final decision 24 Mar 2026, binding order 22 Sep 2026: published price lists, ownership branding, written estimates above £500, disclosure of the right to a written prescription, a written-prescription fee cap of £21 including VAT plus £12.50 for further drugs); statutory employer-cost rises (April 2025) and periods of high vet wage inflation (2018 to 2019); the post-pandemic decline in routine visits (2023 to 2026); the demand surge of 2020 to 2022; the 2008 to 2009 recession; the Australian market with lower insurance penetration and rising interest rates in 2026. Inputs: What price increases have UK corporate vet groups put through each year from 2023 to 2026, and how much of their like-for-like growth was price against volume? CVS reported like-for-like growth of 2.1 percent in FY2026 and 1.0 percent in Veterinary Practices (results, 24 Sep 2026), and Deutsche Bank reports larger CVS price increases in summer 2026: what volume response followed? CVS's Healthy Pet Club members fell to 508,000 from 519,000 while subscription revenue rose 3.5 percent to £95.5m (FY2026 results): what is the churn response to subscription price rises at CVS and peers? What is the consultation-fee gap between corporate groups and independents, what did the CMA's final report find on corporate prices against independents and Pets at Home joint ventures, and has the gap moved since prices went online? What share of clinic revenue and gross profit comes from medicines, how much medicine volume moved to online pharmacies in the first year after mandatory prescription disclosure, and what do US precedents show? How do insurance penetration (about 50 percent in the UK, about 15 percent in Australia) and insurers' coverage limits affect owners' acceptance of price rises? How is CVS's Healthy Pet Club Advanced (unlimited consultations, launched 1 Jul 2026, about 14,500 clients in three months per the company) priced, and what do comparable unlimited-consultation plans show about upgrade, cannibalisation and visit frequency? How did veterinary pricing behave in the 2008 to 2009 recession and the 2022 to 2023 inflation spike? How do acquired Australian practices' prices change after acquisition? Focus: Whether CVS can keep raising UK practice prices at recent rates without losing more visits and members now that prices are published and owners are squeezed, and how much of its medicine margin is at risk from mandated prescription disclosure.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: CVS's earnings path turns on several interacting dynamics (price against visits and membership, staff availability against capacity and locum cost, acquisitions against purchasing scale and laboratory volume, transparency against the corporate price premium), and whether they reinforce or offset each other decides the UK practice volume trend. Peers: Pets at Home Group (PETS-GB; joint-venture model with lower clinician turnover); IVC Evidensia, Medivet, VetPartners and Linnaeus (UK corporate groups under the same transparency regime); Greencross and VetPartners Australia (Australian consolidation dynamics); Vimian Group (VIMIAN-SE; diagnostics demand). Inputs: A price-and-volume loop: do higher prices lead to deferred routine care and subscription churn, lower visits and then further price rises to cover fixed costs, or do subscription plans lock in visits? A staffing loop: CVS reported a vet vacancy rate of 5.8 percent in FY2026 against 6.5 percent (annual report, September 2026); do lower vacancies cut locum use, improve continuity of care and raise client retention and visits? A scale loop: do acquisitions improve purchasing terms and internal laboratory volume, lifting margins and cash for further acquisitions (for example CVS moving acquired Australian practices to a single wholesaler)? A demographic loop: as the 2020 to 2022 pet cohort reaches higher-need ages, do diagnostics and chronic care rise, and do owners upgrade subscriptions? A transparency loop: do published prices let independents win share on price, narrowing the corporate premium? A talent loop: do acquisitions trigger founder and senior-vet departures that cause client leakage? A valuation loop: does a low share-price multiple bring buybacks, activist pressure and takeover interest (Converium Capital's public letter of 13 May 2026 urging a £100m buyback; CVS's £50m buyback launched 26 May 2026)? Focus: Which of these loops are operating now at CVS and its peers, in which direction and how strongly, and which is likely to dominate UK practice visit volumes over the next two to three years.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: CVS's adjusted earnings sit far above its reported earnings (adjusted profit before tax £84.9m against reported £32.0m in FY2026, per the annual report), and several inputs between EBITDA and adjusted earnings per share (acquired EBITDA, the R&D credit, contingent consideration, depreciation, interest, tax and the share count) are opaque or changing. Peers: Pets at Home Group (PETS-GB; capital returns and adjusted-earnings practice); Vimian Group (VIMIAN-SE; an acquisitive veterinary group with deferred consideration); Medicover (MCOV.B-SE) and Spire Healthcare (SPI-GB) (healthcare-services groups with lease-heavy balance sheets and private-market valuation evidence); IVC Evidensia and Greencross (private acquirers setting Australian and UK practice prices). Inputs: Australian acquisition pace, entry multiples (about 6x EBITDA per the company and Deutsche Bank; 9x to 10x per a former director in October 2025; 5x to 8x per a Vets Central CFO), post-acquisition margins (25 percent-plus per the company) and the share of consideration deferred; UK acquisition multiples after the restart (the first deal, two sites and nine vets for an initial £15m, September 2026) and the UK pipeline; the R&D tax credit inside adjusted EBITDA (£12.8m in FY2024, £15.1m in FY2025) and its sustainability; contingent consideration expensed because it is tied to vendors' continued employment (£6.8m in the first half of FY2026) and acquisition fees; the path of adjusted depreciation, including right-of-use assets, with capital expenditure guided to about £30m a year; interest (facility margin of 1.25 to 2.50 percent over SONIA, £100m swapped until February 2028, the Bank of England rate path); the adjusted tax rate and the effect of a rising Australian share of profit; the share count (2.3m shares bought for about £28.8m under the £50m programme by early September 2026) and the likelihood of a further programme under a new chief executive; working-capital timing, including R&D credit receipts (a £12.8m outflow in FY2026); and the profit trajectories of the Laboratories and Animed Direct segments. Focus: Which inputs between EBITDA and adjusted earnings per share will move most over FY2027 to FY2029, and how far apart management, the brokers and the evidence are on acquired EBITDA, the R&D credit and the share count.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 31 October 2026, 01:51 to 02:11 UK, Opus, richie login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
CVS Group plc (CVSG-GB): answers to the second set of questions (financial forecasts), as at 31 Oct 2026

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reader's key: conventions, the base, our base case in one table, and what changed since the first set

The case for the model in one paragraph (our judgement). Over the next four years CVS becomes more of an acquisition and capital-allocation compounder and no more of an organic grower. Our base case has revenue growth stepping up from 5.4 to 5.9 percent (FY2025 to FY2026) to 6.2 to 7.0 percent a year. About two-thirds of that comes from acquisitions, mainly in Australia. UK practice like-for-like (LFL) growth stays at 1.4 to 3 percent, because price only matches wage inflation and volume keeps falling until about FY2029. The adjusted EBITDA margin dips to 19.7 percent in FY2027, because the FY2026 R&D credit contained a non-recurring catch-up of about £6m that we do not repeat in full. It then rises by only 0.1 to 0.2 points a year, to 20.3 percent by FY2031, carried by the higher-margin mix of Australia and the laboratories rather than by operating leverage in the core. Adjusted EPS grows about 9 percent in FY2027 (to 93.3p, 1.8 percent below the 95.0p consensus) and 9 to 11 percent a year after that. EPS grows faster than EBITDA because the share count falls and the costs below EBITDA grow more slowly than EBITDA. Free cash flow roughly doubles in FY2027 on a working-capital reversal and lower exceptional costs and capital spending. Bank leverage peaks at about 1.85x in June 2027 and then falls, even with £50m a year of acquisitions and modest renewed buybacks. For FY2027 and FY2028 the forecasts are close to consensus, with offsetting errors. From FY2029 onwards they sit above consensus, because consensus does not model the acquisitions the company says it will keep making.

Conventions.

  • The fiscal year ends 30 June; FY2027 is the year to June 2027.
  • Money is in £m. EPS is in pence on the brokers' adjusted basis unless stated (the forecast-inputs form writes per-share figures in pounds).
  • "(derived)" marks our arithmetic from disclosed figures. "(model)" marks the output of our bottom-up segment model, which is the source of every number in forecast-inputs.md.
  • Consensus is the FactSet median at 25 Oct 2026. The price is 1315p (29 Oct 2026).
  • "Our FCF basis" means free cash flow after capital spending (including intangibles), lease principal, net interest, tax, working capital and exceptional cash costs. It is before acquisitions, deferred and contingent consideration, dividends and buybacks. It differs from both the company's figure (£69.2m in FY2026) and FactSet's (£36.7m).

The FY2026 base, restated for the model (derived where marked).

  • Revenue £712.8m:
  • UK practices about £569.1m (derived);
  • Australia £79.1m;
  • Laboratories £35.0m;
  • Animed Direct £51.0m;
  • intercompany eliminations −£21.4m.
  • Adjusted EBITDA £141.5m (19.85 percent):
  • UK practices about £118.7m (20.9 percent; 18.1 percent excluding the R&D credit; derived);
  • Australia about £20m (about 25 percent);
  • Laboratories £11.3m (32 percent);
  • Animed £1.4m (2.7 percent);
  • central costs −£9.9m (+13.8 percent).
  • R&D expenditure credit inside EBITDA: £15.7m. This includes a £6.6m release of provisions on prior-year claims after a change in recognition method (£3.0m in FY2025).
  • Below EBITDA:
  • adjusted depreciation and amortisation about £41.0m (derived as EBITDA less adjusted profit before tax less interest);
  • net interest £15.6m;
  • adjusted profit before tax £84.9m.
  • Tax and EPS:
  • an adjusted tax rate of about 28.5 percent is implied by 85.6p on about 71m diluted average shares (the company states 27.4 percent, so there is a basis gap of about 1 point);
  • adjusted EPS 85.6p (85.8p on the brokers' basis).
  • Balance sheet and shares:
  • net bank debt £199.6m, 1.63x bank-test EBITDA;
  • lease liabilities £106.2m;
  • shares 69.2m at June 2026 and 66.73m voting rights by late October 2026.

New findings since the first set that change the model (each is used below).

  1. The R&D credit is smaller on a recurring basis than the first set assumed. - The FY2026 credit of £15.7m contained a £6.6m release of prior-year provisions after a change in recognition method (AlphaSense reports on the earnings bridge and on operating leverage, citing the 2026 annual report). The FY2025 release was £3.0m. - The recurring in-year credit is therefore about £9m to £12m, not £16m. - We carry £12.5m from FY2027, flat. That is a £3.2m EBITDA headwind in FY2027 (about 3.4p, or 3.5 percent of EPS) that most consensus models do not show separately. - The history is £9.6m (FY2023), £12.8m (FY2024), £15.1m (FY2025) and £15.7m (FY2026). The rising credit is what held the headline margin flat. Excluding it, the margin fell from 18.4 percent (FY2023) to 17.7, 17.8 and 17.65 percent (derived). - HMRC enquiry windows remain open on up to £38.3m of cumulative claims.
  2. The precedent the operating-leverage report leans on is mostly an accounting change. - The jump in the H1 FY2020 EBITDA margin from 14.1 to 18.6 percent on 7.4 percent LFL coincides with CVS adopting IFRS 16 on 1 Jul 2019. That moved roughly 3.5 to 4 points of rent out of EBITDA. - The underlying leverage was about 0.5 to 1 point. It should not be used as evidence that a volume recovery adds 4 points of margin.
  3. The buyback has gone further than the first set recorded. Voting rights were 66.73m by late October 2026 (company, via AlphaSense). The £50m programme will leave about 66.2m to 66.3m shares by late November 2026. FY2027 average diluted shares are about 67.3m, against about 68.5m implied by consensus net income over EPS.
  4. The April 2027 wage floor is now known. The National Living Wage rises 3.9 percent to £13.21 (Rathbones, 29 Oct 2026). Morgan Stanley calls this in line with corporate expectations. The 28 Oct 2026 Budget added no employer-tax step.
  5. The costs of the acquisition model are recurring and quantified. - Business combination costs were £14.8m in FY2026 (£14.9m in FY2025): £10.5m of contingent consideration expensed because it is tied to vendors' continued employment, and £4.3m of fees. - The maximum remaining undiscounted contingent liability is £29.5m. - All of this sits outside adjusted earnings.
  6. Australia's merger regime has teeth. The ACCC sent Vets Central's acquisition of Hills Veterinary Centre to a Phase 2 review (18 Aug 2026). The first set called the regime "non-onerous". Our view is that the regime is light for CVS's small deals, but it can slow a cluster of deals in one town.
  7. The consensus gap below EBITDA is small. The consensus median lines are internally inconsistent: EBITDA of £151m less D&A of £41.1m gives £110m, while the EBIT median is £107m. On the sum that matters, EBITDA less profit before tax, consensus carries £59.9m for FY2027 against our £60.9m. The gap is about 1.1p, not the 2p to 4p the first set estimated from the D&A line alone.
  8. Half of FY2026's group LFL came from the two small segments (derived). - Group LFL of 2.1 percent ≈ practices 1.0 percent × 0.87 weight (0.87 points) + Animed 11.1 percent × 0.07 (about 0.8 points) + Laboratories about 11 percent × 0.035 (about 0.4 points). - With Animed normalising to about 5 percent and the laboratories to about 7 percent, group LFL can be flat at about 2.0 to 2.2 percent in FY2027 even if UK practice LFL improves. This matters for how the market reads the November and January trading updates.

Where we differ from the first set's answers.

  • Group LFL for FY2027: about 2.0 to 2.3 percent, not 2.5 to 3.5 percent (finding 8). The difference is the normalisation of Animed and the laboratories, not a weaker UK practice view. UK practice LFL improves from about 1.0 to about 1.4 percent.
  • The R&D credit: a smaller recurring base (finding 1). A total loss of the credit costs about 14 percent of FY2027 EPS, not 17 percent. The near-term risk is the absence of a repeat release, which is about 3 to 4 percent of EPS.
  • The FY2027 EBITDA composition:
  • acquired growth is larger than the first set's 3 to 4 points: about 5.5 points (Australian rollover plus new deals, plus the UK deal);
  • organic growth is smaller than its 2.5 to 3 points: about 2.2 points (UK practices about 1 point, the laboratories and Animed about 1.1 points, central costs −0.4);
  • the R&D credit is −2.3 points.
  • The "95p achievable with modest upside" call: we place FY2027 at 93.3p. Lower profit before tax costs about 3.4p (2.3p from EBITDA, 1.1p from the costs below it). The lower share count adds back about 1.7p.

Half-years (Part 3), built from the segments:

  • H1 FY2027: revenue about £377m (H1 FY2026: £357.0m); EBITDA about £72.0m (£67.7m).
  • H2 FY2027: revenue about £380m (£355.8m); EBITDA about £76.8m (£73.8m, which included most of the £6.6m R&D release).
  • H1 FY2028: revenue about £404m; EBITDA about £78m.
  • Why H1 grows faster in revenue but H2 carries the higher margin: H1 growth is helped by the rollover of the Australian deals bought in FY2026, nine of fourteen sites by late 2025. H2 margins are seasonally about 1 point higher than H1.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

UK practices (about 80 percent of revenue).

  • Price 4.0, 3.5, 3.3, 3.2 and 3.1 percent.
  • Volume and mix −2.5, −1.5, −0.7, −0.2 and 0.0 percent.
  • LFL therefore 1.4, 2.0, 2.6, 3.0 and 3.1 percent.
  • Cost base (FY2026, derived): staff about £305m, drugs and consumables about £120m, other about £41m. Rent is below EBITDA under IFRS 16.
  • Staff inflation is 3.6, 3.7, 3.5, 3.4 and 3.3 percent. The April 2027 National Living Wage step is +3.9 percent to £13.21, and vet salary inflation is easing as vacancies fall to 5.8 percent.
  • Productivity is 0.6 percent of staff cost in FY2027, 0.5 percent in FY2028 and 0.3 percent thereafter. The rostering gain was largely banked in FY2026, when clinical staff fell to 34.3 percent of revenue.
  • Drug and other cost inflation is 3 percent.
  • Lost volume drops through at about 50 percent once rosters flex.
  • R&D credit £12.5m a year, against £15.7m in FY2026, which included a £6.6m release of prior-year provisions.
  • UK acquisitions: £15m in FY2027 (the two-site, nine-vet practice; completion assumed about mid-H1), then £12m a year, at about 8x EBITDA and 25 percent margins.

Australia.

  • Organic growth 2.5 percent. Undisclosed; Jefferies says it is not clearly stronger than the UK's.
  • Rollover of FY2026 deals about £13m of revenue.
  • New deals of about £33m initial cash in FY2027 (including the £8.9m completed or signed after the year end), then £30m a year.
  • Multiples 6.2x, 6.6x, 7.0x, 7.3x and 7.5x on total consideration, of which 20 percent is deferred. Margins 24 percent on new deals and about 25 percent for the segment.
  • The run-rate of about £100m annualised revenue reported for late 2026 is consistent with this.

Laboratories. Revenue +7 percent in FY2027 and +6 percent a year after (volumes +4 to 5 percent plus price), at an incremental margin of 45 percent.

Animed Direct. Revenue +5 percent a year. Margin 3.5 percent in FY2027, recovering to 4.5 percent by FY2029.

Central costs. +6, +5, +5, +4 and +4 percent: the change of chief executive, the Chief Client Officer and executive pay.

Group LFL is about 2.0 to 2.3 percent in FY2027 (H1 about 1.5 percent, H2 about 2.6 percent) and about 3 percent by FY2030. Animed and the Laboratories contributed about 1.1 points of FY2026's 2.1 percent and will contribute about half that.

Below EBITDA.

  • D&A about 5.65 percent of revenue, falling to 5.45 percent. It grows with acquired right-of-use and fixed assets, against FY2026's derived £41.0m.
  • Net financial items:
  • bank debt at an all-in cost of about 6.0 percent including fees, with £100m swapped at 3.83 percent to February 2028, a SONIA margin of 1.25 to 2.50 percent (1.6 percent average in FY2026), and Bank Rate 4.00 percent from November 2026 and about 4.25 percent by mid-2027;
  • lease interest at about 4.3 percent on about £106m to £121m of lease liabilities;
  • this excludes the unwinding of the discount on deferred consideration, which is outside adjusted earnings.
  • Tax 28.5 percent, rising 0.2 points a year as Australia (taxed at 30 percent) grows from about 13 to 26 percent of EBITDA before central costs.
  • Lease payments in Part 2 are the principal element only, because lease interest is inside net financial items.

Cash and capital.

  • Capital spending is guided at about £30m, rising with the estate.
  • Working capital partly reverses the FY2026 timing outflow of £12.8m on R&D credit receipts and buying terms: from 2.7 percent to 2.2 percent of revenue. It swings by about £40m between half-years.
  • The acquisitions line includes about £8m to £9m a year of deferred and contingent consideration. The maximum remaining undiscounted contingent liability is £29.5m.
  • Buybacks:
  • FY2027: £38.3m completes the £50m programme, taking shares from 69.2m to about 66.3m. Voting rights were 66.73m in late October 2026.
  • FY2028 to FY2031: £10m, £20m, £25m and £25m. This is a probability-weighted renewal sized to keep bank leverage at about 1.6x to 1.75x while about £50m a year goes on acquisitions.
  • Dividend progressive at about 10 percent payout.
  • Bank leverage (pre-IFRS 16 proxy) is 1.85x, 1.74x, 1.66x, 1.57x and 1.44x, against the below-2.0x guardrail and the 3.25x covenant.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

The company gives no numeric EBITDA or EPS guidance. At the FY2026 results (24 Sep 2026) it said it was trading in line, with positive early LFL growth. It expressed comfort with FY2027 consensus EBITDA of £149.0m to £151.7m (average £150.4m) and EPS of about 94.9p (Jefferies' compilation).

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

FY2026, the company's reported figures to adjusted (derived where marked).

  • Reported profit before tax £32.0m, plus:
  • amortisation of acquired intangibles about £23.7m (derived: FactSet total D&A of £64.7m less adjusted D&A of about £41.0m);
  • business combination costs £14.8m (contingent consideration expensed under IAS 19 of £10.5m, plus fees of £4.3m);
  • exceptional costs £10.6m (CMA remedies and rebranding £5.1m, the listing move, investigation costs);
  • finance items excluded from adjusted earnings about £1.2m (FactSet net interest of £16.8m against the adjusted £15.6m);
  • other items not separately identified about £2.6m (derived residual);
  • equals adjusted profit before tax £84.9m.

Tax and EPS.

  • The reported tax rate is 44.4 percent. The adjusted rate is about 28.5 percent as implied; the company states 27.4 percent.
  • Reported basic EPS is 24.4p. The company's adjusted EPS is 85.6p, and FactSet's brokers'-basis actual is 85.8p; the 0.2p difference is a share-count basis difference.
  • Our EPS inputs and cross-check are on the brokers' adjusted basis.

EBITDA.

  • Company adjusted £141.5m, against the brokers'-basis actual of £142m (rounded) and FactSet operating EBITDA of £140m.
  • All three include the R&D credit (£15.7m) and are IFRS 16 (after moving rent below EBITDA).
  • The bank-test EBITDA used for leverage is about £122.5m (pre-IFRS 16; derived from £199.6m at 1.63x).

Reported EPS forecast (our estimate, not entered in the model). About 42p, 50p, 60p, 70p and 82p for FY2027 to FY2031. This assumes acquired amortisation of £25m to £31m, business combination costs of £14.5m to £15m, exceptional costs of £4m, £3m, then £2m, and a reported tax rate of 33 to 36 percent.

Free cash flow definitions.

  • The company's free cash flow was £69.2m in FY2026.
  • FactSet's was £36.7m: operating cash flow of £71.3m less fixed-asset capital spending of £28.5m, before intangible capital spending, lease principal and acquisitions.
  • Our cross-check is after all capital spending (including intangibles), lease principal, net interest, tax, working capital and exceptional cash costs. It is before acquisitions, deferred and contingent consideration, dividends and buybacks. On that basis FY2026 was about £29m (derived; lease principal and cash tax estimated).
  • Adding back lease principal and intangible capital spending, and deducting contingent consideration paid through operating cash flow, brings our FY2027 figure close to consensus FactSet free cash flow of £76.6m.
  • Our figure deducts £2m to £4m a year of exceptional cash that a model building free cash flow from adjusted EBITDA may not.

Consensus basis cautions.

  • The consensus D&A line (£41.1m, £41.1m, then £22.7m for FY2029) is a thin-coverage artefact.
  • The consensus EBIT median (£107m) is not consistent with EBITDA less D&A (£110m).
  • Compare on adjusted profit before tax instead. For FY2027 consensus is £91.1m against our £87.9m. The difference is £2.2m of EBITDA and £1.0m of D&A plus interest.
