Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 1 November 2026, 07:51 to 08:08 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
CVS Group plc (CVSG-GB): answers to the third and fourth sets of questions, as at 1 Nov 2026

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reader's key: conventions, our conclusion in one paragraph, and the Financial Analyst's model

Conventions. As in the earlier sets: the fiscal year ends 30 June; EPS is on the brokers' adjusted basis in pence unless stated; "(derived)" marks our arithmetic; consensus is the FactSet median at 25 Oct 2026 and the revision data are at 29 Oct 2026; the price is 1315p (29 Oct 2026). "Our base case" is the second set's model (FY2027 adjusted EBITDA £148.8m and adjusted EPS 93.3p; FY2028 £160.1m and 102.1p), which the Financial Analyst's model reproduces as £149.1m and 94p, and £160.4m and 103p.

Our conclusion in one paragraph. The financial forecasts for the next 18 months are more predictable than the share price. About 60 percent of FY2027 EPS growth is the share count and most of the rest is acquisitions that are already owned or signed; the organic engine, the one part of the business whose strength is not demonstrated, contributes almost nothing to FY2027 and is not needed to deliver consensus within a few percent. The cone for FY2027 adjusted EPS is roughly 82p to 104p around 93p, narrower than for most UK consumer-facing mid-caps, with a mild downward tilt for the next three prints (November, January, March) because they lap the hardest comparative and because the FY2026 R&D credit contained a release that will not repeat in full. The drivers that separate the forecasts from the past are few and mostly mechanical, which makes the case simple to monitor, but two of them are decisions (acquisition pace and buyback renewal) that will shortly belong to a chief executive who has not been named. Guidance and consensus already reflect most of this: the company points at consensus and consensus assumes only 2.5 to 3 percent organic growth, so the gap between our view and the market's is small and slightly unfavourable for FY2027 (we are 1 to 2 percent below), neutral for FY2028 and favourable from FY2029, where consensus omits the acquisitions the company says it will keep making. The dynamics the market knows least are the ones that bite first (the R&D release, the February 2028 swap expiry) and the ones that help last (acquired growth in the out-years, buyback renewal); the market knows the big picture (price-led growth, Australia at 6x, the activist, the vacancy) well. Net: a predictable earnings path, an unpredictable multiple, and no cushion of conservatism in guidance or estimates to be unlocked over the next six months.

The Financial Analyst's model: the two cross-check gaps, explained.

  1. EPS. The model gives 0.94, 1.03, 1.14, 1.28 and 1.43 against the Analyst's 0.933, 1.021, 1.129, 1.256 and 1.398: gaps of 0.7, 0.9, 1.0, 1.9 and 2.3 percent. Net income is the same in both (the model's £63.1m, £68.1m, £74.1m, £80.9m and £88.3m against the Analyst's EPS times average shares of £62.8m, £68.0m, £74.2m, £80.9m and £88.1m, derived). The gap is therefore entirely the share count the model divides by: about 67.1m, 66.1m, 65.0m, 63.2m and 61.7m (derived from net income over EPS), against the Analyst's average diluted 67.3m, 66.6m, 65.7m, 64.4m and 63.0m. The model's divisor sits close to the Analyst's year-end counts (66.3m, 65.8m, 64.6m, 63.2m, 61.9m) and falls faster than them in the later years, which is what a template does when it retires shares at a fixed price (the £13.17 it holds) rather than at the rising prices the Analyst assumed for the FY2028 to FY2031 programmes (about £14 to £17 a share, derived), or counts them at the year end rather than on average. This is a template convention, not a wrong input: the buyback cash, the net income and the FY2027 count are all right, and lowering the buyback inputs to correct the EPS would mis-state cash and leverage. We change nothing. For the memo: quote FY2027 and FY2028 adjusted EPS as 93p to 94p and 102p to 103p; treat the model's FY2030 and FY2031 EPS as about 1.5 to 2.5 percent generous relative to an average share count; and note that the model's EPS growth is flattered by about half a point in each year with a buyback.
  2. Free cash flow. The gaps are £4.2m, £3.2m, £1.9m, £2.0m and £2.1m, which equal the exceptional cash costs the Analyst deducted (£4m, £3m, £2m, £2m, £2m) and the model omits, because it builds cash from adjusted EBITDA and the input form has no line for exceptional cash. Not an input error. For the memo: the model's equity free cash flow is before exceptional cash; the cash available for deals, dividends and buybacks is £2m to £4m a year lower, which is about 0.03x of leverage and immaterial.
  3. Two reporting-basis artefacts to keep out of the memo. The model's FY2026 EPS of 0.56 is the Viewforth undiluted definition, so its "+68.7 percent" EPS growth for FY2027 is a basis break, not a forecast; like-for-like growth is about +9 percent on the brokers' basis (85.8p to 93.3p). The model's FY2026 EBITA of £75.3m is after amortisation of acquired intangibles and its FY2027 EBITA of £106.3m is before it, so the EBITA line also breaks between history and forecast; and its FY2026 EBITDA of £140.0m is FactSet operating EBITDA against the company's adjusted £141.5m.
  4. The adjusted tax rate. The company states 27.4 percent; the second set used 28.5 percent as implied by EPS. The reconciliation is minority interests of about £0.7m to £0.9m (derived: £84.9m of adjusted profit before tax at 27.4 percent gives £61.6m, against the £60.8m implied by 85.6p on about 71.0m diluted shares), which the model does not carry (Richard, 16 Oct 2026). The 28.5 percent rate, rising 0.2 points a year, is therefore a proxy for tax plus minorities, and it is the right proxy because the Australian deals increasingly include minority partners (65 percent of Animal Medical Centre, June 2026). Keep it.

The forecast inputs: reviewed line by line, none changed. The near-misses are recorded so the Consumer knows which way each input leans. (i) The R&D credit at £12.5m: the 2026 annual report's schedule (the OWN-1 report) shows FY2026 in-year recognition of £12.2m gross (a £13.2m claim less a £1.0m provision), £4.7m of prior-year true-ups, and £1.2m of fees deducted; the recurring net run-rate is therefore about £11m to £11.5m, plus up to £2.9m of remaining provision that may release over several years. £12.5m is at the generous end by about £1m (about 1p of EPS), with small future releases as the offset. (ii) Australian organic growth at 2.5 percent: the company disclosed Australian LFL of +3.0 percent for FY2026 at the results presentation (the OWN-2 report), so 2.5 percent is half a point conservative, worth about £0.1m of EBITDA. (iii) The H1 FY2027 margin at 19.1 percent: H1 FY2026 recognised about £7.1m of R&D credit (Deutsche Bank: 19.0 percent reported, 17.0 percent excluding it, derived), and half of £12.5m is £6.25m, so the credit is a headwind of about £0.9m (0.2 points of margin) in H1; the input needs ex-credit H1 EBITDA growth of about 8.5 percent, which the Australian rollover supplies, but the lean is slightly down (18.9 percent is as likely as 19.3 percent). None of the three moves a rounded input, so inputs-changed.md is not written.

Appendix The share-price co-movement table the Investment Analyst was given

Co-movement of CVSG-GB with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-10-30 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: CVS Group plc (CVSG-GB), a UK and Australian veterinary services group: first-opinion and referral companion-animal practices in the UK and Australia (the Veterinary Practices segment, about 90 percent of revenue), UK reference laboratories (Laboratories), and the online pharmacy and pet retailer Animed Direct (Online Retail); fiscal year ends 30 June.

Request: OWN-1

Question: For CVS Group plc (CVSG-GB), how have the forward price-to-earnings and EV/EBITDA multiples of UK and European listed veterinary and healthcare-services consolidators behaved in the 6 to 24 months after three kinds of event, and what multiple did each trade at while its organic growth ran at low single digits and its earnings growth came mainly from acquisitions and share buybacks: (a) a new chief executive's first strategy update that reset or replaced medium-term targets (for example Vimian's new targets of 8 Mar 2024, Pets at Home's retail turnaround plan of November 2025 and James Bailey's appointment on 30 Mar 2026, Restore's new margin target of November 2023, Terveystalo's profit improvement programme of October 2022, Spire's transformation programme of 2024 to 2025); (b) a re-acceleration of like-for-like or organic growth from below 2 percent to above 3 percent (CVS Group itself in 2019 to 2021, Pets at Home's vet group, Medicover, Terveystalo in 2023); and (c) the closing of a regulatory investigation or overhang (CVS Group after the CMA's final decision of 24 Mar 2026 and binding order of 22 Sep 2026; Spire after the CMA's private healthcare remedies; Pets at Home after the same CMA investigation)? For CVS Group specifically, what 24-month-forward price-to-earnings multiple did it trade at in each fiscal year from FY2015 to FY2026, what was its like-for-like growth and adjusted EPS growth in each of those years, and what did brokers (Deutsche Bank, Jefferies, Shore Capital, Investec, Davy, Barclays, RBC, Peel Hunt) cite as the reason for each re-rating and de-rating? Public reference points: Deutsche Bank (24 Sep 2026) describes CVS as trading at about 7.6x EV/EBITDA against historical UK practice acquisition multiples of about 10x; Spire Healthcare's board recommended a 250p cash offer on 7 Sep 2026 at about 9x pre-IFRS 16 EBITDA; the CMA's final decision imposed transparency remedies and no divestments or service-price caps.

Request: OWN-2

Question: For CVS Group plc (CVSG-GB), what do the published forecasts and notes of each covering broker (Deutsche Bank, Jefferies, Shore Capital, Investec, Davy, Barclays, RBC Capital Markets, Peel Hunt, and any other house with published research) assume for FY2027 and FY2028 on each of the following, and which brokers have updated their models since the FY2026 results of 24 Sep 2026: the R&D expenditure credit recognised inside adjusted EBITDA (the 2026 annual report discloses a net credit of £15.7m in FY2026 including a £6.6m release of prior-year provisions after a change in recognition method, and HMRC enquiry windows open on up to £38.3m of cumulative claims); the average and year-end share count after the £50m buyback launched 26 May 2026 (total voting rights 66,725,634 at 30 Oct 2026); net finance costs, including the treatment of the £100m interest-rate swap fixed at 3.831 percent that expires in February 2028; acquired revenue and EBITDA from announced deals and from unannounced deals at the company's stated pace of about £50m a year; the adjusted tax rate (27.4 percent in FY2026 per the company) and minority interests; the split of revenue, like-for-like growth and EBITDA between H1 and H2; and the UK practice price and volume assumptions behind like-for-like growth? What did each broker say the company would need to report at the four-month trading update expected in late November 2026, the half-year update in January 2027 and the interim results on 4 Mar 2027 to hold, raise or cut its estimates and rating, and what did Shore Capital's initiation (Hold, 1,250p, September 2026) and RBC's 1,900p target rest on?

Request: OWN-3

Question: For CVS Group plc (CVSG-GB), what do the most recent available data show on UK companion-animal veterinary demand and pricing for calendar Q3 and Q4 2026, and how did UK veterinary visit volumes and spending behave relative to real household disposable income in the 2008 to 2012 and 2022 to 2024 squeezes, so as to judge when visit volumes turn after a period of deferred routine care: specifically, UK practice visit volumes and average transaction values (VetCompass, SPVS and Vet Times surveys, practice-management-system data from Provet Cloud, Nordhealth, Covetrus or IDEXX Animana, Vetsource or IDEXX UK practice intelligence); pet insurance claims frequency, claims severity and policy lapse rates from UK insurers (Pets at Home insurance, Admiral, ManyPets, Animal Friends, Petplan, Policy Expert) and from the Association of British Insurers; preventive health plan membership and churn at Pets at Home (Vets for Pets care plans; H1 FY2027 results due late November 2026), IVC Evidensia (Pet Health Club, about 1m members) and Medivet; the share of prescription medicines bought online and Molecule Group's (Hyperdrug, Molecule Veterinary) trading since the CMA's prescription-disclosure rule of 22 Sep 2026; and Office for National Statistics and Bank of England data on UK household spending on pets and veterinary services in 2026? Public reference points: CVS Group reported veterinary practice like-for-like growth of 1.0 percent and Healthy Pet Club membership of 508,000 for FY2026 (24 Sep 2026); Pets at Home reported vet group consumer revenue growth of 1.9 percent for Q1 FY2027 (30 Jul 2026); Morgan Stanley's survey of 28 Oct 2026 reported US clinic visits down 2.2 percent in Q3 2026; the Office for Budget Responsibility (28 Oct 2026) forecasts UK growth of 1.0 percent in 2027.
